UNIT 1 — Trading Terminology
NODE 1
TITLE: Long vs. Short
CELEBRATION: You understand going long vs. short!

INTRO
TITLE: Two Directions to Trade
TEXT: Going <b>long</b> means buying first, hoping to sell later at a higher price. Going <b>short</b> means selling first (borrowing shares), hoping to buy back later at a lower price.
CHART: uptrend

[MC] "Going long" means...
Buying first, hoping to sell later at a higher price.
Selling first, hoping to buy back lower.
Never trading at all.
WHY: Long describes buying first, profiting if price rises.

[TF] "Going short" means selling first and hoping to buy back at a lower price.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Short positions profit if price falls, since you buy back cheaper than you sold.

[FILL] A long position profits if price moves [___].
up
down
sideways only
WHY: A long position was bought first, so it benefits from price rising.

[FILL] A short position profits if price moves [___].
down
up
sideways only
WHY: A short position was sold first, so it benefits from price falling.

[MATCH] Match the position type to its profit direction.
Long => Profits if price rises
Short => Profits if price falls
Position => A general term for either a long or short trade
WHY: Long and short are the two basic directions a trade can take.

[FILL2] Long is [1] first, then sell; short is [2] first, then buy back.
BLANK1: *buy | sell | hold
BLANK2: *sell | buy | hold
WHY: The order of buying and selling is what separates long from short.

[PICK] Which best describes a "short" position?
CONCERN: Understanding this specific term
Selling first, hoping to buy back at a lower price later | detail: Profits from a decline
Buying first, hoping to sell higher later | detail: That's a long position | wrong: That describes going long, not short.
Holding cash with no position at all | detail: Incorrect | wrong: A short is an active position, not being in cash.
WHY: Short describes selling first and profiting from a decline.

[SPOT] Someone says "going short" means buying shares to hold for the long term.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
"Short" describes selling first hoping for a price decline. It has nothing to do with a long-term buy-and-hold.
Short always means holding for years.
Long and short are the same thing.
WHY: "Short" and "long-term" are unrelated ideas that happen to share a similar-sounding word.

NODE 2
TITLE: Position, Fill, and Size
CELEBRATION: You understand position, fill, and size!

INTRO
TITLE: The Vocabulary of an Open Trade
TEXT: A <b>position</b> is an open trade you're currently holding. A <b>fill</b> is the price at which your order actually executed. <b>Size</b> is how much (shares/contracts) you're holding.

[MC] A "position" refers to...
A trade you currently have open.
The exact time of day you traded.
A candle's wick length.
WHY: Position describes an open, active trade.

[TF] A "fill" is the price at which your order actually executed.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Fill specifically refers to the actual executed price, which can differ from the expected one.

[FILL] The amount of shares or contracts you're holding is called your [___].
size
color
timeframe
WHY: "Size" describes how much of a position you're holding.

[FILL] Once you buy or sell, you now have an open [___].
position
gap
spread
WHY: An open trade is referred to as a position.

[MATCH] Match the term to its meaning.
Position => A currently open trade
Fill => The actual executed price
Size => How much you're holding
WHY: These three terms describe the basic mechanics of holding a trade.

[PICK] Which best describes a "fill"?
CONCERN: Understanding this specific term
The actual price your order executed at | detail: Can differ slightly from what was expected
The exact price shown on the chart at all times | detail: Overstates it | wrong: The fill can differ from the displayed chart price.
A synonym for position size | detail: Incorrect | wrong: Fill refers to price, not amount held.
WHY: Fill is specifically the executed price, not the position's size.

[SPOT] Someone assumes their "fill" price will always exactly match the last price shown on the chart.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Fills can differ slightly from the displayed chart price due to spread, slippage, and order type.
Fills always match the chart exactly.
Fills have nothing to do with price at all.
WHY: As covered in prior lessons, several factors can make a fill differ from the chart's displayed price.

NODE 3
TITLE: Unrealized vs. Realized P&L
CELEBRATION: You understand unrealized vs. realized P&L!

INTRO
TITLE: On Paper vs. Locked In
TEXT: <b>Unrealized P&L</b> (profit & loss) is the paper gain/loss on a position still open. <b>Realized P&L</b> is the actual gain/loss once the position is closed.

[MC] "Unrealized P&L" refers to...
The paper gain or loss on a position that's still open.
The gain/loss only after closing a trade.
The company's stated earnings.
WHY: Unrealized P&L reflects an open position's current paper value, not yet locked in.

[TF] Realized P&L only exists after a position has been closed.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Realized P&L is the actual, locked-in result once a trade is closed.

[FILL] Profit or loss on a still-open position is called [___] P&L.
unrealized
realized
guaranteed
WHY: "Unrealized" describes a paper gain/loss that hasn't been locked in yet.

[FILL] Profit or loss once a position is closed is called [___] P&L.
realized
unrealized
random
WHY: "Realized" describes the actual, final result once closed.

[MATCH] Match the P&L term to its meaning.
Unrealized P&L => Paper gain/loss on an open position
Realized P&L => Actual gain/loss after closing
P&L => Shorthand for "profit and loss"
WHY: These terms describe the difference between an open trade's paper value and its final locked-in result.

[FILL2] Unrealized P&L can [1] before a position closes; realized P&L is [2] once locked in.
BLANK1: *change | never change | guarantee
BLANK2: *fixed | changeable | random
WHY: An open position's paper value can move until it's closed and the result becomes fixed.

[PICK] Which best describes unrealized P&L?
CONCERN: Understanding this specific term
A paper gain/loss that can still change while the position stays open | detail: Not yet locked in
A final, unchangeable result | detail: Describes realized P&L instead | wrong: That describes realized P&L, not unrealized.
The company's total revenue | detail: Unrelated | wrong: P&L describes a trade's result, not company revenue.
WHY: Unrealized P&L is a paper value that can still move until the position closes.

[SPOT] Someone treats their unrealized P&L as a final, guaranteed result before closing the position.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Unrealized P&L can still change until the position is actually closed. It isn't locked in yet.
Unrealized and realized P&L are identical.
Once a position is open, its P&L never changes.
WHY: Only realized P&L (after closing) is the final, locked-in result.

NODE 4
TITLE: Market Cap: Large vs. Small
CELEBRATION: You understand market cap!

INTRO
TITLE: The Size of a Company
TEXT: Market capitalization ("market cap") is a company's total share price multiplied by its total shares outstanding, a common way to describe company size.

[MC] Market cap is calculated as...
Share price multiplied by total shares outstanding.
The company's yearly revenue.
The number of employees.
WHY: Market cap specifically multiplies price by total shares outstanding.

[TF] A "large cap" company is generally bigger, by market cap, than a "small cap" company.
ANSWER: True
WHY: "Large cap" and "small cap" are labels describing relative company size by market cap.

[FILL] Market cap stands for market [___].
capitalization
capacity
category
WHY: "Capitalization" reflects the total value of a company's shares.

[FILL] A company with a very large market cap is often called a [___] cap.
large
small
zero
WHY: "Large cap" describes companies with the biggest market capitalizations.

[MATCH] Match the term to its meaning.
Market cap => Share price × total shares outstanding
Large cap => A company with a large market cap
Small cap => A company with a small market cap
WHY: These terms describe company size using the same underlying calculation.

[PICK] Which best describes "market cap"?
CONCERN: Understanding this specific term
A measure of company size based on share price and shares outstanding | detail: A sizing metric
The company's total cash on hand | detail: Incorrect | wrong: Market cap is about share value, not cash reserves.
A guarantee of future stock performance | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Market cap describes size, not future performance.
WHY: Market cap is a size metric calculated from price and shares outstanding.

[SPOT] Someone assumes market cap measures a company's cash reserves.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Market cap is share price times shares outstanding. It's a size metric, not a cash measurement.
Market cap and cash reserves are the same thing.
Market cap has nothing to do with share price.
WHY: Market cap is specifically calculated from share price and share count, not cash on hand.

NODE 5
TITLE: Float
CELEBRATION: You understand float!

INTRO
TITLE: Shares Available to Trade
TEXT: "Float" describes the number of shares actually available for public trading, excluding shares held by insiders or locked up.

[MC] A stock's "float" refers to...
Shares actually available for public trading.
The company's total revenue.
The exact time the market opens.
WHY: Float specifically counts shares available to the public, not the company's revenue or schedule.

[TF] Shares held by company insiders are commonly excluded from the float.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Float commonly excludes insider-held or locked-up shares from the public trading count.

[FILL] A stock with a very small float is often called a "low [___]" stock.
float
volume
market
WHY: "Low float" describes a stock with relatively few shares available for public trading.

[MC] Why might a low-float stock commonly show larger price swings?
Fewer shares available can mean relatively small trades move the price more.
Low float guarantees the price never moves.
Low float has no relationship to price behavior.
WHY: With fewer shares available, the same order size can have a bigger relative impact on price.

[MATCH] Match the float description to its common trait.
High float => Many shares available; often steadier price moves
Low float => Few shares available; often larger price swings
Float => Total shares available for public trading
WHY: Float size is commonly linked to how much a given trade size can move price.

[PICK] Which best describes "float"?
CONCERN: Understanding this specific term
The number of shares actually available for public trading | detail: Excludes insider/locked shares
The company's total shares ever issued, including insider holdings | detail: Overcounts it | wrong: Float specifically excludes insider/locked shares.
A synonym for market cap | detail: Different concept | wrong: Float counts available shares; market cap measures total value.
WHY: Float specifically counts publicly tradeable shares, distinct from total shares issued or market cap.

[SPOT] Someone assumes a low-float stock will always have small, steady price moves.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Low-float stocks are commonly associated with LARGER price swings, not smaller ones, since fewer shares are available.
Low float always means calmer price action.
Float has no relationship to price behavior at all.
WHY: Fewer available shares commonly means relatively bigger price impact per trade, not steadier action.

NODE 6
TITLE: Terminology Review
CELEBRATION: You've completed the Trading Terminology unit!

INTRO
TITLE: Bringing the Vocabulary Together
TEXT: A final review of long/short, position/fill/size, unrealized/realized P&L, market cap, and float.

[TF] Going long profits from a rising price; going short profits from a falling price.
ANSWER: True
WHY: That's the core distinction between the two basic trade directions.

[FILL] Profit or loss on a position that's still open is called [___] P&L.
unrealized
realized
guaranteed
WHY: Reviewing the term from earlier in this unit.

[MC] What do market cap and float have in common?
Both describe aspects of a company's shares (total value vs. shares available to trade).
Both measure the exact same thing.
Neither has any relationship to a company's shares.
WHY: Market cap measures total share value; float measures shares available, related but distinct share-based metrics.

[MATCH] Match each term to its one-line meaning.
Long => Buy first, profit if price rises
Short => Sell first, profit if price falls
Fill => The actual executed price
WHY: A quick recap of the unit's core vocabulary.

[FILL2] A position's [1] P&L can still change; its [2] P&L is locked in after closing.
BLANK1: *unrealized | realized | guaranteed
BLANK2: *realized | unrealized | random
WHY: Unrealized P&L moves until the position closes; realized P&L is final.

[ORDER] A simple way to describe an open long trade using this unit's vocabulary:
Identify it as a long position (bought first)
Note its size (how much is held)
Track its unrealized P&L while it stays open
Note the fill price once it's eventually closed, locking in realized P&L
WHY: This sequence uses the full vocabulary from this unit to describe one trade's lifecycle.

[PICK] Which best summarizes this unit?
CONCERN: Reviewing the big picture
Core trading vocabulary, long/short, position mechanics, P&L, and company size terms | detail: The building blocks for everything else
A set of guaranteed trading rules | detail: Overstates it | wrong: This unit is vocabulary, not rules or guarantees.
Advanced strategy concepts | detail: Too advanced | wrong: This unit covers foundational terms, not advanced strategy.
WHY: This unit builds the foundational vocabulary used throughout the rest of the course.

[SPOT] Someone finishes this unit and still confuses "short" with "long-term holding."
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
"Short" describes selling first to profit from a decline. It's unrelated to how long a trade is held.
Short and long-term are synonyms.
Vocabulary from this unit doesn't matter going forward.
WHY: Precise use of this vocabulary matters, since the rest of the course builds on these exact terms.

UNIT 2 — Market Foundations
NODE 1
TITLE: What Is a Stock?
CELEBRATION: You understand what a share actually is!

INTRO
TITLE: A Piece of the Company
TEXT: A stock is a small slice of ownership in a company. Owning a share makes you a partial owner, not an employee or a lender.

[TF] A share of stock represents partial ownership in a company.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Buying stock literally means buying a small ownership stake.

[FILL] Owning stock means owning a small [___] of a company.
share
loan
coupon
WHY: "Share" describes that fractional ownership stake.

[MC] What does buying one share of a company generally represent?
A small, fractional ownership stake in that company.
A loan you've given the company that must be repaid.
A one-day job working for the company.
WHY: Shareholders own a slice of the company, not a debt owed to them or a job.

[MATCH] Match the term to its meaning.
Stock => A small unit of ownership in a company
Shareholder => Someone who owns at least one share
Company => The business whose ownership is divided into shares
WHY: These three terms describe the basic relationship between a business and its owners.

[FILL2] A stock's [1] reflects what someone is willing to pay right now, not a fixed [2] set by the company.
BLANK1: *price | value | guess
BLANK2: *number | price | law
WHY: Price is a live, constantly negotiated number, not something the company dictates.

[ORDER] A simplified path for how a share ends up in your hands:
A company decides to sell ownership stakes to raise money
Shares are created and offered to investors
Investors buy shares through an exchange
Those shares can later be resold to other investors
WHY: After the initial offering, most shares trade between investors rather than involving the company directly.

[PICK] Which best describes owning a single share of a company?
CONCERN: Understanding basic ownership
A small, fractional ownership stake in that company | detail: You own a tiny piece of the business
A loan you gave the company | detail: Incorrect | wrong: Stock ownership isn't a loan; that's closer to a bond.
A coupon for company products | detail: Incorrect | wrong: Owning stock doesn't entitle you to discounts or products.
WHY: Stock ownership is equity, a piece of the business itself.

[SPOT] Someone believes owning one share means they help run the company's daily operations.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Owning shares generally doesn't give an individual shareholder control over daily operations.
They should buy even more shares to get more control.
They should contact the company to request a job.
WHY: Day-to-day operations are run by management, not by individual shareholders.


[MC] Which of these is an example of equity ownership, like a stock?
A share representing partial ownership in a company.
A savings account deposit.
A gym membership.
WHY: Equity ownership means owning a stake in the business, unlike a deposit or membership.

[TF] All shareholders of a company automatically get an equal say in daily decisions.
ANSWER: False
WHY: Ownership doesn't equal management control; influence often scales with how many shares are owned and formal voting processes.

[FILL] A person or entity that owns at least one share is called a [___].
shareholder
employee
creditor
WHY: "Shareholder" is the standard term for anyone owning stock in a company.

NODE 2
TITLE: What Is a Chart?
CELEBRATION: You can read what a chart actually shows!

INTRO
TITLE: A Picture of Price Over Time
TEXT: A chart is simply a visual plot of price against time. Nothing more mysterious than that.
CHART: uptrend

[TF] A chart is a visual plot of price over time.
ANSWER: True
WHY: That's the entire function of a price chart, at its core.

[FILL] Charts plot price against [___].
time
company size
country
WHY: The two axes of a standard price chart are price and time.

[MC] What does a stock chart primarily show?
Historical price movement over a chosen time period.
A guarantee of the company's future earnings.
The exact identities of everyone currently trading it.
WHY: A chart is a record of what price has already done, not a prediction or a directory of traders.

[MATCH] Match the chart element to what it represents.
X-axis => Time
Y-axis => Price
A single data point => The recorded price at that moment
WHY: These are the basic building blocks of any price chart.

[FILL2] Charts describe what has [1] happened, not what will [2] happen next.
BLANK1: *already | never | randomly
BLANK2: *definitely | possibly | rarely
WHY: A chart is a historical record, not a guarantee about the future.

[ORDER] A simple way to approach an unfamiliar chart for the first time:
Check the price range on the vertical axis
Check the time range on the horizontal axis
Note the overall shape of the price movement
Look for any labeled events or notable points
WHY: Orienting yourself to scale and timeframe first makes the shape of the chart easier to interpret.

[PICK] What is the main purpose of a price chart?
CHART: uptrend
CONCERN: Understanding the tool itself
Visualizing how price has moved over a period of time | detail: A historical picture, not a forecast
Guaranteeing what the future price will be | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Charts show history, not guaranteed futures.
Replacing the need to understand the underlying company | detail: Too narrow | wrong: A chart is one tool among several, not a full substitute.
WHY: A chart's core job is visualization of price history.

[SPOT] Someone assumes a chart alone tells them everything they need to know about a company's health.
CHART: uptrend
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
A chart shows price history, not the full picture of the underlying business.
They should look at an even shorter timeframe instead.
They should ignore the chart entirely from now on.
WHY: Charts are one useful tool, but they don't capture everything about a company.


[MC] If you wanted to see the very first data point on a chart, where would you look?
The leftmost edge of the chart.
The rightmost edge of the chart.
The top of the chart only.
WHY: Charts are typically read left-to-right as time progressing forward.

[TF] A chart can only display one single timeframe and can never be adjusted.
ANSWER: False
WHY: Most charting tools let you switch between many different timeframes for the same asset.

[FILL] The vertical axis of a standard price chart represents [___].
price
volume only
company size
WHY: The Y-axis (vertical) shows price; the X-axis (horizontal) shows time.

NODE 3
TITLE: Why Prices Move
CELEBRATION: You understand the tug-of-war behind every price!

INTRO
TITLE: Buyers vs. Sellers
TEXT: Price moves because of an ongoing back-and-forth between buyers and sellers, not because of a single decision-maker.

[TF] Price moves because of an ongoing negotiation between buyers and sellers.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Every trade is an agreement between someone willing to buy and someone willing to sell.

[FILL] When more people want to buy than sell at a given price, price tends to move [___].
up
down
sideways only
WHY: More buying pressure than selling pressure tends to push price higher.

[MC] What generally happens when sellers outnumber buyers at a price?
Price tends to fall.
Price stays perfectly fixed forever.
Trading stops completely.
WHY: More selling pressure than buying pressure tends to push price lower.

[MATCH] Match the balance of buyers/sellers to the common price effect.
More buyers than sellers => Price tends to rise
More sellers than buyers => Price tends to fall
Roughly balanced => Price tends to stay steadier
WHY: Price is commonly described as a reflection of this shifting balance.

[FILL2] Price is often described as a live [1] between buyers and sellers, not a value set by [2].
BLANK1: *negotiation | guess | coincidence
BLANK2: *decree | agreement | trading
WHY: No single party sets the price; it emerges from many participants trading.

[ORDER] A simplified view of how a new price forms:
Buyers submit the prices they're willing to pay
Sellers submit the prices they're willing to accept
A trade occurs when a buyer and seller agree on a price
That agreed price becomes the new "last traded price"
WHY: Each new trade is the result of buyers and sellers finding common ground.

[PICK] Which best explains a sudden, sharp price move?
CONCERN: Interpreting price moves
A shift in the balance between buyers and sellers | detail: More pressure on one side than the other
The company changing its logo | detail: Unrelated | wrong: Branding changes don't directly move price like this.
The chart being recalibrated | detail: Unrelated | wrong: Charts display real trades, not arbitrary recalibrations.
WHY: Price moves reflect shifts in buying and selling pressure.

[SPOT] Someone believes a single company employee personally sets the stock's price each day.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Price emerges from many buyers and sellers trading, not one person's decision.
They should ask a different employee instead.
They should assume the price is fixed once set.
WHY: No individual sets the price directly. It comes from collective trading activity.


[MC] What would you expect if buying and selling pressure were perfectly balanced?
Price would tend to stay relatively steady.
Price would spike upward immediately.
Trading would stop entirely.
WHY: Balanced pressure between buyers and sellers tends to produce calmer, steadier price behavior.

[TF] A single large seller entering the market can shift the balance toward more selling pressure.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Any participant's buying or selling activity contributes to the overall balance driving price.

[FILL] Price is often described as emerging from the ongoing [___] between buyers and sellers.
negotiation
company decision
government rule
WHY: No single authority sets price. It comes from continuous trading activity.

NODE 4
TITLE: What Is Volume?
CELEBRATION: You understand what volume tells you!

INTRO
TITLE: How Much Traded, Not Where
TEXT: Volume measures how many shares changed hands during a period, a separate idea from price itself.

[TF] Volume measures how many shares changed hands during a period.
ANSWER: True
WHY: That's the definition of volume, a count of shares traded, not a price level.

[FILL] Higher volume generally means more shares were [___].
traded
printed
canceled
WHY: Volume counts the number of shares that actually changed hands.

[MC] What does very low volume commonly suggest about a price move?
Fewer participants were involved in that particular move.
The move is guaranteed to reverse immediately.
The chart's data must be incorrect.
WHY: Low volume simply describes fewer shares trading, not a guaranteed outcome.

[MATCH] Match the volume description to its meaning.
High volume => Many shares traded in that period
Low volume => Few shares traded in that period
Volume spike => A sudden jump in trading activity
WHY: Volume is a separate dimension from price, describing activity level.

[FILL2] Volume describes [1] of trading activity, while price describes [2].
BLANK1: *how much | how fast | how legal
BLANK2: *at what level | how much | how fast
WHY: Volume and price answer two different questions about the same period.

[ORDER] A simple way to read a volume bar under a candle:
Note the height of the volume bar
Compare it to nearby volume bars
Note whether the price candle above it was bullish or bearish
Consider volume and price movement together
WHY: Volume is usually read alongside price action, not in isolation.

[PICK] What does a volume spike alongside a large price move commonly suggest?
CONCERN: Reading volume in context
A larger number of participants were involved in that particular move | detail: More activity accompanied the move
A guarantee that the move will continue forever | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Volume describes activity, not a guaranteed future direction.
A chart data error | detail: Unrelated | wrong: A volume spike is a normal, valid occurrence.
WHY: Volume spikes are commonly read as a sign of increased participation, not a certainty about what happens next.

[SPOT] Someone claims volume numbers are meaningless and should always be ignored.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Volume is commonly used as one added piece of context, not something to dismiss outright.
They should only look at volume and ignore price entirely.
They should stop looking at charts altogether.
WHY: Volume adds useful context; the mistake is dismissing it entirely, not the reverse.


[MC] Which of these would most likely show HIGH volume?
A widely-traded, popular stock during active market hours.
An obscure stock with almost no interested traders.
A market that is currently closed.
WHY: Popular assets during active trading hours commonly see more shares change hands.

[TF] Volume and price are actually the same measurement, just displayed differently.
ANSWER: False
WHY: Volume counts shares traded; price measures the level shares traded at, two separate dimensions.

[FILL] A sudden, unusually large increase in volume is often called a volume [___].
spike
gap
wick
WHY: "Volume spike" is the standard term for a sudden jump in trading activity.

NODE 5
TITLE: What Is Liquidity?
CELEBRATION: You understand how easily a market can be traded!

INTRO
TITLE: How Easily You Can Get In or Out
TEXT: Liquidity describes how easily an asset can be bought or sold without significantly moving its price.

[TF] Liquidity describes how easily an asset can be bought or sold without significantly moving its price.
ANSWER: True
WHY: That's the standard definition of liquidity.

[FILL] A market with many active buyers and sellers is generally described as [___].
liquid
frozen
closed
WHY: "Liquid" describes an asset that's easy to trade without big price impact.

[MC] What is a commonly cited downside of low liquidity?
Larger price swings can result from relatively small trades.
It guarantees higher profits.
It removes the need for a bid and ask price.
WHY: In illiquid markets, even modest trades can move the price more noticeably.

[MATCH] Match the liquidity level to its common description.
High liquidity => Easy to buy or sell without much price impact
Low liquidity => Trades can move price more easily
Liquidity => The general ease of entering or exiting a position
WHY: These describe the same underlying concept from different angles.

[FILL2] [1] liquidity generally means tighter bid/ask spreads, while [2] liquidity often means wider ones.
BLANK1: *Higher | Lower | Zero
BLANK2: *Lower | Higher | Constant
WHY: More active participation tends to narrow the gap between bid and ask.

[ORDER] A simple way to evaluate how liquid an asset is:
Check how many buyers and sellers are currently active
Check the size of the bid/ask spread
Check typical trading volume for that asset
Form a view on how liquid it currently is
WHY: These three signals together give a rough sense of liquidity.

[PICK] Which is generally a sign of low liquidity?
CONCERN: Spotting illiquid markets
A wide gap between the highest bid and lowest ask | detail: Fewer participants narrowing that gap
A very tight bid/ask spread | detail: Opposite sign | wrong: A tight spread is more commonly associated with higher liquidity.
Very high daily trading volume | detail: Opposite sign | wrong: High volume is more commonly associated with higher liquidity.
WHY: Wide spreads are commonly linked to thinner, less liquid markets.

[SPOT] Someone buys a large position in a very illiquid stock, expecting to exit instantly at the same price.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Low liquidity can make it harder to exit a large position without affecting the price.
They should have bought an even larger position.
They should have ignored the bid/ask spread entirely.
WHY: Illiquid markets can make large trades harder to exit cleanly at the expected price.


[MC] Which is a more likely sign of HIGH liquidity?
Many active buyers and sellers with a tight bid/ask spread.
Very few participants and a wide bid/ask spread.
A market that hasn't traded in days.
WHY: Active participation with tight spreads is a hallmark of high liquidity.

[TF] Liquidity has no effect on how much a large trade might move the price.
ANSWER: False
WHY: Lower liquidity generally means large trades can move price more noticeably.

[FILL] An asset that's easy to buy or sell without moving its price much is described as highly [___].
liquid
volatile
closed
WHY: "Liquid" specifically describes ease of trading without big price impact.

NODE 6
TITLE: Bid vs Ask
CELEBRATION: You understand the two-sided price!

INTRO
TITLE: Two Prices, Not One
TEXT: Most markets quote two prices at once: the bid (what buyers offer) and the ask (what sellers want).

[TF] The ask price is what buyers are offering to pay.
ANSWER: False
WHY: The ask is what sellers want to receive; the bid is what buyers are offering.

[FILL] The gap between the highest bid and the lowest ask is called the [___].
spread
volume
gap
WHY: "Spread" is the standard term for that price gap.

[MC] Why does a spread generally exist?
Buyers and sellers don't always fully agree on price.
Trading halts require it by law.
It's a fee charged only on weekends.
WHY: The spread reflects the natural difference between what each side is willing to accept.

[MATCH] Match the term to its meaning.
Bid => The price buyers are currently offering to pay
Ask => The price sellers are currently asking to receive
Spread => The gap between the bid and the ask
WHY: These three terms describe the two-sided nature of most markets.

[FILL2] The [1] is generally lower than the [2] at any given moment.
BLANK1: *bid | ask | spread
BLANK2: *ask | bid | spread
WHY: Buyers typically offer slightly less than sellers ask, creating the spread.

[ORDER] A simple way to read a live quote:
Check the current bid
Check the current ask
Note the size of the gap between them
Understand a market order typically fills near one of these prices
WHY: This explains why an executed price can differ slightly from what you expected.

[PICK] What does a "wide spread" generally suggest?
CONCERN: Reading quotes
A larger-than-usual gap between the bid and ask prices | detail: More distance between what buyers offer and sellers ask
A guarantee of profit on the next trade | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Spread size doesn't guarantee an outcome.
Bid and ask being identical | detail: Contradicts the term | wrong: A wide spread means a bigger gap, not an identical price.
WHY: "Spread" specifically refers to the bid/ask gap.

[SPOT] Someone expects to always buy at the exact last traded price shown on a chart.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Actual fills happen near the current bid or ask, which can differ slightly from the last traded price.
The chart itself must be broken.
They should refresh the page many times instead.
WHY: A small gap between the last trade and the current bid/ask is a normal part of how markets work.


[MC] If the bid is $10.00 and the ask is $10.05, what is the spread?
$0.05
$10.00
$10.05
WHY: The spread is simply the difference between the ask and the bid.

[TF] A market order will typically fill near the current bid or ask, not necessarily the last traded price.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Market orders match against the best currently available bid or ask, which can differ slightly from the last trade.

[FILL] The price sellers are currently willing to accept is called the [___].
ask
bid
spread
WHY: "Ask" is the price sellers want; "bid" is what buyers offer.

NODE 7
TITLE: Market vs Limit Orders (Intro)
CELEBRATION: You understand the two basic order types!

INTRO
TITLE: Speed vs Price Control
TEXT: A market order and a limit order are two basic ways to place a trade, trading off speed against price control.

[TF] A market order lets you name the exact price you want to pay.
ANSWER: False
WHY: A market order executes at the best currently available price, not a chosen price.

[FILL] An order that only executes at a specific price (or better) is called a [___] order.
limit
market
random
WHY: "Limit" refers to the price limit you've set.

[MC] What does a market order prioritize?
Immediate execution, at whatever the current price happens to be.
Getting a specific, guaranteed price.
Waiting indefinitely for a price target.
WHY: A market order trades price certainty for speed of execution.

[MATCH] Match the order type to its trade-off.
Market order => Executes immediately, price isn't guaranteed
Limit order => Executes only at your chosen price or better, but may not fill at all
Order (general) => An instruction to buy or sell under certain conditions
WHY: Each order type balances speed against price control differently.

[FILL2] A market order prioritizes [1], while a limit order prioritizes [2].
BLANK1: *speed | price | neither
BLANK2: *price | speed | neither
WHY: This is the core trade-off between the two order types.

[ORDER] What happens when you place a market order:
You submit the order
It matches against the best currently available price
It executes right away
You receive confirmation of the fill
WHY: Market orders are designed to execute quickly without waiting for a specific price.

[PICK] When might a limit order simply never execute?
CONCERN: Understanding limit order risk
If the market price never reaches your specified level | detail: The order just waits, unfilled
If you set the price too close to the current price | detail: Less accurate | wrong: Being close to the current price makes filling more likely, not less.
Limit orders always execute immediately | detail: Incorrect | wrong: That describes a market order, not a limit order.
WHY: A limit order only fills if the market actually reaches your specified price.

[SPOT] Someone is confused why their limit order never filled, even though they set a specific price.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
If the market price never reached their limit, the order simply remained unfilled. That's expected behavior.
They should have set an even lower limit price regardless of the market.
Limit orders must be broken and shouldn't be used.
WHY: An unfilled limit order usually just means price never reached that level.


[MC] Which order type guarantees a specific price (or better), but not necessarily a fill?
A limit order.
A market order.
Neither order type.
WHY: A limit order only executes at your specified price or better, which means it might not fill at all.

[TF] A market order guarantees the price you'll pay, down to the exact cent.
ANSWER: False
WHY: A market order guarantees speed of execution, not an exact guaranteed price.

[FILL] An order designed to execute immediately at the best available price is called a [___] order.
market
limit
pending
WHY: "Market" orders prioritize speed over price certainty.

NODE 8
TITLE: What Is Volatility?
CELEBRATION: You understand how "choppy" a market can be!

INTRO
TITLE: How Big and Fast the Swings Are
TEXT: Volatility describes how much, and how quickly, price moves, in either direction.
CHART: breakout

[TF] Volatility describes how much and how quickly price moves, regardless of direction.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Volatility is about the size and speed of movement, not which direction it goes.

[FILL] A market moving through large price swings quickly is often described as [___].
volatile
liquid
closed
WHY: "Volatile" describes large, fast price swings.

[MC] What does "low volatility" commonly describe?
Smaller, slower price movements over time.
A guarantee of steady profit.
A market that has stopped trading.
WHY: Low volatility simply means calmer, smaller price movement.

[MATCH] Match the volatility level to its description.
High volatility => Large, fast price swings
Low volatility => Smaller, slower price movement
Volatility (general) => The overall size and speed of price movement
WHY: Volatility is a measurement of movement, not a directional signal.

[FILL2] [1] volatility can mean bigger potential price swings in [2] time.
BLANK1: *Higher | Lower | Zero
BLANK2: *less | more | random
WHY: Higher volatility often means more movement can happen within a shorter window.

[ORDER] A simple way to gauge current volatility on a chart:
Look at the size of recent candles
Compare them to that asset's typical candle size
Note if swings currently look unusually large or small
Form a rough view on current volatility
WHY: Comparing recent movement to what's typical is a simple way to estimate volatility.

[PICK] Which chart is more likely to be described as "volatile"?
CHART: breakout
CONCERN: Comparing two hypothetical charts
One with large, fast price swings | detail: Bigger, quicker movement
One with small, steady, gradual movement | detail: Opposite description | wrong: That would more likely be described as low volatility.
One that hasn't moved at all | detail: Opposite extreme | wrong: No movement is the lowest possible volatility, not high.
WHY: Larger, faster swings are the hallmark of higher volatility.

[SPOT] Someone assumes high volatility guarantees a specific direction for price.
CHART: breakout
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Volatility describes the size and speed of movement, not which direction it goes.
They should assume volatility always means an uptrend.
They should assume volatility always means a downtrend.
WHY: A highly volatile market can move sharply in either direction, volatility alone doesn't determine direction.


[MC] Which market condition would most likely be described as "volatile"?
Large, fast price swings in a short period.
Small, slow, steady movement.
A market that's completely closed.
WHY: Volatility is about the size and speed of price movement, regardless of direction.

[TF] Volatility can be high even if a market is trending strongly upward.
ANSWER: True
WHY: A strong uptrend can still include large, fast swings, volatility measures movement size, not direction.

[FILL] Markets with small, slow price movement are often described as having [___] volatility.
low
high
zero
WHY: "Low volatility" describes calmer, smaller price movement.

NODE 9
TITLE: Market Hours
CELEBRATION: You understand when markets are actually open!

INTRO
TITLE: Not Always Open
TEXT: Most stock markets have set trading hours, with additional (often thinner) trading happening outside them.

[TF] Every stock market is open for trading 24 hours a day, 7 days a week.
ANSWER: False
WHY: Most stock markets have defined standard trading hours, with limited activity outside them.

[FILL] Trading that happens outside standard hours is often called [___] hours trading.
extended
frozen
guaranteed
WHY: "Extended hours" (or after-hours/pre-market) describes trading outside the standard session.

[MC] Why do market hours generally matter?
Liquidity and volatility can differ noticeably outside standard hours.
Prices freeze completely during standard hours.
Order types stop functioning outside standard hours.
WHY: Extended-hours sessions commonly have thinner trading, which can affect price behavior.

[MATCH] Match the trading period to its common characteristic.
Standard hours => Typically the highest liquidity of the day
Extended/after hours => Often lower liquidity than standard hours
Market holiday => Market typically closed for regular trading
WHY: Liquidity commonly varies a lot depending on which period you're trading in.

[FILL2] Trading outside standard hours can mean [1] liquidity and [2] spreads.
BLANK1: *lower | higher | identical
BLANK2: *wider | narrower | identical
WHY: Thinner participation outside standard hours commonly widens the bid/ask spread.

[ORDER] A simple habit around market hours:
Check whether the market is currently open
Note whether it's standard or extended hours
Adjust expectations for liquidity accordingly
Proceed with that context in mind
WHY: Knowing which session you're in helps set realistic expectations about liquidity.

[PICK] What's commonly true about extended-hours trading compared to standard hours?
CONCERN: Comparing sessions
It often has lower liquidity than the standard session | detail: Fewer participants are typically active
It always has higher liquidity than standard hours | detail: Overstates it | wrong: Extended hours are more commonly thinner, not thicker.
Spreads are always identical to standard hours | detail: Overstates it | wrong: Spreads are commonly wider during thinner extended-hours trading.
WHY: Extended-hours sessions are commonly described as having thinner liquidity.

[SPOT] Someone is surprised that a trade placed during after-hours filled at a very different price than expected.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Lower liquidity outside standard hours can lead to bigger differences between expected and actual fill prices.
The trading platform must be broken.
Extended hours should have identical liquidity to standard hours.
WHY: Thinner participation outside standard hours is a commonly cited reason for wider price gaps on fills.


[MC] Which is more likely true of pre-market trading hours?
Typically thinner liquidity than the standard session.
Always identical liquidity to standard hours.
Guaranteed higher volume than standard hours.
WHY: Extended hours commonly see fewer active participants than the standard session.

[TF] Some markets are completely closed on certain holidays.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Most stock exchanges observe scheduled holidays when regular trading doesn't occur.

[FILL] Trading hours before the standard session opens are often called [___]-market hours.
pre
post
mid
WHY: "Pre-market" describes the extended session before the standard open.

NODE 10
TITLE: Timeframes
CELEBRATION: You understand how timeframes change the view!

INTRO
TITLE: Zooming In and Out
TEXT: The same chart can look very different depending on which timeframe you're viewing it on.
CHART: sideways

[TF] A 1-minute chart and a 1-day chart of the same asset will always look identical.
ANSWER: False
WHY: Different timeframes summarize price differently, so the same asset can look quite different across them.

[FILL] Choosing a shorter timeframe generally shows [___] detail over a shorter period.
more
less
zero
WHY: Shorter timeframes zoom in, showing more granular detail over less total time.

[MC] What determines how much time a single candlestick represents?
The selected timeframe (e.g. 1-minute, 5-minute, daily).
The candle's color.
The number of wicks it has.
WHY: Switching the timeframe changes what one candlestick summarizes.

[MATCH] Match the timeframe choice to its trade-off.
Shorter timeframe => More detail, less overall context
Longer timeframe => Less detail, more overall context
Multiple timeframes => A way to combine both views
WHY: Each timeframe offers a different balance of detail versus context.

[FILL2] Zooming to a [1] timeframe shows more detail over a shorter period, while a [2] timeframe shows less detail over a longer period.
BLANK1: *shorter | longer | random
BLANK2: *longer | shorter | random
WHY: Timeframe choice is a trade-off between detail and overall context.

[ORDER] A sensible way to look at a new chart across timeframes:
Check a longer timeframe first for general context
Zoom into a shorter timeframe for more detail
Compare what you see across both timeframes
Form an overall view combining both
WHY: Looking at multiple timeframes is a common way to build a fuller picture.

[PICK] Which best describes why traders often check more than one timeframe?
CHART: sideways
CONCERN: Understanding timeframe use
To combine broader context with more detailed, recent price action | detail: Each timeframe adds something different
Because shorter timeframes are always more accurate | detail: Overstates it | wrong: Shorter timeframes aren't inherently more accurate, just more detailed.
Because longer timeframes replace the need for candles entirely | detail: Incorrect | wrong: Candles still apply on any timeframe.
WHY: Different timeframes are complementary, not competing, sources of context.

[SPOT] Someone judges an asset's entire long-term direction using only a 1-minute chart.
CHART: sideways
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
A very short timeframe mostly shows recent detail, not the bigger long-term picture.
They should have used an even shorter timeframe instead.
They should have ignored the chart entirely.
WHY: Judging the bigger picture usually calls for checking a longer timeframe as well.


[MC] Which timeframe would show the LEAST detail per candle, but the MOST overall historical context?
A long timeframe, like a weekly or monthly chart.
A very short timeframe, like a 1-minute chart.
No timeframe shows any context.
WHY: Longer timeframes compress more history into each candle, showing less granular detail but more overall context.

[TF] Switching timeframes changes how much time a single candlestick represents.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Each timeframe defines what period of time one candle summarizes.

[FILL] A very short timeframe, like 1-minute, shows [___] detail over a shorter period.
more
less
zero
WHY: Shorter timeframes zoom in, offering finer detail over a smaller total window.

NODE 11
TITLE: Candlestick Basics (Intro)
CELEBRATION: You can read a single candlestick!

INTRO
TITLE: Reading a Candle
TEXT: A candlestick is a compact picture of price during a fixed period of time, a preview before Unit 2 goes deeper.
CHART: doji

[TF] A candlestick only shows the price at one single instant in time.
ANSWER: False
WHY: A candlestick summarizes an entire time period, not a single instant.

[FILL] The thick middle part of a candlestick is called the [___].
body
wick
label
WHY: The body spans between the open and close prices for that period.

[MC] What does the color of a candlestick commonly indicate?
Whether the close was higher (bullish) or lower (bearish) than the open.
The exact number of shares traded.
The current bid/ask spread.
WHY: Color is a quick visual summary of direction for that period.

[MATCH] Match the candlestick part to what it shows.
Body => The range between the open and close price
Wick => The high or low reached during that period
Color => Whether that period closed higher or lower than it opened
WHY: Each part of a candle encodes a different piece of information.

[FILL2] A [1] candle typically means the close was higher than the open, while a [2] candle means the close was lower.
BLANK1: *green | red | grey
BLANK2: *red | green | grey
WHY: Color convention (usually green/red) quickly summarizes direction.

[ORDER] A simple way to read a single candle's story:
Identify the open and close (the body)
Identify the high and low reached (the wicks)
Compare the wick length to the body length
Note which direction any long wick points
WHY: Reading a candle means examining all four price points, not just its color.

[PICK] Which term describes a candlestick with a very small body and long wicks on both sides?
CHART: doji
CONCERN: Reading indecision
Doji | detail: Open and close are close together
Hammer | detail: A specific single-wick shape | wrong: A hammer typically has one long wick, not two.
Marubozu | detail: A candle with almost no wicks | wrong: This is the opposite, a marubozu has little to no wick.
WHY: A doji is one of the most commonly referenced single-candle patterns.

[SPOT] Someone says "this one candle proves price will definitely go up next."
CHART: doji
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
No single candlestick can reliably predict future price with certainty.
They should have looked at an even longer wick.
They should have used a different-colored candle.
WHY: Candlesticks describe what already happened. They aren't guarantees about what happens next.


[MC] What does a candlestick's wick represent?
The high and/or low price reached during that period.
The exact trading volume for that period.
The company's name.
WHY: Wicks mark the extreme high and low prices reached, beyond the body.

[TF] Every single candlestick must have at least one visible wick.
ANSWER: False
WHY: A candle can have a tiny or nonexistent wick if price barely moved beyond the open/close range.

[FILL] The colored, thick part of a candlestick between the open and close is called the [___].
body
wick
spread
WHY: "Body" specifically refers to the open-to-close range.

NODE 12
TITLE: Open, High, Low, Close
CELEBRATION: You know your OHLC!

INTRO
TITLE: The Four Numbers Behind Every Candle
TEXT: Every candlestick is built from exactly four numbers: the open, high, low, and close for that period.

[TF] Every candlestick is built from exactly four price values.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Open, high, low, and close are the four numbers that define a candle.

[FILL] The price at the very start of a candle's period is called the [___].
open
close
wick
WHY: "Open" is the first traded price of that period.

[MC] What does "close" refer to on a candlestick?
The final traded price at the end of that period.
The very first traded price of that period.
The highest price reached during the period.
WHY: "Close" specifically refers to the ending price of that timeframe.

[MATCH] Match the OHLC term to its meaning.
Open => First traded price of the period
High => Highest price reached during the period
Low => Lowest price reached during the period
WHY: Together with the close, these four values fully describe a candle.

[FILL2] The [1] and [2] together define the wick's extremes on a candle.
BLANK1: *high | open | close
BLANK2: *low | close | open
WHY: The high and low mark the top and bottom reach of the wick.

[ORDER] How one candle's OHLC values are set, over its time period:
The period begins at the open price
Price moves up and down, setting the high and low
The period ends at the close price
All four values are then locked in for that candle
WHY: OHLC values are only finalized once that period's candle has fully closed.

[PICK] Which best describes the "high" of a candlestick?
CONCERN: Understanding OHLC
The highest price reached at any point during that period | detail: Even if it didn't close there
The price the candle opened at | detail: Different value | wrong: That's the "open," not the "high."
The average of open and close | detail: Different concept | wrong: The high is a single peak value, not an average.
WHY: The high specifically marks the peak price reached, regardless of where it closed.

[SPOT] Someone assumes the "close" of a candle is always the same as its highest price.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
The close and the high are often different values, the close is simply where price ended.
They should assume close always equals the low instead.
They should ignore the close value entirely.
WHY: A candle can close well below its high if price pulled back before the period ended.


[MC] Which value marks the LOWEST price reached during a candle's period?
The low.
The open.
The close.
WHY: "Low" specifically marks the lowest price touched during that period.

[TF] The open and close values are always the same as the high and low values.
ANSWER: False
WHY: Open/close and high/low are often different; a candle's body and wicks can differ in position.

[FILL] The very last traded price within a candle's period is called the [___].
close
open
high
WHY: "Close" refers to the final price of that period.

NODE 13
TITLE: Why Gaps Happen
CELEBRATION: You understand price gaps!

INTRO
TITLE: The Space Between Candles
TEXT: A gap happens when price opens noticeably above or below where it previously closed, often due to news or events between sessions.

[TF] A gap occurs when a candle opens at the exact same price the previous candle closed at.
ANSWER: False
WHY: A gap specifically describes a noticeable difference between the prior close and the new open.

[FILL] A jump between one candle's close and the next candle's open is called a [___].
gap
wick
spread
WHY: "Gap" is the standard term for this kind of jump.

[MC] Why do gaps commonly occur between trading sessions?
News or events can shift buyer/seller sentiment while the market is closed.
The chart software resets randomly overnight.
Volume always drops to zero between sessions.
WHY: News overnight or over a weekend is a common reason expectations shift before the next open.

[MATCH] Match the gap type to its description.
Gap up => Next open is noticeably higher than the prior close
Gap down => Next open is noticeably lower than the prior close
Gap fill => Price later trades back to close that gap
WHY: These are standard descriptive terms for gap behavior.

[FILL2] Gaps often form when the market is [1], and information arrives that shifts sentiment before the [2].
BLANK1: *closed | open | volatile
BLANK2: *next open | prior close | spread
WHY: The information can't be traded on until the market reopens, causing a jump at the open.

[ORDER] A simple way to notice a gap on a chart:
Compare where the prior candle closed
Compare where the next candle opened
Note whether there's a noticeable jump between them
Classify it as a gap up or gap down accordingly
WHY: A gap is identified by comparing two specific price points across the session break.

[PICK] Which best describes a "gap fill"?
CONCERN: Understanding gap terminology
Price later trading back to the level before the gap occurred | detail: Closing the price space that was skipped
A gap that never closes | detail: Opposite idea | wrong: That would simply be described as an unfilled gap.
A candle with no wick | detail: Unrelated | wrong: That's a separate candlestick concept, not about gaps.
WHY: "Gap fill" specifically describes price retracing back through the skipped range.

[SPOT] Someone assumes every gap must always get filled eventually, as a guaranteed rule.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Not every gap gets filled, some do, some don't; there's no guarantee either way.
They should assume no gap is ever filled instead.
They should ignore gaps entirely from now on.
WHY: Treating "gaps always fill" as a certainty overstates what is really just a commonly observed tendency, not a rule.


[MC] Which scenario would most likely cause a stock to gap up at the next open?
Strong positive news released after the market closed.
No news at all overnight.
The market staying open continuously.
WHY: News released while the market is closed is a common cause of a gap at the next open.

[TF] A "gap down" means the next candle opens noticeably higher than the prior close.
ANSWER: False
WHY: A gap down means the next open is noticeably LOWER than the prior close, not higher.

[FILL] Price later trading back through a gap's skipped range is called a gap [___].
fill
spread
wick
WHY: "Gap fill" describes price retracing back through the previously skipped range.

NODE 14
TITLE: What "Trend" Means
CELEBRATION: You understand the basic idea of a trend!

INTRO
TITLE: Which Way Is It Going, Generally?
TEXT: A trend is simply a description of the general direction price has been moving over some period.
CHART: downtrend

[TF] A trend is a description of the general direction price has been moving.
ANSWER: True
WHY: That's the core, descriptive meaning of "trend."

[FILL] A chart generally moving from lower prices to higher prices is described as being in an [___] trend.
uptrend
downtrend
sideways
WHY: "Uptrend" is the standard descriptive term for that general direction.

[MC] What does a "downtrend" commonly describe?
A pattern of price generally moving lower over time.
A single red candle appearing anywhere on the chart.
A guarantee that price will never rise again.
WHY: Downtrend describes an overall pattern, not a single candle or a certain future.

[MATCH] Match the trend type to its general description.
Uptrend => Price generally moving higher over time
Downtrend => Price generally moving lower over time
Sideways/range => Price moving without a clear overall direction
WHY: These are the three standard descriptive labels for overall price direction.

[FILL2] "Trend" is a [1] label for a pattern that has [2] happened, not a guarantee of what comes next.
BLANK1: *descriptive | predictive | random
BLANK2: *already | never | rarely
WHY: Trend describes the past and present pattern, not a certain future.

[ORDER] A simple way to get an initial read on a chart's trend:
Look at the overall shape across a longer timeframe
Note whether it's generally moving up, down, or sideways
Assign the matching descriptive label
Treat that label as a description, not a guarantee
WHY: This keeps the exercise descriptive rather than predictive.

[PICK] Which best describes what "trend" means as a term?
CHART: downtrend
CONCERN: Understanding trend terminology
A description of the general direction price has recently moved | detail: Descriptive label, not a guarantee
A guarantee that price will keep moving that way forever | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: A trend describes the past pattern, not a certain future.
A specific candlestick shape | detail: Not quite | wrong: Trend describes multiple candles together, not one shape.
WHY: "Trend" is a useful descriptive shorthand, not a promise about what happens next.

[SPOT] Someone assumes that because a chart has trended upward for months, it can never turn downward.
CHART: downtrend
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
A trend describes what has already happened. It doesn't guarantee it will continue forever.
They should have used a shorter timeframe instead.
They should have looked at a totally different asset.
WHY: Trends can and do change; treating a past trend as a permanent guarantee is a common misconception.


[MC] Which best describes a "sideways" or range-bound market?
Price moving without a clear overall upward or downward direction.
Price moving strongly and consistently higher.
Price moving strongly and consistently lower.
WHY: Sideways/range describes a lack of clear directional progress, unlike an uptrend or downtrend.

[TF] A downtrend can eventually turn into an uptrend over time.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Trends can and do change; a past trend doesn't lock in the future direction forever.

[FILL] Price generally moving from higher levels to lower levels over time is called a [___].
downtrend
uptrend
spread
WHY: "Downtrend" describes an overall pattern of declining price.

NODE 15
TITLE: Mini Simulation: Identify Direction
CELEBRATION: You can classify chart direction on sight!

INTRO
TITLE: Putting It Together
TEXT: Let's practice classifying a chart's overall direction, purely a descriptive exercise, not a prediction of what comes next.
CHART: uptrend

[MC] What is the overall direction shown in this chart?
CHART: uptrend
An uptrend: price generally moving higher over time
A downtrend: price generally moving lower over time
A sideways range with no clear direction
WHY: The overall shape here moves generally higher over the period shown.

[MC] What is the overall direction shown in this chart?
CHART: downtrend
A downtrend: price generally moving lower over time
An uptrend: price generally moving higher over time
A sideways range with no clear direction
WHY: The overall shape here moves generally lower over the period shown.

[MC] How would you classify the price action in this chart?
CHART: sideways
A sideways range: without a clear overall direction
A strong uptrend
A strong downtrend
WHY: Price here stays within a similar band rather than clearly trending.

[TF] A chart can only ever be classified as one of exactly three directions: up, down, or sideways.
ANSWER: True
WHY: These three descriptive labels are the standard way charts are broadly classified by overall direction.

[MATCH] Match the classification to its defining shape.
Uptrend => Generally moving from lower prices to higher prices
Downtrend => Generally moving from higher prices to lower prices
Sideways => Bouncing within a similar band without a clear direction
WHY: A quick recap of the three classifications from this unit.

[ORDER] A simple process for classifying any new chart's direction:
Look at where price started on the chart
Look at where price ended on the chart
Note whether it moved mostly up, down, or stayed within a band
Assign the matching classification
WHY: Comparing the start and end of a period is a simple first step toward classifying direction.

[PICK] What is the purpose of this kind of classification exercise?
CHART: uptrend
CONCERN: Understanding the point of the drill
Practicing a clear, descriptive read of what a chart has already done | detail: A literacy skill, not a forecast
Predicting the next candle with total certainty | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Classification describes the past, not a guaranteed future.
Deciding whether to buy or sell right now | detail: Out of scope | wrong: This drill is about reading direction, not making a trade decision.
WHY: This exercise builds descriptive chart-reading skill, not predictive or trading decisions.

[SPOT] Someone finishes this drill and claims they can now predict every future chart's direction with certainty.
CHART: uptrend
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
This drill practices describing direction that has already happened. It doesn't grant predictive certainty about the future.
They should have practiced on an even smaller chart.
They should have skipped straight to a real account instead.
WHY: This is a literacy exercise in reading what already happened, not a predictive skill.


[MC] What's the most reliable first step when classifying a chart's direction?
Compare where price started versus where it ended over the period.
Guess based on the chart's color scheme.
Ignore the chart and check the news instead.
WHY: Comparing the start and end points is a simple, direct way to gauge overall direction.

[TF] Classifying a chart's direction is the same thing as predicting its next move.
ANSWER: False
WHY: Classification describes what has already happened; it is not a prediction of the future.

[FILL] A chart bouncing within a similar band with no clear direction is classified as [___].
sideways
uptrend
downtrend
WHY: "Sideways" (or range-bound) is the classification for directionless bouncing.

UNIT 3 — Candlesticks
NODE 1
TITLE: What Is a Candlestick?
CELEBRATION: You can read the anatomy of a candle off a chart!

INTRO
TITLE: The Full Anatomy
TEXT: Every candle has a <b>body</b> (the open-to-close range) and <b>wicks</b> (the highs and lows it reached). Reading both off the chart is the core skill of this unit.
CHART: marubozu

[MC] Look at the tall highlighted candle above. Its thick middle section represents what?
CHART: marubozu
The open-to-close range (the "real body").
The single highest price of the day only.
The trading volume for that period.
WHY: The thick body always spans the open and close; the thin wicks show the extremes.

[TF] On the chart above, the thin lines poking out of each candle's body are the wicks (the high/low reach).
CHART: marubozu
ANSWER: True
WHY: Wicks (or shadows) mark how far price stretched beyond the body.

[FILL] A green (or hollow) candle like the highlighted one closed [___] than it opened.
CHART: marubozu
higher
lower
exactly equal
WHY: By convention a bullish/green candle closes above its open.

[MATCH] Match each part of the candle above to what it shows.
Real body => The open-to-close range
Upper wick => The high reached above the body
Lower wick => The low reached below the body
WHY: Body and wicks together tell the full story of the period.

[OHLC] Drag Open, High, Low, and Close onto the matching part of this candle.
WHY: High is the top wick and Low is the bottom wick. On a green candle the close is the body top and the open is the body bottom; on a red candle it's reversed.

[PICK] The highlighted candle has a large body and almost no wicks. What does that shape suggest?
CHART: marubozu
CONCERN: Reading a big-body candle
Price moved decisively one way and held there into the close | detail: A strong, one-sided period
That the chart data is broken | detail: Incorrect | wrong: A near-wickless candle is a normal, valid shape.
That nothing happened that period | detail: Opposite | wrong: A large body shows a big move, not a quiet one.
WHY: A big body with tiny wicks means price pushed one direction and stayed there.

[SPOT] Someone glances at the chart and reads only the wicks, ignoring the bodies entirely.
CHART: marubozu
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
The body and wicks are read together, the body shows the net move, the wicks the extremes.
They should ignore the wicks too and just guess.
They should only ever look at the very first candle.
WHY: Reading body and wick together gives a fuller picture than either alone.


[TF] A candlestick with a small body and long wicks on both sides would generally NOT be called a marubozu.
CHART: marubozu
ANSWER: True
WHY: A marubozu specifically needs a large body and almost no wicks, the opposite of that description.

[FILL] Wicks show the [___] and low reached during the period, beyond the body.
high
volume
color
WHY: Wicks mark the high and low extremes; the body marks the open-to-close range.

[MC] If a candle's open and close are far apart, its body will be...
large.
tiny.
invisible.
WHY: A bigger gap between open and close makes for a larger body.

NODE 2
TITLE: Reading a Doji
CELEBRATION: You can spot a doji on a chart instantly!

INTRO
TITLE: When Neither Side Wins
TEXT: A doji has a tiny body, open and close almost equal, with wicks on both sides. It's commonly read as a moment of balance. The highlighted candle below is a doji.
CHART: doji

[MC] The highlighted candle above is best described as a...
CHART: doji
Doji: a tiny body with wicks on both sides.
Marubozu: a large body with no wicks.
Bullish engulfing: a big candle swallowing the one before it.
WHY: The near-nonexistent body is what makes the highlighted candle a doji.

[TF] The chart above shows a candle with a large, decisive body.
CHART: doji
ANSWER: False
WHY: The highlighted candle is a doji. Its body is tiny, the opposite of decisive.

[FILL] The tiny body on the highlighted candle is commonly read as market [___] between buyers and sellers.
CHART: doji
indecision
certainty
volume
WHY: Neither side pushed price clearly higher or lower by the close.

[PICK] Reading the doji on the chart above, what does it tell you on its own?
CHART: doji
CONCERN: Interpreting a doji from the chart
That buyers and sellers finished that period roughly balanced | detail: Descriptive, not predictive
That a reversal is guaranteed next | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: No single candle guarantees what comes next.
That the market is closed | detail: Unrelated | wrong: A doji is a normal candle, not a closed market.
WHY: A doji describes balance in that period; it doesn't predict the next move by itself.

[SPOT] Looking at this chart, someone calls the highlighted doji a "100% guaranteed reversal."
CHART: doji
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
A doji describes balance. No single candle is a guaranteed signal of what happens next.
The doji should have an even bigger body to count.
A doji can only ever appear at the start of a chart.
WHY: A doji is a useful descriptive clue, not a certainty.

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the doji on the chart.
CHART: doji
TARGET: 2
WHY: The middle candle has an almost nonexistent body with wicks on both sides. That's the doji.

[TF] A doji's open and close prices are nearly identical.
CHART: doji
ANSWER: True
WHY: The near-equal open and close is exactly what makes the tiny body of a doji.

[MC] Which best describes what "indecision" means for a doji?
Neither buyers nor sellers clearly won that period.
The chart data is missing.
The candle has no high or low.
WHY: Indecision describes a balanced tug-of-war, not missing data.

[FILL] A doji can appear at any point in a chart, [___] just at the very start.
not
only
always

NODE 3
TITLE: Reading a Hammer
CELEBRATION: You can identify a hammer on a chart!

INTRO
TITLE: A Long Lower Wick
TEXT: A hammer has a small body near the top and a long lower wick, appearing after a down-move. The long wick shows price was pushed down, then recovered by the close. The highlighted candle is a hammer.
CHART: hammer

[MC] The highlighted candle above, small body up top, long lower wick, after a drop, is a...
CHART: hammer
Hammer.
Shooting star.
Doji.
WHY: A long lower wick with the body near the top, after a decline, is the hammer shape.

[FILL] The long lower wick on the highlighted candle shows price was pushed down but [___] by the close.
CHART: hammer
recovered
collapsed further
never moved
WHY: The body finishing near the top means buyers pulled price back up before the close.

[TF] The chart above shows a candle with a long <b>upper</b> wick and body near the bottom.
CHART: hammer
ANSWER: False
WHY: This is a hammer, a long LOWER wick with the body near the top.

[PICK] What does the hammer on the chart above describe?
CHART: hammer
CONCERN: Reading the hammer in context
Sellers pushed price down, but buyers recovered much of it by the close | detail: A descriptive read of the wick
A guarantee price rises from here | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: The shape is descriptive, not a promise.
That the candle has no body at all | detail: Incorrect | wrong: A hammer has a small but real body near the top.
WHY: The hammer describes a failed push lower that buyers largely reversed.

[MATCH] Match each candle feature above to what it represents.
Long lower wick => Price was pushed down intraperiod
Small body near top => The close finished back near the high
Appears after a decline => The context that makes it a "hammer"
WHY: Shape plus context together define the hammer.

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the hammer on the chart.
CHART: hammer
TARGET: 3
WHY: The last candle has a small body up top and a long lower wick, the hammer.

[TF] A hammer's long wick points downward, below the body.
CHART: hammer
ANSWER: True
WHY: The long lower wick is what defines the hammer shape.

[MC] Where does a hammer's small body sit relative to the candle's overall range?
Near the top.
Near the bottom.
Exactly in the middle.
WHY: A hammer's body sits near the top, with the long wick trailing below it.

[FILL] A hammer commonly appears after a [___] in price.
decline
rise
gap
WHY: The hammer shape is read in the context of a preceding down-move.

NODE 4
TITLE: Reading a Shooting Star
CELEBRATION: You can identify a shooting star on a chart!

INTRO
TITLE: A Long Upper Wick
TEXT: A shooting star is the hammer's mirror image: a small body near the bottom and a long upper wick, appearing after an up-move. Price was pushed up but slid back by the close. The highlighted candle is a shooting star.
CHART: shootingStar

[MC] The highlighted candle above, small body near the bottom, long upper wick, after a rise, is a...
CHART: shootingStar
Shooting star.
Hammer.
Marubozu.
WHY: A long upper wick with the body near the bottom, after a rise, is the shooting star shape.

[FILL] The long upper wick on the highlighted candle shows price was pushed up but [___] by the close.
CHART: shootingStar
slid back down
kept climbing
never moved
WHY: The body finishing near the low means the higher prices were rejected before the close.

[TF] The chart above shows the same shape as a hammer.
CHART: shootingStar
ANSWER: False
WHY: It's the mirror image, a long UPPER wick (shooting star), not a long lower wick (hammer).

[PICK] What does the shooting star on the chart above describe?
CHART: shootingStar
CONCERN: Reading the shooting star
Buyers pushed price up, but it was rejected back down by the close | detail: A descriptive read of the upper wick
A guarantee price falls from here | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: The shape describes, it doesn't promise.
The exact volume traded | detail: Unrelated | wrong: Candle shape doesn't show volume directly.
WHY: The shooting star describes an upward push that got rejected.

[SPOT] Someone sees this chart and labels the highlighted candle a hammer.
CHART: shootingStar
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
The long wick here is on top, after a rise. That's a shooting star, not a hammer.
A shooting star and a hammer are the exact same shape.
Wick direction doesn't matter when naming a candle.
WHY: Which side the long wick is on (and the preceding move) is what separates the two.


[TF] A shooting star's long wick points upward, above the body.
CHART: shootingStar
ANSWER: True
WHY: The long upper wick is what defines the shooting star shape.

[MC] A shooting star commonly appears after what kind of move?
A preceding rise in price.
A preceding decline in price.
No preceding move at all.
WHY: The shooting star is read in the context of a preceding up-move.

[MATCH] Match each shape to its wick position.
Hammer => Long wick below the body
Shooting star => Long wick above the body
Doji => Wicks on both sides, tiny body
WHY: Wick position (and preceding trend) is what names each shape.


[SPOT] Someone assumes a shooting star's long wick means volume was extremely high.
CHART: shootingStar
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Wick length shows price extremes, not trading volume. They're two separate ideas.
Wick length always equals volume.
Volume can't be measured separately from wicks.
WHY: Wicks describe price reach, not trading volume.

[MATCH] Match each shape to the trend it commonly follows.
Hammer => A preceding decline
Shooting star => A preceding rise
Doji => Either. It just shows balance
WHY: Preceding trend is part of what gives each shape its common reading.

NODE 5
TITLE: Reading Bullish Engulfing
CELEBRATION: You can spot a bullish engulfing on a chart!

INTRO
TITLE: One Candle Swallows the Last
TEXT: A bullish engulfing is a small down candle followed by a larger up candle whose body fully covers it. The highlighted candle is the engulfing one.
CHART: bullishEngulfing

[MC] On the chart above, the highlighted candle's body fully covers the previous candle's body, and it closed up. This is a...
CHART: bullishEngulfing
Bullish engulfing.
Bearish engulfing.
Doji.
WHY: A larger up-candle body swallowing the prior down candle is the bullish engulfing shape.

[TF] In a bullish engulfing like the one shown, the second (highlighted) candle's body is smaller than the first.
CHART: bullishEngulfing
ANSWER: False
WHY: The whole point is the second body is LARGER. It engulfs the first.

[FILL] A bullish engulfing pattern needs the up candle to fully [___] the prior candle's body.
cover
match
ignore
WHY: "Engulfing" means the second body completely covers the first.

[PICK] What does the bullish engulfing on the chart above describe?
CHART: bullishEngulfing
CONCERN: Reading the pattern
Buyers overwhelmed the prior down candle within one period | detail: A descriptive read of the two candles
A guaranteed uptrend from here | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: A pattern describes; it doesn't guarantee.
That both candles are red | detail: Incorrect | wrong: The engulfing candle here is an up (green) candle.
WHY: The pattern describes buyers taking over that period, nothing guaranteed.

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the candle that engulfs the one before it.
CHART: bullishEngulfing
TARGET: 2
WHY: The second candle's larger up body fully covers the prior down candle.

[TF] A bullish engulfing pattern involves exactly two candles.
CHART: bullishEngulfing
ANSWER: True
WHY: The pattern is specifically a two-candle relationship, a small one, then a larger engulfing one.

[MC] In a bullish engulfing, which candle comes first?
The smaller down candle.
The larger up candle.
Neither. They're simultaneous.
WHY: The smaller down candle is followed by the larger engulfing up candle.

[FILL] The word "engulfing" describes one candle's body completely [___] another's.
covering
ignoring
matching
WHY: "Engulfing" specifically means the second body fully covers the first.


[FILL] The candle that gets engulfed in a bullish engulfing pattern is the [___] one.
first (down)
second (up)
neither
WHY: The smaller, earlier down candle is the one that gets engulfed.

[TF] Bullish engulfing can only occur after an uptrend, never after a decline.
ANSWER: False
WHY: Bullish engulfing is commonly read in the context of a prior decline, not an uptrend.

NODE 6
TITLE: Reading Bearish Engulfing
CELEBRATION: You can spot a bearish engulfing on a chart!

INTRO
TITLE: The Mirror Image
TEXT: A bearish engulfing is a small up candle followed by a larger down candle whose body fully covers it. The highlighted candle is the engulfing one.
CHART: bearishEngulfing

[MC] On the chart above, the highlighted down candle fully covers the previous up candle's body. This is a...
CHART: bearishEngulfing
Bearish engulfing.
Bullish engulfing.
Hammer.
WHY: A larger down-candle body swallowing the prior up candle is the bearish engulfing shape.

[TF] The chart above shows an up (green) candle engulfing a down candle.
CHART: bearishEngulfing
ANSWER: False
WHY: Here a DOWN candle engulfs an up candle. That's bearish engulfing.

[FILL] A bearish engulfing is commonly read as [___] taking over from buyers for that period.
sellers
volume
wicks
WHY: The larger down candle shows selling pressure overwhelmed the prior up candle.

[MATCH] Match each engulfing pattern to its description.
Bullish engulfing => Larger up candle covers a prior down candle
Bearish engulfing => Larger down candle covers a prior up candle
Neither => Two candles of the same small size, no engulfing
WHY: The engulfing direction depends on which candle swallows the other.

[SPOT] Someone sees this chart and calls it bullish because "there's a big candle."
CHART: bearishEngulfing
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Size alone isn't the point. Here the big candle is a DOWN candle engulfing an up one, which is bearish.
A big candle always means bullish.
The color of the engulfing candle never matters.
