WHY: Direction of the engulfing candle, not just its size, sets bullish vs bearish.


[TF] A bearish engulfing pattern involves exactly two candles.
CHART: bearishEngulfing
ANSWER: True
WHY: Like bullish engulfing, this is a two-candle relationship.

[MC] In a bearish engulfing, which candle comes first?
The smaller up candle.
The larger down candle.
Neither. They're simultaneous.
WHY: The smaller up candle is followed by the larger engulfing down candle.

[PICK] What does a bearish engulfing describe about that period?
CHART: bearishEngulfing
CONCERN: Reading the pattern
Sellers overwhelmed the prior up candle within one period | detail: A descriptive read
A guaranteed downtrend forever | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: A pattern describes, it doesn't guarantee.
That both candles are the same color | detail: Incorrect | wrong: One is up, one is down. That's the point.
WHY: The pattern describes selling pressure taking over, not a guaranteed outcome.


[FILL] The candle that gets engulfed in a bearish engulfing pattern is the [___] one.
first (up)
second (down)
neither
WHY: The smaller, earlier up candle is the one that gets engulfed.

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the engulfing candle on the chart.
CHART: bearishEngulfing
TARGET: 2
WHY: The second, larger down candle is the one that engulfs the first.

NODE 7
TITLE: Reading a Spinning Top
CELEBRATION: You can identify a spinning top on a chart!

INTRO
TITLE: A Small Body, Wicks Both Sides
TEXT: A spinning top has a small body with wicks of similar length on both sides, like a doji but with a slightly larger body. It's commonly read as hesitation. The highlighted candle is a spinning top.
CHART: spinningTop

[MC] The highlighted candle, small body, similar wicks on both sides, is a...
CHART: spinningTop
Spinning top.
Marubozu.
Bearish engulfing.
WHY: A small body centered between two similar wicks is the spinning top shape.

[TF] The chart above shows a candle with a large body and no wicks.
CHART: spinningTop
ANSWER: False
WHY: A spinning top has a SMALL body with wicks on both sides, the opposite of a marubozu.

[FILL] A spinning top is commonly read as [___], with neither side clearly in control.
hesitation
certainty
a guarantee
WHY: The small body and balanced wicks suggest an indecisive period.

[PICK] How does the spinning top on the chart differ from a marubozu?
CHART: spinningTop
CONCERN: Telling the two shapes apart
The spinning top has a small body and wicks; a marubozu has a big body and almost none | detail: Opposite ends of the "decisiveness" scale
They are exactly the same shape | detail: Incorrect | wrong: One shows hesitation, the other decisiveness.
The spinning top has no body at all | detail: Incorrect | wrong: It has a small but visible body.
WHY: Body size and wick presence are what separate hesitation from decisiveness.


[TF] A spinning top has wicks of roughly similar length on both sides.
CHART: spinningTop
ANSWER: True
WHY: Balanced wicks on both sides, with a small body, is the spinning top shape.

[MC] Compared to a doji, a spinning top's body is...
slightly larger, but still small.
much larger.
completely nonexistent.
WHY: A spinning top has a bit more body than a doji, but still small overall.

[SPOT] Someone claims a spinning top always signals an immediate reversal.
CHART: spinningTop
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
A spinning top describes hesitation, not a guaranteed reversal signal.
Spinning tops always reverse the trend immediately.
Spinning tops only appear once per chart.
WHY: Like other single candles, a spinning top is descriptive, not a certainty.


[FILL] A spinning top's wicks are roughly [___] in length on both sides.
equal
opposite
absent
WHY: Balanced wick length on both sides is part of the spinning top's definition.

[MC] Which is closest in meaning to a spinning top's "hesitation" reading?
Neither buyers nor sellers clearly won that period.
Buyers completely dominated.
Sellers completely dominated.
WHY: Hesitation means neither side won decisively.

NODE 8
TITLE: Reading a Marubozu
CELEBRATION: You can identify a marubozu on a chart!

INTRO
TITLE: All Body, No Wick
TEXT: A marubozu has a large body and almost no wicks, price opened at one end, closed at the other, and barely looked back. The highlighted candle is a marubozu.
CHART: marubozu

[MC] The highlighted candle, big body, almost no wicks, is a...
CHART: marubozu
Marubozu.
Doji.
Spinning top.
WHY: A large body with virtually no wicks is the marubozu shape.

[FILL] A marubozu is commonly read as a [___], one-sided period.
decisive
hesitant
balanced
WHY: The full body with no wicks shows price moved one way and held.

[TF] The chart above shows more hesitation than a spinning top would.
CHART: marubozu
ANSWER: False

ANSWER: True

[MC] Which best describes the price action inside a marubozu candle?
Price moved in one direction steadily and closed near its extreme.
Price bounced back and forth with no clear direction.
Price didn't move at all.
WHY: A marubozu reflects steady, one-directional movement through the period.

[FILL] A marubozu's lack of wicks suggests neither side pushed price [___] before the close.
back

sideways
WHY: With no meaningful wick, price wasn't pushed back from its extreme before closing.


[FILL] A marubozu's open is at (or very near) one extreme, and its close is at the [___] extreme.
opposite
same

WHY: Opening at one extreme and closing at the other, with little wick, is the marubozu shape.

[MC] The highlighted candle's long lower wick is commonly read as a rejection of the...
CHART: rejectionWickDown
lows, price dipped, then buyers pushed it back up.
highs, price spiked, then sellers pushed it back down.
volume, the number of shares traded.

ANSWER: True

[FILL] A rejection wick shows price [___] a level but failed to hold there into the close.
reached

Price is certain to reverse now | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: A rejection is a clue, not a promise.
The wick shows the trading volume | detail: Incorrect | wrong: Wicks show price extremes, not volume.
WHY: Rejection wicks describe where price failed to hold. Context, not certainty.


[TF] A rejection wick shows price failed to hold at an extreme it briefly reached.
CHART: rejectionWickDown
ANSWER: True
WHY: The wick marks a level price touched but couldn't sustain into the close.

[MC] A long lower wick on a candle is commonly called a rejection of the...
lows.
highs.
open price.

Long lower wick => Rejection of the lows
Long upper wick => Rejection of the highs
Small wick => Little rejection either way
WHY: Wick length and direction show which extreme was tested and rejected.


[FILL2] A rejection of the [1] shows a long lower wick; a rejection of the [2] shows a long upper wick.
BLANK1: *lows | highs | open
BLANK2: *highs | lows | close
WHY: Lower wick = lows rejected; upper wick = highs rejected.

TITLE: Identification Drill: Reversal Shapes
CELEBRATION: You can tell reversal candles apart at a glance!

INTRO
TITLE: Name the Shape
TEXT: Time to read charts fast. For each chart, identify the highlighted candle.

[MC] Which candle is highlighted on the chart above?
CHART: hammer
Hammer: long lower wick, body up top.

Doji: almost no body.

[MC] Which candle is highlighted on the chart above?
CHART: shootingStar
Shooting star: long upper wick, body down low.
Hammer: long lower wick, body up top.
Marubozu: big body, no wick.
WHY: Long upper wick with the body near the bottom is a shooting star.

CHART: invertedHammer
Inverted hammer: small body low, long upper wick, after a drop.
Marubozu: big body, no wick.

CHART: hangingMan
Hanging man: small body up top, long lower wick, after a rise.
Bullish engulfing: big up candle covering the last.
Doji: almost no body.
WHY: A hammer shape appearing after a rise is called a hanging man.

[TF] The chart above shows a doji.
CHART: doji
ANSWER: True
WHY: Tiny body with wicks both sides. That's a doji.

CHART: hangingMan
ANSWER: True
WHY: Same shape, different context, a hammer after a decline, a hanging man after a rise.

[MC] What separates a hanging man from a hammer?
The trend context it appears in (a rise vs. a decline).
The candle's color.
The number of wicks.
WHY: Both share the same shape; only the preceding trend differs.

[FILL] Correctly identifying a candle shape means checking both its form and its [___].
context
color
volume

INTRO
TITLE: From Shape to Meaning
TEXT: Now go past naming, read what each chart <b>suggests</b>, always descriptively.

[PICK] The highlighted candle on the chart above has a long lower wick. What does it suggest, descriptively?
CHART: rejectionWickDown
Sellers pushed price down, but buyers recovered it by the close | detail: A read of the lower wick
Price is guaranteed to rise next | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: A rejection is a clue, not a promise.
The market data is broken | detail: Incorrect | wrong: A long wick is a normal, valid candle.
WHY: A long lower wick describes a failed push down that buyers reversed.

[PICK] The highlighted candle on the chart above has a long upper wick. What does it suggest?
CHART: rejectionWickUp
Buyers pushed price up, but sellers rejected it by the close | detail: A read of the upper wick
A guaranteed crash | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Descriptive, not a promise.
That volume was zero | detail: Incorrect | wrong: Wicks show price extremes, not volume.
WHY: A long upper wick describes an upward push that got rejected.

[TF] The hammer on the chart above suggests sellers stayed fully in control into the close.
CHART: hammer
ANSWER: False

[SPOT] Someone reads the rejection wick on this chart as a guaranteed reversal.
CHART: rejectionWickDown
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
A rejection wick is descriptive context. It doesn't guarantee the direction of the next candle.
Rejection wicks always mean the trend reverses.
The wick shows the exact future price.
WHY: Rejection describes what happened at a level, not what happens next.


[TF] "What a candle suggests" is always a certain, guaranteed prediction.
ANSWER: False
WHY: These reads are descriptive clues, never guarantees about the future.

[MC] Reading a rejection wick's "suggestion" means considering...
what the wick implies about buyer/seller behavior that period.
only the wick's exact pixel length.
the account balance.
WHY: The suggestion is about behavior (who got rejected), not a measurement exercise.

[FILL] Moving from naming a shape to reading its suggestion adds a layer of [___].
interpretation
certainty
volume
WHY: Interpreting what a shape implies goes beyond simply naming it.


[MC] What's the difference between "naming" a candle and reading its "suggestion"?
Naming identifies the shape; suggestion interprets what it implies about behavior.
They are exactly the same task.
Suggestion is about volume, naming is about color.
WHY: Naming is identification; suggestion is interpretation of the shape's meaning.

[FILL] A rejection wick's suggestion is read [___], not as a certainty.
descriptively
as a guarantee
randomly
WHY: All single-candle suggestions in this unit stay descriptive, not certain.

Hanging man.
Hammer.
Doji.

CHART: invertedHammer
Inverted hammer.
Shooting star.
Marubozu.

[TF] Where a candle appears in the trend can change what it's called and what it suggests.
CHART: hangingMan
ANSWER: True
WHY: Identical shapes (hammer vs hanging man) get different names based on the preceding move.

[PICK] Why read a candle alongside the trend around it, as on the chart above?
CHART: invertedHammer

The same shape can mean different things depending on what came before it | detail: Context shapes the read
Because the trend never matters | detail: Incorrect | wrong: Context is central to reading candles.
Because it guarantees the next move | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Context informs, it doesn't guarantee.
WHY: Reading a candle in context is what turns a shape into a meaningful description.


[TF] The same candle shape can carry a different name depending on the preceding trend.
CHART: hangingMan
ANSWER: True
WHY: Hammer vs. hanging man, and shooting star vs. inverted hammer, are exactly this kind of pair.

[MC] Which pair of shapes shares an identical form but different context-based names?
Hammer and hanging man.

WHY: Hammer/hanging man is the classic same-shape-different-context pair.

[FILL] Reading candles "in context" means considering the trend that came [___] them.
before

without regard to
WHY: The preceding trend is the context that shapes a candle's meaning.


[FILL] An inverted hammer appears after a [___], while a shooting star appears after a rise.

rise
gap
WHY: Same upper-wick shape, but the inverted hammer's context is a preceding decline.

[TF] Context can change a candle's name even when its shape stays identical.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Hammer/hanging man and shooting star/inverted hammer are examples of this.

strong, sustained momentum.
a guaranteed reversal.

UNIT 4 — Orders & Execution
NODE 1
TITLE: Market Orders (Deep Dive)
CELEBRATION: You understand market orders in depth!

INTRO
TITLE: Prioritizing Speed
TEXT: A market order executes immediately at the best currently available price, a deeper look at the order type introduced back in Unit 1.
CHART: uptrend

[TF] A market order guarantees a specific, pre-chosen execution price.
ANSWER: False
WHY: A market order executes at the best available price at that moment, not a chosen one.

[FILL] A market order prioritizes [___] of execution above all else.
speed
price
patience
WHY: Speed is the defining trade-off a market order makes.

[MC] Why might a market order fill at a slightly different price than the last quote you saw?
Price can shift in the brief moment between placing and executing the order.
Market orders always execute exactly one minute later.
Market orders are only valid on weekends.
WHY: Prices can move quickly, so the fill may differ slightly from what was last displayed.

[MATCH] Match the order concern to its relevance to market orders.
Speed => The main priority of a market order
Exact price => Not guaranteed with a market order
Liquidity => Affects how much the fill price may differ from the last quote
WHY: These concepts together explain how market orders behave in practice.

[FILL2] In a fast-moving, [1] liquidity market, a market order's fill price may differ [2] from the last quote.
BLANK1: *low | high | zero
BLANK2: *more | less | never
WHY: Thinner liquidity can mean bigger gaps between the last quote and the actual fill.

[ORDER] What happens step by step with a market order:
The order is submitted
It matches against the best currently available price
It executes without waiting for a specific level
Confirmation of the fill is received
WHY: Market orders are built for immediate execution, not price precision.

[PICK] When might a market order's fill differ most from the last displayed price?
CHART: uptrend
CONCERN: Understanding execution risk
In a fast-moving or thin-liquidity market | detail: Price can shift quickly with fewer participants
In a very calm, highly liquid market | detail: Less likely | wrong: Calm, liquid markets tend to have smaller gaps between quote and fill.
Only on public holidays | detail: Unrelated | wrong: Holiday scheduling isn't what drives this gap.
WHY: Fast-moving or thin markets are where the gap between expected and actual fill price tends to be largest.

[SPOT] Someone is surprised their market order filled at a noticeably different price during a very fast-moving moment.
CHART: uptrend
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Market orders prioritize speed over exact price, so some difference is expected in fast conditions.
The order must have been placed incorrectly.
Market orders should never be used under any circumstances.
WHY: This is a normal trade-off of the order type, not evidence of an error.


[FILL] Once submitted, a market order typically executes [___] rather than waiting.
right away
after a delay
never
WHY: Immediate execution is the whole point of a market order.

[TF] A market order can be used to both buy and sell.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Market orders apply to both buying and selling; speed of execution is the shared trait.

NODE 2
TITLE: Limit Orders (Deep Dive)
CELEBRATION: You understand limit orders in depth!

INTRO
TITLE: Prioritizing Price
TEXT: A limit order only executes at your chosen price or better, trading execution certainty for price control.
CHART: resistanceRejection

[TF] A limit order is guaranteed to execute immediately once placed.
ANSWER: False
WHY: A limit order only executes if the market reaches your specified price. It may never fill.

[FILL] A limit order to buy will only execute at your set price or [___].
lower
higher
random
WHY: A buy limit order fills at your specified price or better (lower).

[MC] What is the main trade-off of using a limit order?
You gain price control but risk the order never filling.
You gain guaranteed execution but lose all price control.
There is no trade-off at all.
WHY: Limit orders trade execution certainty for a chosen price level.

[MATCH] Match the order type to its execution behavior.
Buy limit => Fills at your price or lower
Sell limit => Fills at your price or higher
Market order => Fills immediately at the best available price
WHY: Reviewing how each order type behaves once triggered.

[FILL2] A [1] order may never fill, while a [2] order fills almost immediately.
BLANK1: *limit | market | stop
BLANK2: *market | limit | stop
WHY: This is the central speed-versus-price trade-off between the two order types.

[ORDER] What happens step by step with a limit order:
The order is submitted with a specific price
It waits until the market reaches that price (or better)
If reached, it executes at that price or better
If never reached, it remains unfilled
WHY: A limit order is patient by design, unlike a market order.

[PICK] Which best describes a limit order that never fills?
CHART: resistanceRejection
CONCERN: Understanding unfilled limit orders
The market simply never reached the specified price | detail: Expected behavior, not an error
The order was corrupted by the platform | detail: Unlikely explanation | wrong: An unfilled limit order is usually just a matter of price never being reached.
Limit orders are broken and unreliable | detail: Overgeneralizes | wrong: This is normal, expected order behavior.
WHY: An unfilled limit order most commonly just reflects price never reaching that level.

[SPOT] Someone is frustrated that their limit order didn't fill even though price came close to their level.
CHART: resistanceRejection
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
"Close" isn't the same as reaching the exact specified price, the order was working as designed.
The platform must have malfunctioned.
Limit orders should always be replaced with market orders.
WHY: A limit order requires the exact specified price (or better) to be reached, not just approached.


[FILL] A sell limit order will only execute at your set price or [___].
higher
lower
random
WHY: A sell limit order fills at your specified price or better (higher).

[TF] A limit order can remain unfilled indefinitely if price never reaches it.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Without a time limit, a limit order simply waits until price reaches the specified level (or is cancelled).

NODE 3
TITLE: Stop-Loss Orders
CELEBRATION: You understand stop-loss orders!

INTRO
TITLE: A Planned Exit
TEXT: A stop-loss order triggers a trade once price reaches a specified level, commonly used to define risk in advance.
CHART: downtrend

[TF] A stop-loss order exists specifically to guarantee a profit.
ANSWER: False
WHY: A stop-loss is commonly used to limit potential losses, not to guarantee profit.

[FILL] An order that triggers a sale once price falls to a specified level is called a [___] order.
stop-loss
limit
market
WHY: "Stop-loss" is the standard term for this kind of risk-defining order.

[MC] Why might someone place a stop-loss order in advance?
To define, ahead of time, the price at which they'd exit if the position moves against them.
To guarantee the exact fill price no matter what.
To prevent the market from ever moving against them.
WHY: A stop-loss is a plan set in advance. It defines a level, not a guaranteed price or outcome.

[MATCH] Match the order concept to its description.
Stop-loss order => Triggers an exit once a specific price level is reached
Take-profit order => Triggers an exit once a specific favorable price level is reached
Market order (once triggered) => How a triggered stop typically then executes
WHY: These three concepts are commonly compared together as planning tools.

[FILL2] A stop-loss order is often described as a way to define [1] in advance, not to guarantee a specific [2].
BLANK1: *risk | profit | color
BLANK2: *price | risk | candle
WHY: Once triggered, a stop-loss commonly behaves like a market order, so the final fill price isn't guaranteed.

[ORDER] A simple way to think through setting a stop-loss level:
Decide on a price level that would change your view of the position
Set that level as your stop-loss
If price reaches it, the order triggers
The resulting exit executes based on available market prices
WHY: This describes the mechanical process, without implying any particular outcome.

[PICK] Which best describes what a stop-loss order is used for, conceptually?
CHART: downtrend
CONCERN: Understanding stop-loss orders
Defining a price level in advance, as part of a risk plan | detail: Descriptive, not a guarantee
Guaranteeing you will never lose money | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: A stop-loss limits how a plan unfolds; it doesn't guarantee outcomes.
Guaranteeing the exact same fill price every time | detail: Incorrect | wrong: Once triggered, actual fill price can vary depending on market conditions.
WHY: A stop-loss order is a planning tool, not a guarantee of outcome or price.

[SPOT] Someone believes setting a stop-loss means they can never lose more than expected, no matter what.
CHART: downtrend
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Fast-moving markets can sometimes fill a triggered stop-loss at a worse price than expected. It isn't an absolute guarantee.
They should have avoided using any stop-loss at all.
They should have only used a stop-loss on weekends.
WHY: A stop-loss defines an intended exit level, but the exact executed price can still vary in fast-moving conditions.


[FILL] A stop-loss is commonly placed at a level that would [___] the original trade idea.
invalidate
guarantee
double
WHY: A stop-loss is commonly tied to the point where the reasoning for the trade no longer holds.

[TF] Once triggered, a basic stop-loss order commonly behaves like a market order.
ANSWER: True
WHY: A triggered stop typically executes at the best available price, like a market order.

NODE 4
TITLE: Take-Profit Orders
CELEBRATION: You understand take-profit orders!

INTRO
TITLE: A Planned Exit on the Upside
TEXT: A take-profit order triggers an exit once price reaches a specified favorable level, commonly used to lock in a planned target in advance.
CHART: uptrend

[TF] A take-profit order is the same thing as a stop-loss order.
ANSWER: False
WHY: A take-profit targets a favorable exit level; a stop-loss targets an unfavorable one.

[FILL] An order set to exit once price reaches a favorable target is called a [___] order.
take-profit
stop-loss
market
WHY: "Take-profit" describes exiting at a pre-planned favorable level.

[MC] Why might someone set a take-profit level in advance?
To define, ahead of time, a target price at which they'd be satisfied exiting.
To guarantee the position will always reach that price.
To prevent any further price movement from happening.
WHY: A take-profit is a plan set in advance. It defines a target, not a guaranteed outcome.

[MATCH] Match the order type to its general purpose.
Take-profit => Plans an exit at a favorable target level
Stop-loss => Plans an exit at an unfavorable level, to limit loss
Both together => Define a full risk/reward plan for a position
WHY: These two order types are commonly used together to bracket a planned trade.

[FILL2] A take-profit order defines a [1] exit level, while a stop-loss defines an [2] one.
BLANK1: *favorable | unfavorable | random
BLANK2: *unfavorable | favorable | random
WHY: These two orders bracket the planned range of outcomes for a position.

[ORDER] A simple way to think through setting a take-profit level:
Decide on a price level that would represent a satisfying outcome
Set that level as your take-profit
If price reaches it, the order triggers
The position exits based on that plan
WHY: This describes the mechanical process, without guaranteeing that price will reach it.

[PICK] Which best describes what a take-profit order is used for?
CHART: uptrend
CONCERN: Understanding take-profit orders
Defining a favorable target level in advance, as part of a plan | detail: Descriptive, not a guarantee
Guaranteeing that price will always reach that level | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Setting a target doesn't guarantee price gets there.
Preventing any losses from ever occurring | detail: Incorrect | wrong: A take-profit addresses the upside target, not downside risk.
WHY: A take-profit order is a planning tool for a target, not a guarantee that price reaches it.

[SPOT] Someone assumes setting a take-profit guarantees price will eventually reach that level.
CHART: uptrend
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Setting a target doesn't guarantee price will ever reach it, the order simply waits until (and unless) it does.
They should set an even higher take-profit level instead.
They should remove all take-profit orders from now on.
WHY: A take-profit order defines an intended target; it doesn't guarantee that target will be reached.


[FILL] A take-profit order is commonly placed at a level considered a [___] outcome.
satisfying
guaranteed
random
WHY: The take-profit level represents a planned, satisfying exit point.

[TF] A take-profit and a stop-loss can be used together on the same position.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Using both together is a common way to bracket a planned trade's range of outcomes.

NODE 5
TITLE: Stop-Limit Orders
CELEBRATION: You understand stop-limit orders!

INTRO
TITLE: Combining Stop and Limit
TEXT: A stop-limit order combines a trigger price with a limit price, adding price control at the cost of a chance it may not fill.
CHART: breakout

[TF] A stop-limit order guarantees execution once the stop price is reached.
ANSWER: False
WHY: Once triggered, a stop-limit becomes a limit order, which may not fill if price moves past the limit price too quickly.

[FILL] A stop-limit order combines a trigger price with a specified [___] price.
limit
market
random
WHY: The "limit" part adds price control once the stop is triggered.

[MC] What happens once a stop-limit order's trigger price is reached?
It becomes a limit order at the specified limit price.
It becomes a guaranteed, immediate market order.
It cancels itself automatically.
WHY: A stop-limit order converts into a limit order once triggered, not a market order.

[MATCH] Match the order type to what it becomes once triggered.
Stop order (basic) => Typically becomes a market order
Stop-limit order => Becomes a limit order at the specified limit price
Limit order (standalone) => Was already a limit order from the start
WHY: This distinction is what separates a stop-limit from a basic stop order.

[FILL2] A stop-limit order adds [1] control, but with the risk of not [2] if price moves too fast.
BLANK1: *price | speed | volume
BLANK2: *filling | triggering | opening
WHY: The trade-off is price precision versus a chance of no execution at all.

[ORDER] What happens step by step with a stop-limit order:
The stop price is reached, triggering the order
It converts into a limit order at your specified limit price
If the market reaches that limit price, it fills
If not, it may remain unfilled
WHY: This shows how a stop-limit order differs from a plain stop order that becomes a market order.

[PICK] Which best describes the main risk of a stop-limit order compared to a basic stop order?
CHART: breakout
CONCERN: Comparing order types
It may not fill at all if price moves quickly past the limit price | detail: Trading execution certainty for price control
It always fills instantly regardless of price movement | detail: Contradicts the definition | wrong: That describes a basic stop order becoming a market order, not a stop-limit.
It guarantees a better price than a market order every time | detail: Overstates it | wrong: There's no such guarantee; it may simply not fill.
WHY: The added price control comes with the risk of the order not filling in a fast move.

[SPOT] Someone is confused why their stop-limit order didn't fill during a very fast price drop.
CHART: breakout
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
If price moved past the limit price too quickly, the order can remain unfilled. That's a known trade-off of this order type.
The platform must have a bug.
Stop-limit orders always behave exactly like market orders.
WHY: A fast move past the limit price is a well-known way a stop-limit order can fail to fill.


[FILL] Once a stop-limit's trigger price is hit, it converts into a [___] order.
limit
market
stop
WHY: The defining feature of a stop-limit is converting into a limit order once triggered.

[TF] A stop-limit order guarantees a fill, just like a market order does.
ANSWER: False
WHY: Unlike a market order, a stop-limit may not fill if price moves past the limit price too fast.

NODE 6
TITLE: Slippage
CELEBRATION: You understand slippage!

INTRO
TITLE: The Gap Between Expected and Actual
TEXT: Slippage describes the difference between the price you expected and the price you actually got.
CHART: breakout

[TF] Slippage means you always get exactly the price you expected.
ANSWER: False
WHY: Slippage specifically refers to getting a different price than expected, usually during fast price movement.

[FILL] The difference between the expected price and the actual executed price is called [___].
slippage
spread
structure
WHY: "Slippage" is the standard term for this gap.

[MC] Why does slippage tend to increase during fast-moving markets?
Prices can change quickly between when an order is placed and when it executes.
Fees are automatically higher on weekends.
Charts stop updating during fast moves.
WHY: Rapid price movement is a common reason execution price can differ from the expected price.

[MATCH] Match the condition to its typical effect on slippage.
Fast-moving market => Slippage tends to be larger
Low liquidity => Slippage tends to be larger
Calm, liquid market => Slippage tends to be smaller
WHY: These conditions are commonly linked to how much slippage tends to occur.

[FILL2] Ignoring slippage can make a trading plan look [1] on paper than it performs in [2].
BLANK1: *better | worse | identical
BLANK2: *practice | theory | color
WHY: Slippage is a real-world cost that's easy to overlook when reviewing a plan only on paper.

[ORDER] A simple way to account for slippage when reviewing a trade idea:
Note the expected entry and exit price
Consider potential slippage during fast moves
Compare the more realistic total cost to the original plan
Adjust expectations accordingly
WHY: This gives a more complete, honest picture than looking at expected prices alone.

[PICK] Which best describes when slippage is likely to be largest?
CHART: breakout
CONCERN: Understanding slippage conditions
During fast-moving, low-liquidity conditions | detail: Bigger, quicker price changes with fewer participants
During calm, highly liquid conditions | detail: Less likely | wrong: Calm, liquid conditions tend to have smaller slippage.
Only during scheduled maintenance windows | detail: Unrelated | wrong: Maintenance windows aren't what drives slippage.
WHY: Fast-moving, thin markets are where the gap between expected and actual price tends to be largest.

[SPOT] Someone calculates a trading plan's results using only the exact prices shown on a chart, ignoring slippage entirely.
CHART: breakout
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Ignoring slippage makes a plan look better on paper than it would likely perform in practice.
They should use an even older chart instead.
They should only look at longer wicks.
WHY: A realistic picture accounts for costs like slippage beyond just the chart's displayed prices.


[FILL] Slippage tends to be [___] in calm, liquid markets.
smaller
larger
guaranteed
WHY: Calmer, more liquid conditions are commonly linked to smaller slippage.

[TF] Slippage can occur on both market and other order types once triggered.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Any order that ends up executing like a market order can experience slippage.

NODE 7
TITLE: Why Execution Differs From Chart Price
CELEBRATION: You understand why real fills can differ from the chart!

INTRO
TITLE: The Chart Isn't the Whole Story
TEXT: Several factors, spread, slippage, and order type, can all cause your actual execution price to differ from what a chart displays.
CHART: sideways

[TF] The price shown on a chart always exactly matches every trader's actual execution price.
ANSWER: False
WHY: Spread, slippage, and order type can all cause real execution to differ from the displayed chart price.

[FILL] The gap between bid and ask is one commonly cited reason execution can differ from the [___] chart price.
displayed
future
guaranteed
WHY: The displayed "last price" on a chart isn't necessarily the exact bid or ask you'd transact at.

[MC] Which of these commonly contributes to execution differing from the chart price?
Bid/ask spread, slippage, and the type of order used.
The chart's color scheme.
The number of indicators displayed on screen.
WHY: These three factors are commonly cited reasons real fills can differ from a displayed price.

[MATCH] Match the factor to how it can affect execution.
Spread => Bid and ask differ from the single "last price" shown
Slippage => Price can shift between order placement and execution
Order type => Market vs. limit orders behave differently around a specific price
WHY: Each of these factors contributes a different piece of the gap between chart and execution.

[FILL2] Combining [1], [2], and order type together explains most differences between chart price and actual fills.
BLANK1: *spread | volume | color
BLANK2: *slippage | trend | doji
WHY: These three factors together commonly explain most of the gap.

[ORDER] A simple way to set realistic expectations before placing an order:
Note the current bid/ask spread
Consider potential slippage given current volatility/liquidity
Consider how the chosen order type will behave
Expect some difference from the exact displayed chart price
WHY: This builds a habit of realistic expectations rather than assuming an exact match.

[PICK] Which best explains why a market order's fill might differ from the "last price" on a chart?
CHART: sideways
CONCERN: Understanding the full picture
Spread and potential slippage between placing and executing the order | detail: Multiple factors combine here
The chart is always inaccurate and unreliable | detail: Overstates it | wrong: Charts are generally accurate; small execution gaps are a separate, normal phenomenon.
Market orders never actually execute at all | detail: Incorrect | wrong: Market orders do execute, just not always at the exact last displayed price.
WHY: Spread and slippage together are the most commonly cited reasons for this gap.

[SPOT] Someone assumes any difference between chart price and their actual fill means the platform cheated them.
CHART: sideways
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Small differences between chart price and fills are commonly explained by spread, slippage, and order type, not platform wrongdoing.
They should assume all trading platforms are identical in this regard.
They should stop using charts entirely.
WHY: These small gaps are a normal, well-understood part of how markets and order types work.


[FILL] Order type is one of [___] factors that can explain a gap between chart price and fill price.
several
zero
a single
WHY: Spread, slippage, and order type together are commonly cited as the reasons for this gap.

[TF] A limit order and a market order can behave differently around the very same price level.
ANSWER: True
WHY: A market order fills immediately regardless of exact price, while a limit order waits for a specific level.

NODE 8
TITLE: Orders & Execution Review
CELEBRATION: You've completed the Orders & Execution unit!

INTRO
TITLE: Bringing It Together
TEXT: A final review of the order types and execution concepts from this unit.
CHART: doji

[TF] Every order type in this unit guarantees a specific outcome once placed.
ANSWER: False
WHY: Each order type has its own trade-offs; none guarantees a specific price or fill with certainty.

[FILL] The order type that prioritizes speed over price is the [___] order.
market
limit
stop-limit
WHY: A market order specifically prioritizes fast execution over a chosen price.

[MC] What do market, limit, stop-loss, take-profit, and stop-limit orders all have in common?
They're all tools for defining how and when a trade executes, each with its own trade-offs.
They all guarantee execution at the exact price you want.
They only apply to one specific asset class.
WHY: Each order type is a tool with a distinct trade-off, not a guarantee.

[MATCH] Match the order type to its primary purpose.
Market order => Immediate execution at the best available price
Limit order => Execution only at a chosen price or better
Stop-loss / Take-profit => Planning an exit level in advance
WHY: A quick recap of the core order types from this unit.

[FILL2] Reading order types well means understanding their [1], not expecting a [2] outcome from any of them.
BLANK1: *trade-offs | guarantee | color
BLANK2: *guaranteed | descriptive | random
WHY: Each order type balances speed, price control, and risk differently, none is a guarantee.

[ORDER] The general path this unit followed:
Learn the basic trade-off between market and limit orders
Learn about stop-loss, take-profit, and stop-limit orders
Learn about slippage and why real fills can differ from the chart
Combine all of it into a realistic view of execution
WHY: Each lesson built on the one before it toward a fuller understanding of execution mechanics.

[PICK] Which best summarizes the overall theme of this unit?
CHART: doji
CONCERN: Reviewing the big picture
Order types are tools with trade-offs, not guarantees of a specific outcome | detail: The central theme throughout
Every order type guarantees an exact, predictable result | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: This directly contradicts what every lesson covered.
Order types don't matter and can be chosen at random | detail: Overcorrects | wrong: Choosing the right order type for the situation does matter.
WHY: This balanced view has been the throughline of every lesson in this unit.

[SPOT] Someone finishes this unit and assumes there's one single "best" order type to use in every situation.
CHART: doji
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Different order types suit different situations, there's no single type that's best in every case.
They should only ever use market orders from now on.
They should only ever use limit orders from now on.
WHY: This unit covered several order types precisely because each fits different situations and priorities.


[FILL] This unit's order types each balance speed, price control, and [___] differently.
risk
color
volume
WHY: Speed, price control, and risk are the three dimensions each order type trades off.

[TF] Knowing multiple order types lets you choose the one that fits a given situation.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Different situations call for different trade-offs, which is why multiple order types exist.

UNIT 5 — Market Mechanics
NODE 1
TITLE: Order Flow (Simplified)
CELEBRATION: You understand order flow at a basic level!

INTRO
TITLE: The Stream of Buy and Sell Orders
TEXT: Order flow describes the ongoing stream of buy and sell orders hitting the market. Watching which side is more aggressive, in simple terms, is the basic idea behind reading order flow.

[MC] "Order flow" refers to...
The ongoing stream of buy and sell orders hitting the market.
The company's quarterly earnings report.
A single candle's wick length.
WHY: Order flow describes the continuous stream of incoming buy/sell orders.

[TF] Order flow describes a single trade, not an ongoing stream of orders.
ANSWER: False
WHY: Order flow specifically describes the ongoing, continuous stream, not one isolated trade.

[FILL] Order flow is commonly described as which side is more [___] at a given moment.
aggressive
colorful
random
WHY: Reading order flow, simply put, means noticing which side (buyers or sellers) is pushing harder.

[MATCH] Match the term to its simplified meaning.
Order flow => The ongoing stream of buy/sell orders
Aggressive buying => Buyers pushing to get filled quickly
Aggressive selling => Sellers pushing to get filled quickly
WHY: These terms describe the basic push-and-pull behind order flow.

[PICK] Why do some chart readers pay attention to order flow?
CONCERN: Understanding the basic idea
It's one simplified way to gauge which side currently has more pressure | detail: Descriptive, not predictive
It guarantees the next price move | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Order flow describes current pressure, not a guaranteed outcome.
It replaces the need to look at price at all | detail: Incomplete | wrong: Order flow is read alongside price, not instead of it.
WHY: Order flow is a descriptive read of current buy/sell pressure, not a guaranteed prediction.

[SPOT] Someone believes reading order flow lets them know the exact next price with certainty.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Order flow describes current buy/sell pressure. It doesn't guarantee the exact next price.
Order flow guarantees perfect predictions every time.
Order flow has no relationship to price at all.
WHY: Order flow is descriptive context about pressure, not a certainty about the future.

NODE 2
TITLE: Supply vs. Demand
CELEBRATION: You understand supply and demand in markets!

INTRO
TITLE: The Basic Economic Engine
TEXT: Just like any market, price reflects the balance of supply (sellers willing to sell) and demand (buyers willing to buy) at each price level.
CHART: uptrend

[MC] When demand exceeds supply at a price, price commonly tends to...
Rise.
Fall.
Stay perfectly fixed forever.
WHY: More buying demand than available supply commonly pushes price up.

[TF] When supply exceeds demand at a price, price commonly tends to fall.
ANSWER: True
WHY: More sellers willing to sell than buyers willing to buy commonly pushes price down.

[FILL] Price is commonly described as reflecting the balance of supply and [___].
demand
volume
color
WHY: Supply and demand together are the basic economic engine behind price.

[MATCH] Match the imbalance to its commonly cited price effect.
More demand than supply => Price tends to rise
More supply than demand => Price tends to fall
Balanced supply and demand => Price tends to stay steadier
WHY: This is the same buyer/seller balance idea from earlier lessons, framed as supply and demand.

[PICK] Which best describes how supply and demand relate to price?
CHART: uptrend
CONCERN: Understanding the basic mechanism
Price commonly reflects the shifting balance between supply and demand | detail: A foundational economic idea
Supply and demand have no relationship to price at all | detail: Incorrect | wrong: This is the basic mechanism behind price moves.
Supply and demand guarantee a specific future price | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: It's a descriptive mechanism, not a guarantee.
WHY: Supply and demand together are commonly described as the basic engine behind price.

[SPOT] Someone claims supply and demand have nothing to do with why a stock's price changes.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Supply and demand are commonly described as the basic economic engine behind price changes.
Price changes for reasons totally unrelated to buyers and sellers.
Supply and demand only apply to physical goods, never stocks.
WHY: This mechanism applies to stocks (and most markets) just as it does to other goods.

NODE 3
TITLE: Market Makers (Simple Explanation)
CELEBRATION: You understand market makers, simply!

INTRO
TITLE: Standing Ready to Trade
TEXT: A market maker is a participant who commonly stands ready to buy and sell continuously, helping keep a market liquid by always offering a bid and ask.

[MC] What does a market maker commonly do?
Stand ready to continuously buy and sell, helping keep the market liquid.
Set the company's stock price by decree.
Only trade once per year.
WHY: Market makers commonly provide continuous liquidity by standing ready to trade.

[TF] Market makers commonly help keep a market liquid.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Their continuous willingness to buy and sell adds liquidity to the market.

[FILL] A market maker commonly profits from the [___] between the bid and ask they quote.
spread
volume
timeframe
WHY: The bid/ask spread is commonly cited as a source of market maker profit.

[MATCH] Match the term to its simplified role.
Market maker => Stands ready to continuously buy and sell
Liquidity => What market makers commonly help provide
Spread => What a market maker commonly profits from
WHY: These three ideas are commonly discussed together when explaining market makers simply.

[PICK] Why are market makers commonly discussed alongside liquidity?
CONCERN: Understanding the simplified role
Their continuous buying/selling commonly adds liquidity to the market | detail: A liquidity-providing role
They guarantee a stock will never crash | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Market makers add liquidity; they don't guarantee price outcomes.
They set official company policy | detail: Incorrect | wrong: Market makers facilitate trading, not company decisions.
WHY: Market makers are commonly described as liquidity providers, not price-outcome guarantors.

[SPOT] Someone believes market makers guarantee a stock can never crash.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Market makers commonly provide liquidity. They don't guarantee against price declines.
Market makers control all price outcomes.
Market makers have no role in markets at all.
WHY: Providing liquidity is a commonly cited role, distinct from guaranteeing any price outcome.

NODE 4
TITLE: Auction Theory Basics
CELEBRATION: You understand basic auction theory!

INTRO
TITLE: Markets as a Continuous Auction
TEXT: A market can be simply described as a continuous auction, where price moves to the level where buyers and sellers are willing to transact.

[MC] Thinking of a market as an "auction" simply means...
Price moves to where buyers and sellers are currently willing to transact.
Only one seller sets the price for everyone.
Prices are fixed and never move.
WHY: The auction framing describes price as constantly seeking a level both sides accept.

[TF] In this simplified auction view, price seeks a level where trade actually happens.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Price gravitates to levels where a buyer and seller are both willing to transact.

[FILL] A market can be simply described as a continuous [___].
auction
guarantee
company decision
WHY: "Continuous auction" is a common simplified way to describe ongoing price discovery.

[MATCH] Match the auction-theory term to its simplified meaning.
Continuous auction => Price constantly seeking a level both sides accept
Price discovery => The ongoing process of finding that agreed level
Transaction => A trade where a buyer and seller actually agree
WHY: These terms describe the basic auction-like process behind price formation.

[PICK] Which best captures this simplified "auction" view of markets?
CONCERN: Understanding the basic framing
Price is constantly seeking a level where trade actually happens | detail: An ongoing, ever-shifting search
Price is permanently fixed by a single authority | detail: Incorrect | wrong: The auction view describes constant, shifting negotiation.
This view guarantees where price ends up | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: It's descriptive, not predictive.
WHY: The continuous-auction framing describes ongoing price discovery, not a fixed or guaranteed outcome.

[SPOT] Someone believes a single company employee sets the price under this "auction" framing.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
The auction framing describes price emerging from many participants, not one person's decision.
One person sets the auction price for everyone.
Auctions have no relationship to how markets work.
WHY: This is the same idea revisited from earlier lessons, price emerges from many participants, not one.

NODE 5
TITLE: Why Candles Form
CELEBRATION: You understand why candles form the shapes they do!

INTRO
TITLE: The Mechanics Behind a Candle's Shape
TEXT: A candle's shape reflects the underlying order flow and supply/demand tug-of-war during that period, big bodies from decisive pressure, long wicks from rejected pushes.
CHART: hammer

[MC] Why might a candle end up with a long lower wick, mechanically?
Sellers pushed price down, but buying pressure pushed it back up before the close.
The chart software randomly added a wick.
Wicks are unrelated to buying and selling pressure.
WHY: A long lower wick reflects a mechanical tug-of-war: a push down that got reversed by buying pressure.

[TF] A candle's shape reflects the underlying supply/demand tug-of-war during that period.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Body size and wick length are mechanical traces of that period's buying/selling pressure.

[FILL] A large candle body reflects [___], decisive pressure from one side.
strong
weak
random
WHY: A big body mechanically reflects one side dominating that period.

[MATCH] Match the candle feature to its underlying mechanical cause.
Large body => Decisive, one-sided pressure that period
Long wick => A push that was mechanically reversed before the close
Tiny body => Roughly balanced pressure between both sides
WHY: Candle shapes are mechanical traces of the underlying order flow.

[PICK] Why do candle shapes connect back to order flow and supply/demand?
CHART: hammer
CONCERN: Connecting mechanics to shape
Because body and wick size are mechanical traces of that period's buy/sell pressure | detail: Shape reflects underlying mechanics
Because candle shapes are randomly generated | detail: Incorrect | wrong: Shapes reflect real trading activity, not randomness.
Because order flow has no relationship to candles | detail: Backwards | wrong: This lesson's whole point is that they're connected.
WHY: This connects the "why" (mechanics) to the "what" (candle shapes) taught earlier in the course.

[SPOT] Someone believes candle shapes are unrelated to actual buying and selling activity.
CHART: hammer
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Candle shapes are mechanical traces of real order flow and supply/demand pressure during that period.
Candles are drawn randomly with no connection to trading.
Only the color of a candle matters, not its shape.
WHY: Body and wick size directly reflect the underlying buy/sell pressure for that period.

NODE 6
TITLE: Why Trends Accelerate
CELEBRATION: You understand why trends can accelerate!

INTRO
TITLE: Momentum Feeding on Itself
TEXT: A trend can accelerate when order flow and sentiment feed on themselves, more participants join a move already in progress, adding to the existing pressure.
CHART: breakout

[MC] Why might a trend commonly accelerate?
More participants join a move already in progress, adding to existing pressure.
Acceleration happens for no describable reason.
Every trend accelerates at exactly the same fixed rate.
WHY: More participants adding to an existing move is a commonly cited reason trends can accelerate.

[TF] A trend can accelerate when more participants join an already-moving direction.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Additional participants adding pressure in the same direction can speed up a trend.

[FILL] Trend acceleration is commonly linked to momentum [___] on itself.
feeding
disappearing
freezing
WHY: "Feeding on itself" describes how added participation can compound an existing move.

[MC] Does every trend accelerate the same way?
No, acceleration varies and isn't guaranteed for every trend.
Yes, every trend accelerates identically.
Trends never accelerate under any circumstances.
WHY: Acceleration is a commonly observed tendency, not a guaranteed or uniform feature of every trend.

[PICK] Which best describes why a trend might accelerate?
CHART: breakout
CONCERN: Understanding this mechanism
Added participants joining an existing move can compound the pressure | detail: A mechanical, descriptive explanation
Acceleration is entirely random with no cause | detail: Overcorrects | wrong: There's a commonly cited mechanical explanation.
Acceleration is guaranteed once a trend starts | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Acceleration is a tendency, not guaranteed.
WHY: Added participation compounding existing pressure is the commonly cited mechanism, without guaranteeing it happens every time.

[SPOT] Someone assumes every trend must accelerate at some predictable, fixed point.
CHART: breakout
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Acceleration is a commonly observed tendency, not a guaranteed or perfectly predictable feature.
All trends accelerate at the exact same point.
Trends never accelerate at all.
WHY: Acceleration can happen, but it's not a fixed, guaranteed pattern.

NODE 7
TITLE: Why Reversals and Breakouts Happen
CELEBRATION: You understand the mechanics behind reversals and breakouts!

INTRO
TITLE: When the Balance Shifts
TEXT: A reversal happens when the supply/demand balance shifts enough to overpower the prior trend. A breakout happens when demand (or supply) finally overwhelms a level that had been holding.
CHART: resistanceRejection

[MC] A reversal is commonly linked to...
A shift in the supply/demand balance strong enough to overpower the prior trend.
A single random candle appearing on the chart.
The chart's software resetting.
WHY: A reversal reflects a mechanical shift in the underlying balance of pressure.

[TF] A breakout happens when demand or supply finally overwhelms a level that had been holding.
ANSWER: True
WHY: A breakout mechanically reflects one side finally overpowering a previously respected level.

[FILL] A reversal reflects a [___] in the underlying supply/demand balance.
shift
guarantee
random event
WHY: A shift strong enough to overpower the prior trend is what mechanically produces a reversal.

[MATCH] Match the event to its underlying mechanical cause.
Reversal => A shift in balance strong enough to overpower the prior trend
Breakout => One side finally overwhelming a level that had been holding
Order flow => The ongoing stream of pressure behind both events
WHY: Both reversals and breakouts trace back to the same underlying order flow and supply/demand mechanics.

[PICK] Which best connects breakouts back to earlier mechanics lessons in this unit?
CHART: resistanceRejection
CONCERN: Tying mechanics together
A breakout reflects demand or supply finally overwhelming a level, via the same order-flow mechanics | detail: Connects mechanism to event
A breakout has no relationship to supply, demand, or order flow | detail: Backwards | wrong: This lesson's point is exactly that connection.
A breakout guarantees the new direction continues forever | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: As covered earlier, breakouts can still fail.
WHY: This ties the "why" mechanics (order flow, supply/demand) back to the "what" (breakouts, reversals) from earlier units.

[SPOT] Someone treats reversals and breakouts as totally random events with no underlying mechanism.
CHART: resistanceRejection

UNIT 6 — Volume Analysis
NODE 1
TITLE: Reading Volume Bars
CELEBRATION: You understand volume bars!

INTRO
TITLE: A Bar for Every Candle
TEXT: Most charts show a volume bar beneath each candle. Its height reflects how many shares traded during that period. Reading volume alongside price is a core intermediate skill.

[MC] A volume bar's height reflects...
How many shares traded during that period.
The candle's exact color.
The company's total revenue.
WHY: Volume bar height specifically shows shares traded in that period.

[TF] Volume bars are typically read alongside the price candle above them, not in isolation.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Volume adds context to the price action it sits beneath.

[FILL] A taller volume bar means [___] shares traded that period.
more
fewer
zero
WHY: Height directly reflects the amount traded.

[MATCH] Match the volume bar height to its meaning.
Tall bar => More shares traded that period
Short bar => Fewer shares traded that period
Volume bar => A visual count of shares traded per period
WHY: Bar height is a direct, visual read of trading activity.

[PICK] Why read volume alongside price, rather than price alone?
CONCERN: Understanding the value of volume
Volume adds context about how much participation was behind a price move | detail: Participation context
Volume replaces the need to look at price entirely | detail: Incomplete | wrong: Volume is read alongside price, not instead of it.
Volume guarantees the next price move | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Volume is descriptive context, not a guarantee.
WHY: Volume adds participation context to a price move. It doesn't replace or guarantee anything.

[SPOT] Someone reads volume bars completely separately, without ever looking at the price candles above them.

Volume should always be read alone.
Volume has no relationship to price at all.
WHY: Volume and price are read together for a fuller picture.

TITLE: High vs. Low Volume

INTRO
TITLE: Compared to What's Typical
TEXT: "High" or "low" volume is judged relative to what's typical for that asset, a big bar for one stock might be an ordinary bar for another.

[MC] Volume is commonly judged as "high" or "low" relative to...
What's typical for that specific asset.
A single universal number for all stocks.
The time of day only.
WHY: High/low volume is a relative judgment, compared to that asset's own typical activity.

[TF] A volume level that's "high" for one stock could be perfectly ordinary for a much more actively traded stock.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Volume is judged relative to each asset's own typical activity level, not a single universal threshold.

[FILL] High or low volume is a [___] judgment, not a fixed universal number.
relative
absolute
random
WHY: Comparing to what's typical for that specific asset is the relative approach.

[MC] Why might comparing volume to recent history matter?
It helps judge whether current activity is unusually high or low for that asset.
It has no bearing on reading volume at all.
It only matters for the very first candle on a chart.
WHY: Comparing to recent history gives a relative baseline for judging current volume.

[PICK] How should "high volume" be judged?
CONCERN: Reading volume relatively
Relative to what's typical for that specific asset | detail: A comparison, not a fixed number
Using one single universal volume number for every stock | detail: Overstates it | wrong: Typical volume varies widely between assets.
Ignoring recent history entirely | detail: Incomplete | wrong: Recent history is exactly the baseline used for comparison.
WHY: Volume is judged relative to that asset's typical activity, not a fixed universal figure.

[SPOT] Someone assumes any stock trading 1 million shares must have "high" volume, without checking that stock's typical activity.

Without comparing to that stock's typical volume, there's no way to know if 1 million shares is high or low for it.
Any large-sounding number is automatically high volume.
Volume never needs a comparison point.
WHY: A single number means nothing without comparing it to that asset's own typical activity.

Having more participation behind the move.
Guaranteed to succeed.
Identical in meaning to a low-volume breakout.

ANSWER: False
WHY: Volume level is commonly read as a meaningful difference between the two, not identical.

[FILL] A breakout with strong volume behind it is commonly described as having more [___].
participation
guarantee
certainty
WHY: Higher volume reflects more participants involved in that move.

Breakout on low volume => Less participation, commonly read with more caution

Identical in meaning to a quiet-volume reversal.

significance
guarantee
certainty

Reversal on low volume => Commonly read with more caution
Volume at a reversal => Another commonly cited form of confluence

UNIT 7 — Price Action Vocabulary
NODE 1
TITLE: Swing Highs & Swing Lows
CELEBRATION: You understand swing highs and lows!

INTRO
TITLE: The Turning Points
TEXT: A swing high is a peak where price turned down; a swing low is a trough where price turned up. These turning points are the raw building blocks behind trend and structure.
CHART: pullbackUptrend

[MC] A "swing high" is...
A peak where price turned down afterward.
A trough where price turned up afterward.
Any random candle on the chart.
WHY: A swing high specifically marks a turning point where price reversed downward.

[TF] A swing low is a trough where price turned up afterward.
ANSWER: True
WHY: That's the standard definition of a swing low.

[FILL] Swing highs and lows are the raw building blocks behind [___] and structure.
trend
volume
color
WHY: These turning points are what trend/structure classification is built from.

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the swing high on this chart.
CHART: pullbackUptrend
TARGET: 1
WHY: The 1st candle's peak, right before the pullback, is a swing high.

[MATCH] Match the term to its meaning.
Swing high => A peak where price turned down
Swing low => A trough where price turned up
Turning point => The general term for either one
WHY: These are the basic vocabulary for describing price's ups and downs.

[PICK] Why are swing highs and lows described as "raw building blocks"?
CHART: pullbackUptrend
CONCERN: Understanding their role
Trend and structure classifications are built by comparing sequences of them | detail: Foundational vocabulary
They have no relationship to trend classification | detail: Backwards | wrong: Trend classification is built directly from comparing these points.
They only apply to downtrends | detail: Incomplete | wrong: Swing highs/lows apply to any market condition.
WHY: Comparing sequences of swing highs/lows is literally how trend structure gets classified.

[SPOT] Someone tries to classify a trend without ever identifying any swing highs or lows.
CHART: pullbackUptrend
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Trend classification is built from comparing swing highs and lows, skipping them removes the basis for the read.
Swing highs and lows are unnecessary for trend reading.
Any single candle can replace a full swing sequence.
WHY: Swing highs/lows are the foundational vocabulary trend classification relies on.

NODE 2
TITLE: Impulses & Corrections
CELEBRATION: You understand impulses and corrections!

INTRO
TITLE: The Push and the Pause
TEXT: An impulse is a strong, decisive move in the trend's direction. A correction is the smaller counter-move that follows, the same idea as a pullback, named differently.
CHART: pullbackUptrend

[MC] An "impulse" is...
A strong, decisive move in the trend's direction.
A small counter-move against the trend.
A single candle with no body.
WHY: Impulse describes the strong, decisive leg of a trend.

[TF] A correction is the same basic idea as a pullback, just a different name.
ANSWER: True
WHY: "Correction" and "pullback" both describe a temporary counter-move within a trend.

[FILL] An impulse moves [___] with the trend; a correction moves against it.
in line
opposite
sideways only
WHY: Impulse legs align with the trend direction; corrections move against it temporarily.

[MATCH] Match the term to its role.
Impulse => The strong, decisive leg in the trend's direction
Correction => The smaller counter-move that follows
Impulse-correction cycle => The repeating pattern that builds a trend
WHY: Trends are commonly described as a repeating cycle of impulses and corrections.

[PICK] How does an impulse differ from a correction?
CHART: pullbackUptrend
CONCERN: Telling them apart
An impulse is the strong move with the trend; a correction is the smaller pause against it | detail: Direction and size both differ
They are exactly the same thing | detail: Incorrect | wrong: One moves with the trend, the other against it.
Impulses only happen in downtrends | detail: Incomplete | wrong: Impulses occur in either direction, aligned with whichever trend is in play.
WHY: Direction (with vs. against the trend) is what separates an impulse from a correction.

[SPOT] Someone labels every single candle in an uptrend as an "impulse," including the pullbacks.
CHART: pullbackUptrend
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Only the strong, trend-aligned legs are impulses. The counter-trend dips are corrections, not impulses.
Impulses and corrections are the same label for everything.
Corrections don't exist as a separate concept.
WHY: Impulses and corrections describe two distinct, alternating phases within a trend.

Price ranges narrowing over time.

A single candle's exact color.

compression
expansion
neither

Expansion => Price ranges widening, often after compression

WHY: These terms describe the narrowing-then-widening rhythm commonly seen in price.

[PICK] What commonly follows a period of compression?

Expansion, the range widening out again | detail: A commonly observed rhythm

NODE 4

CELEBRATION: You understand rejection and acceptance!

INTRO
TITLE: Testing a Level
TEXT: Rejection describes price testing a level and getting pushed back (like a long wick). Acceptance describes price testing a level and then holding there, trading through it comfortably.
CHART: rejectionWickUp

[MC] "Rejection" at a level describes...
Price testing the level and getting pushed back.
Price testing the level and comfortably holding there.
A candle with no wicks at all.
WHY: Rejection specifically describes a failed test that gets pushed back.

[TF] "Acceptance" describes price testing a level and then comfortably holding or trading through it.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Acceptance is the opposite of rejection, the level is absorbed rather than pushed back from.

[FILL] A long wick at a level is commonly read as a sign of [___].
rejection
acceptance
neither
WHY: A long wick shows a level was tested and pushed back from, rejection.

[MATCH] Match the term to its description.
Rejection => Price tested a level and got pushed back
Acceptance => Price tested a level and comfortably held there
Both => Ways of describing how price behaves at a tested level
WHY: Rejection and acceptance are two contrasting ways price can behave at the same level.

[PICK] How would you describe price that breaks a level and then holds calmly above it?

CONCERN: Reading level behavior
Acceptance, the level was tested and comfortably held through | detail: Calm holding above the level
Rejection, the level pushed price back | detail: Opposite behavior | wrong: Rejection describes a push-back, not a calm hold.
Neither term applies here | detail: Incomplete | wrong: Acceptance specifically describes this calm-holding behavior.
WHY: Calmly holding through a level, rather than being pushed back, is acceptance.

Wick length has no relationship to rejection or acceptance.

TITLE: Keep Going or Run Out of Steam
TEXT: Continuation describes a move persisting in its existing direction. Exhaustion describes signs a move may be running out of steam, like shrinking bodies or rejected pushes.

[MC] "Continuation" describes...
A move persisting in its existing direction.
A move suddenly reversing direction.
A candle with no body.
WHY: Continuation specifically describes the existing direction persisting.

[TF] Exhaustion describes signs a move may be running out of steam.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Shrinking bodies or rejected pushes are commonly cited exhaustion signs.

[FILL] Shrinking candle bodies late in a move are a commonly cited sign of [___].
exhaustion
continuation
acceptance
WHY: Smaller bodies suggest weakening momentum, a commonly cited exhaustion sign.

[MATCH] Match the term to its description.
Continuation => The existing move persisting
Exhaustion => Signs the move may be running out of steam
Both => Contrasting reads of an ongoing trend's health
WHY: Continuation and exhaustion are contrasting ways to read whether a move still has strength.

[PICK] How should exhaustion signs be read?
CHART: pullbackUptrend
CONCERN: Reading them honestly
As a descriptive clue the move may be weakening, not a guaranteed end | detail: Descriptive, not certain
As a guarantee the move ends immediately | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Exhaustion signs are clues, not guarantees.
As proof continuation is impossible from here | detail: Overstates it | wrong: The move can still continue despite exhaustion signs.
WHY: Exhaustion signs are descriptive clues about weakening momentum, not a certain ending.

CHART: pullbackUptrend
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
One candle is a clue, not a guarantee. Exhaustion signs describe a possibility, not a certainty.
A single candle always ends a trend for certain.
Exhaustion signs never mean anything at all.
WHY: Exhaustion signs are descriptive context, read alongside everything else, not a standalone guarantee.

TITLE: Reading Context

TEXT: Context, where a candle or move sits relative to the broader trend, level, and recent structure, changes how every term in this unit should be read.
CHART: breakoutRetest

[MC] Why does "context" matter so much in price action reading?

WHY: The surrounding trend and levels can change the meaning of an identical shape or move.

[TF] An impulse in an uptrend and an impulse in a downtrend describe the same underlying idea, just in different directions.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Impulse describes a strong, trend-aligned move regardless of which direction the trend runs.

[FILL] Reading price action well means combining vocabulary with [___].
context
guesswork
random guessing
WHY: Context is what turns raw vocabulary into a meaningful read.

Continuation vs. exhaustion => Depends on where the move sits in its overall structure

WHY: Every term in this unit changes meaning depending on the surrounding context.

[PICK] Which best captures why context matters in price action reading?
CHART: breakoutRetest

CHART: breakoutRetest

Vocabulary without context misses the surrounding trend and levels that actually give each term its meaning.
Memorizing terms is the only skill that matters here.
Context has no relationship to price-action vocabulary.
WHY: Context is what turns raw vocabulary into a meaningful, applied reading skill.

UNIT 8 — Trends & Structure
NODE 1
TITLE: Uptrend Basics
CELEBRATION: You understand uptrends!

INTRO
TITLE: Climbing Step by Step
TEXT: An uptrend is commonly described as a series of higher swing highs and higher swing lows.
CHART: uptrend

[TF] An uptrend is commonly described as higher swing highs and higher swing lows over time.
ANSWER: True
WHY: That's the standard descriptive definition of an uptrend's structure.

[FILL] In an uptrend, each pullback often finds a [___] low than the one before.
higher
lower
identical
WHY: Higher lows stepping up is part of the classic uptrend description.

[MC] What would commonly lead a chart reader to classify a chart as an uptrend?
A pattern of higher swing highs and higher swing lows.
A single green candle appearing anywhere on the chart.
The asset's name containing a positive-sounding word.
WHY: Classification is based on the pattern of swings, not one candle or the name.

[MATCH] Match the trend feature to its role in an uptrend.
Higher highs => Each rally reaches further than the last
Higher lows => Each pullback holds above the prior low
Overall shape => A step-up pattern over time
WHY: These features together build the descriptive picture of an uptrend.

[FILL2] An uptrend is a [1] label describing a pattern that has already [2] happened.
BLANK1: *descriptive | predictive | random
BLANK2: *already | never | rarely
WHY: Trend labels describe the past and present pattern, not a guaranteed future.

[ORDER] A simple way to classify a chart as an uptrend:
Identify the recent sequence of swing highs and lows
Compare each swing to the one before it
Confirm both highs and lows are generally rising
Assign the "uptrend" label if so
WHY: This turns raw swing points into a simple, consistent classification process.

[PICK] Which best describes what "uptrend" means as a term?
CHART: uptrend
CONCERN: Understanding the terminology
A description of a pattern of rising swing highs and lows | detail: Descriptive label, not a guarantee
A guarantee that price will keep rising forever | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: A trend describes the past pattern, not a certain future.
A specific single candlestick shape | detail: Not quite | wrong: Trend describes multiple candles together, not one shape.
WHY: "Uptrend" is a useful descriptive shorthand, not a promise about what happens next.

[SPOT] Someone assumes a chart "can't" be in an uptrend just because one single candle closed red.
CHART: uptrend
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
One candle within an overall uptrend doesn't change the broader classification by itself.
They should have ignored the red candle's existence entirely.
They should have switched to a totally different asset.
WHY: Trend classification is based on the broader swing pattern, not any single candle.


[FILL] Both the highs and the lows in an uptrend generally move [___] together.
upward
downward
opposite
WHY: Both swing highs and swing lows rising together is the defining structure.

[TF] A single lower low automatically disqualifies an otherwise clear uptrend.
ANSWER: False
WHY: One isolated swing doesn't override the broader pattern; classification looks at the overall sequence.

NODE 2
TITLE: Downtrend Basics
CELEBRATION: You understand downtrends!

INTRO
TITLE: Stepping Down
TEXT: A downtrend is commonly described as a series of lower swing highs and lower swing lows.
CHART: downtrend

[TF] A downtrend is commonly described as lower swing highs and lower swing lows over time.
ANSWER: True
WHY: That's the standard descriptive definition of a downtrend's structure.

[FILL] In a downtrend, each rally often reaches a [___] high than the one before.
lower
higher
identical
WHY: Lower highs stepping down is part of the classic downtrend description.

[MC] What would commonly lead a chart reader to classify a chart as a downtrend?
A pattern of lower swing highs and lower swing lows.
A single red candle appearing anywhere on the chart.
The asset's name containing a negative-sounding word.
WHY: Classification is based on the pattern of swings, not one candle or the name.

[MATCH] Match the trend feature to its role in a downtrend.
Lower lows => Each decline reaches further than the last
Lower highs => Each rally fails to reach the prior high
Overall shape => A step-down pattern over time
WHY: These features together build the descriptive picture of a downtrend.

[FILL2] A downtrend is a [1] label, not a [2] of what happens next.
BLANK1: *descriptive | predictive | random
BLANK2: *guarantee | description | pattern
WHY: Trend labels describe the past and present, not a certain future.

[ORDER] A simple way to classify a chart as a downtrend:
