Compare each swing to the one before it
Confirm both highs and lows are generally falling
Assign the "downtrend" label if so
WHY: This turns raw swing points into a simple, consistent classification process.

[PICK] Which best describes what "downtrend" means as a term?
CHART: downtrend
CONCERN: Understanding the terminology
A description of a pattern of falling swing highs and lows | detail: Descriptive label, not a guarantee
A guarantee that price will keep falling forever | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: A trend describes the past pattern, not a certain future.
A specific single candlestick shape | detail: Not quite | wrong: Trend describes multiple candles together, not one shape.
WHY: "Downtrend" is a useful descriptive shorthand, not a promise about what happens next.

[SPOT] Someone says a chart "can't" be in a downtrend just because one single candle closed green.
CHART: downtrend
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
One candle within an overall downtrend doesn't change the broader classification by itself.
They should have ignored the green candle's existence entirely.
They should have switched to a totally different asset.
WHY: Trend classification is based on the broader swing pattern, not any single candle.


[FILL] Both the highs and the lows in a downtrend generally move [___] together.
downward
upward
sideways
WHY: Both swing highs and swing lows falling together is the defining structure.

[TF] A single higher high automatically disqualifies an otherwise clear downtrend.
ANSWER: False
WHY: One isolated swing doesn't override the broader pattern; classification looks at the overall sequence.

NODE 3
TITLE: Sideways Markets
CELEBRATION: You can spot sideways price action!

INTRO
TITLE: Going Nowhere (For Now)
TEXT: A sideways market bounces between a rough high and low without a clear overall direction.
CHART: sideways

[TF] A sideways market shows a clear upward direction.
ANSWER: False
WHY: A sideways market describes a lack of clear direction, bouncing within a band instead.

[FILL] Price moving back and forth between a rough ceiling and floor is often called a [___].
range
breakout
gap
WHY: "Range" (or sideways/consolidation) describes this kind of bounded movement.

[MC] What generally characterizes a sideways market?
Swing highs and lows staying within a similar band over time.
Swing highs and lows both rising steadily.
Swing highs and lows both falling steadily.
WHY: A lack of clear directional progress in the swings is the hallmark of sideways action.

[MATCH] Match the market phase to its swing description.
Uptrend => Swing highs and lows both rising
Downtrend => Swing highs and lows both falling
Sideways => Swing highs and lows staying roughly similar
WHY: Comparing all three side by side highlights what makes sideways distinct.

[FILL2] During a range, price tends to stay between a rough [1] and a rough [2].
BLANK1: *ceiling | trend | wick
BLANK2: *floor | breakout | doji
WHY: This floor/ceiling description matches the earlier support/resistance concept.

[ORDER] A simple way to identify a sideways market:
Look for a period without clear higher highs or lower lows
Note the rough high and low it's bouncing between
Confirm price has touched each side more than once
Label it as sideways/range for that period
WHY: Multiple touches on both sides is what distinguishes a range from a brief pause.

[PICK] Which best describes a chart currently moving sideways?
CHART: sideways
CONCERN: Reading non-trending price action
Bouncing between a rough high and low without a clear direction | detail: The standard description of sideways action
Making new all-time highs every single candle | detail: Doesn't match | wrong: That would describe a strong uptrend, not sideways action.
Falling to zero without pause | detail: Doesn't match | wrong: That would be an extreme downtrend, not sideways action.
WHY: Sideways action specifically describes bounded, directionless movement.

[SPOT] Someone assumes a sideways market will stay sideways forever.
CHART: sideways
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Sideways periods are commonly followed by a move in one direction or another. They don't last forever.
They should have widened their definition of the range instead.
They should have only looked at a shorter timeframe.
WHY: Treating any market phase as permanent overstates what is really just a temporary description.


[FILL] A sideways market is sometimes also called a [___].
range
breakout
gap
WHY: "Range" is a common alternate term for sideways price action.

[TF] A sideways market can eventually break into either an uptrend or a downtrend.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Ranges are commonly described as temporary, eventually resolving in one direction or another.

NODE 4
TITLE: Higher Highs & Higher Lows
CELEBRATION: You can spot the building blocks of an uptrend!

INTRO
TITLE: The Uptrend's Signature
TEXT: "Higher highs and higher lows" is the specific swing pattern behind every uptrend classification.
CHART: uptrend

[TAPCANDLES] Tap the two swing highs that make higher highs in this uptrend.
CHART: pullbackUptrend
TARGETS: 2, 4
WHY: Candles 2 and 4 are the swing highs, each peaking higher than the last, the "higher highs" of the uptrend.

[TF] "Higher highs and higher lows" describes the swing pattern behind an uptrend.
ANSWER: True
WHY: This phrase is the standard descriptive shorthand for uptrend structure.

[FILL] A swing high that is higher than the previous swing high is called a [___] high.
higher
lower
equal
WHY: "Higher high" specifically means it exceeds the prior swing high.

[MC] Why does a rising sequence of swing lows matter for an uptrend?
It shows pullbacks are holding at progressively higher levels.
It shows the asset is about to be delisted.
It has no relationship to trend classification.
WHY: Rising swing lows are part of the defining structure of an uptrend.

[MATCH] Match the swing sequence to its label.
Each high exceeds the last => Higher highs
Each low holds above the last => Higher lows
Both together over time => The structure behind an uptrend
WHY: These two sequences combined are what define uptrend structure.

[FILL2] If either swing highs or swing lows stop [1], the uptrend structure may be starting to [2].
BLANK1: *rising | falling | doubling
BLANK2: *weaken | strengthen | vanish
WHY: A break in this rising pattern is commonly noted as an early sign structure may be shifting.

[ORDER] A simple way to check for higher-highs-and-higher-lows structure:
Mark the recent swing highs in order
Mark the recent swing lows in order
Check that each high exceeds the last
Check that each low holds above the last
WHY: Confirming both sequences together is what verifies uptrend structure.

[PICK] Which best describes what "higher highs and higher lows" means?
CHART: uptrend
CONCERN: Understanding this specific phrase
Each new swing high and swing low both exceed the prior ones | detail: The defining uptrend structure
Only the highs need to rise; lows don't matter | detail: Incomplete | wrong: Both highs and lows rising together define this structure.
Only the lows need to rise; highs don't matter | detail: Incomplete | wrong: Both highs and lows rising together define this structure.
WHY: Both sequences rising together is what the phrase specifically describes.

[SPOT] Someone claims a chart has "higher highs and higher lows" after checking only the swing highs.
CHART: uptrend
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Confirming this structure requires checking both the swing highs AND the swing lows, not just one.
They should check the swing highs twice instead.
They should ignore swing lows entirely from now on.
WHY: Both sequences need to be checked together to properly confirm this structure.


[MC] Which best completes an uptrend's structure alongside higher highs?
Higher lows.
Lower lows.
Equal lows.
WHY: Higher highs paired with higher lows together define the uptrend structure.

[TF] Checking only swing highs is enough to confirm an uptrend.
ANSWER: False
WHY: Both swing highs and swing lows need to be checked together.

Each new swing high and swing low both fall below the prior ones | detail: The defining downtrend structure
Only the highs need to fall; lows don't matter | detail: Incomplete | wrong: Both highs and lows falling together define this structure.
Only the lows need to fall; highs don't matter | detail: Incomplete | wrong: Both highs and lows falling together define this structure.
WHY: Both sequences falling together is what the phrase specifically describes.

[SPOT] Someone claims a chart has "lower highs and lower lows" after checking only the swing lows.
CHART: downtrend
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Confirming this structure requires checking both the swing highs AND the swing lows, not just one.
They should check the swing lows twice instead.
They should ignore swing highs entirely from now on.
WHY: Both sequences need to be checked together to properly confirm this structure.

Equal highs.
WHY: Lower highs paired with lower lows together define the downtrend structure.

INTRO
TITLE: Steep vs. Gradual
TEXT: Trends aren't just "up" or "down". They can look stronger or weaker depending on how steadily price is moving.
CHART: breakout

[TF] All uptrends move at exactly the same speed and steepness.
ANSWER: False
WHY: Trends can vary widely in steepness and consistency, even when sharing the same overall direction.

[FILL] A trend with large, consistent moves in one direction is often described as [___].
strong
weak
sideways
WHY: "Strong" commonly describes a trend with consistent, sizable moves in one direction.

[MC] What might commonly suggest a trend is weakening?
Swings becoming smaller or less consistent than before.
The chart changing to a different color scheme.
The asset's name being mentioned in the news.
WHY: Smaller, less consistent swings are commonly read as a sign of weakening momentum.

[MATCH] Match the trend description to its common reading.
Large, consistent swings in one direction => Often read as a stronger trend
Small, inconsistent swings => Often read as a weaker or fading trend
Increasingly choppy price action => Often read as declining trend conviction
WHY: These descriptions offer a rough, non-certain way to gauge trend strength.

[FILL2] Trend strength is a matter of [1], not a simple [2] label.
BLANK1: *degree | binary | color
BLANK2: *binary | degree | random
WHY: Trends exist on a spectrum from very strong to very weak, not just "on" or "off."

[ORDER] A simple way to informally gauge trend strength:
Compare the size of recent swings to earlier ones
Note how consistently price is moving in one direction
Note whether pullbacks are getting larger or smaller
Form a general (not certain) sense of trend strength
WHY: These comparisons together give a rough sense of how strong a trend currently looks.

[PICK] Which best describes a "weakening" trend, as commonly read?
CHART: breakout
CONCERN: Reading trend strength
Swings becoming smaller and less consistent than before | detail: A commonly cited sign of fading momentum
Swings becoming larger and more consistent | detail: Opposite description | wrong: That would more commonly be read as a strengthening trend.
No candles forming at all | detail: Unrelated | wrong: A lack of candles isn't how trend strength is measured.
WHY: Smaller, less consistent swings are the commonly cited sign of a weakening trend.

[SPOT] Someone assumes trend strength is either "fully strong" or "fully weak," with nothing in between.
CHART: breakout
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Trend strength exists on a spectrum, not as a simple two-option label.
They should only ever describe trends as strong.
They should only ever describe trends as weak.
WHY: Treating strength as binary oversimplifies what's actually a matter of degree.


[FILL] Trend strength is commonly judged by comparing swing size to what's [___] for that chart.
typical
colorful
random
WHY: Comparing recent swings to what's typical gives a rough sense of relative strength.

[TF] A trend can remain in the same direction while getting weaker over time.
ANSWER: True

INTRO
TITLE: How Fast, Not Just Which Way

[TF] Momentum describes how quickly and forcefully price is currently moving.
ANSWER: True
WHY: That's the standard descriptive meaning of momentum in this context.

[FILL] A market with fast, forceful moves in one direction is often described as having strong [___].
momentum
liquidity
volume

[MC] How does momentum generally differ from trend?
Momentum describes the current speed/force of movement; trend describes overall direction over time.
They are exactly the same concept with different names.

[MATCH] Match the term to its focus.

Momentum => Current speed and force of movement
Volatility => Size and speed of price swings generally

BLANK1: *slow down | reverse | disappear
BLANK2: *same | different | random
WHY: A trend can continue in the same direction while its momentum weakens or strengthens.

[ORDER] A simple way to get a rough read on current momentum:

Note whether pullbacks are shallow or deep
Form a general (not certain) sense of current momentum
WHY: These comparisons together give a rough sense of how forceful the current move looks.

[PICK] Which best describes "momentum" as used here?
CHART: breakout
CONCERN: Understanding this specific term
The current speed and force behind a price move | detail: A description of intensity, not direction alone
The exact same concept as "trend" | detail: Conflates two terms | wrong: Trend describes direction; momentum describes speed/force.
A guarantee of how long a move will last | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Momentum describes the present, not a guaranteed duration.
WHY: Momentum specifically describes the intensity of a move, distinct from its direction.

[SPOT] Someone assumes strong momentum today guarantees the same strong momentum tomorrow.
CHART: breakout
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Momentum can shift day to day, strong momentum now doesn't guarantee it continues at the same intensity.
They should assume momentum can only ever increase.
They should assume momentum never changes once established.
WHY: Momentum is a current, changeable reading, not a fixed guarantee going forward.


[FILL] Momentum is often gauged by how [___] price is covering distance.
quickly
colorfully
randomly
WHY: Speed of movement is central to reading momentum.

[TF] Momentum and volatility describe the exact same thing.
ANSWER: False

NODE 8
TITLE: Pullbacks
CELEBRATION: You understand pullbacks within a trend!

INTRO
TITLE: A Pause, Not Necessarily a Reversal
TEXT: A pullback is a temporary move against the prevailing trend, commonly followed by a resumption of that trend, though not guaranteed.
CHART: uptrend

[TF] A pullback means the overall trend has definitely and permanently reversed.
ANSWER: False
WHY: A pullback is commonly described as a temporary move against the trend, not a guaranteed permanent reversal.

[FILL] A short-term move against the prevailing trend is commonly called a [___].
pullback
breakout
gap
WHY: "Pullback" is the standard term for this kind of temporary counter-move.

[MC] What is commonly true about a pullback within an uptrend?
It's a temporary dip that may be followed by the uptrend resuming.
It always means the uptrend has permanently ended.
It only happens exactly once per uptrend.
WHY: Pullbacks are commonly described as temporary pauses, not confirmed permanent reversals.

[MATCH] Match the term to its relationship with the trend.
Pullback => A temporary move against the current trend
Trend resumption => The prevailing trend continuing after a pullback
Reversal => The trend actually changing direction for good
WHY: These three terms describe different possible outcomes after a counter-move.

[FILL2] A pullback is commonly described as [1], while a full reversal describes the trend actually [2].
BLANK1: *temporary | permanent | random
BLANK2: *changing direction | pausing | consolidating
WHY: The key distinction is whether the move is a pause or a genuine change in direction.

[ORDER] A simple way to think through a pullback as it happens:
Notice price moving against the prevailing trend
Consider it a pullback candidate, not confirmed either way
Watch whether the prior trend direction resumes afterward
Only then describe it as a pullback or a reversal, after the fact
WHY: You typically can't be certain whether it's a pullback or reversal until after it's played out.

[PICK] Which best describes how to think about a fresh counter-trend move?
CHART: uptrend
CONCERN: Interpreting a possible pullback
It could be a temporary pullback or the start of a reversal, often unclear at first | detail: Honest uncertainty in the moment
It's always guaranteed to be just a pullback | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Some counter-moves do turn into full reversals.
It's always guaranteed to be a full reversal | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Many counter-moves are just temporary pullbacks.
WHY: In the moment, it's often genuinely unclear which one you're seeing. That's honest uncertainty, not a flaw.

[SPOT] Someone labels every single counter-trend move a "pullback" no matter how it later plays out.
CHART: uptrend
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Some counter-trend moves do turn into full reversals. Not every one stays a temporary pullback.
They should label every counter-move a reversal instead.
They should ignore counter-trend moves entirely.
WHY: Whether something was a pullback or a reversal is really only clear in hindsight.


[FILL] Whether a counter-trend move is a pullback or reversal is often only clear in [___].
hindsight
advance
theory
WHY: You typically can't be certain which one it is until after it plays out.

[TF] A pullback is generally smaller in scale than the trend it's occurring within.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Pullbacks are commonly described as smaller, temporary counter-moves relative to the larger trend.

NODE 9
TITLE: Consolidation
CELEBRATION: You understand consolidation phases!

INTRO
TITLE: Catching a Breath
TEXT: Consolidation describes a pause where price moves sideways within a tighter range, often after a stronger directional move.
CHART: sideways

[TF] Consolidation describes price moving sideways within a tighter range.
ANSWER: True
WHY: That's the standard descriptive meaning of consolidation.

[FILL] A period of sideways movement within a tighter range is commonly called [___].
consolidation
a breakout
a gap
WHY: "Consolidation" is the standard term for this kind of pause.

[MC] When does consolidation commonly appear on a chart?
Often after a stronger directional move, as a pause.
Only at the very start of any chart.
Only immediately before a market closes.
WHY: Consolidation is commonly noted as a pause following a stronger directional move.

[MATCH] Match the phase to its general description.
Trend (up or down) => Swings generally moving in one direction
Consolidation => A tighter, sideways pause, often after a stronger move
Breakout => Price clearly leaving a prior range or consolidation
WHY: These three phases are commonly described together as parts of a market's ongoing cycle.

[FILL2] Consolidation is often described as the market [1], before eventually [2] in one direction or another.
BLANK1: *pausing | crashing | ending
BLANK2: *resolving | consolidating | pausing
WHY: Consolidation is commonly viewed as a temporary phase that eventually resolves.

[ORDER] A simple way to identify a consolidation phase:
Look for a tightening, sideways range following a stronger move
Note how narrow the range is compared to the prior move
Confirm price is staying within that tighter band
Watch for how it eventually resolves
WHY: Comparing the range's width to the prior move helps confirm it's consolidation rather than just noise.

[PICK] Which best describes consolidation, as commonly used?
CHART: sideways
CONCERN: Understanding this specific term
A tighter, sideways pause, often following a stronger directional move | detail: A temporary phase, not a permanent state
A guarantee that price will break upward next | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Consolidation doesn't determine which direction it resolves.
The exact same thing as a full reversal | detail: Conflates two ideas | wrong: Consolidation is a pause, not necessarily a reversal.
WHY: Consolidation describes a temporary, tighter pause, not a certain outcome afterward.

[SPOT] Someone assumes consolidation always resolves in the same direction as the move that preceded it.
CHART: sideways
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Consolidation can resolve in either direction. It doesn't guarantee a continuation of the prior move.
They should assume it always reverses instead.
They should ignore consolidation phases entirely.
WHY: Which direction consolidation resolves in isn't something that can be assumed in advance.


[FILL] Consolidation is commonly described as tighter than the [___] that preceded it.
move
consolidation
gap
WHY: A consolidation range is typically narrower than the stronger move before it.

[TF] Consolidation can only occur after an uptrend, never after a downtrend.
ANSWER: False
WHY: Consolidation can follow either an uptrend or a downtrend as a pause.

NODE 10
TITLE: Break of Structure (Intro)

INTRO
TITLE: When the Pattern Changes
TEXT: A break of structure describes price breaking the established pattern of swing highs/lows, commonly noted as an early sign the trend may be shifting.
CHART: breakout

[TF] A break of structure means price has moved against the established pattern of recent swing highs/lows.
ANSWER: True
WHY: That's the core descriptive meaning of a structure break.

[FILL] Breaking the established pattern of swing highs and lows is commonly called a break of [___].
structure
volume
liquidity
WHY: "Break of structure" is the standard term for this kind of shift.

[MC] Why do chart readers commonly pay attention to a break of structure?
It's commonly noted as one of the earliest signs a trend's pattern may be shifting.
It guarantees the trend has permanently reversed.
It means the chart's data has become invalid.
WHY: A structure break is a commonly cited early clue, not a certainty about the future.

[MATCH] Match the structure event to its description.
Uptrend structure => Higher highs and higher lows holding
Break of structure (in an uptrend) => A swing low breaks below the prior swing low
Confirmed shift => The new pattern continuing after the break
WHY: These terms describe a possible sequence of events as structure changes.

[FILL2] A break of structure is commonly read as an early [1], not a guaranteed [2].
BLANK1: *signal | promise | wick
BLANK2: *confirmation | signal | pattern
WHY: It's commonly treated as a signal worth noting, not proof of what happens next.

[ORDER] A simple way to think through a possible break of structure:
Confirm the established swing high/low pattern beforehand
Notice a swing breaking that established pattern
Treat it as an early, not yet confirmed, signal
Watch subsequent price action for further confirmation
WHY: Patience before fully committing to a "confirmed" break is a commonly cited habit.

[PICK] Which best describes a break of structure, as commonly used?
CHART: breakout
CONCERN: Understanding this specific term
Price breaking the established pattern of recent swing highs/lows | detail: An early, descriptive signal
A guarantee the trend has permanently reversed | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: It's commonly read as an early signal, not a certainty.
A completely random, meaningless event | detail: Overcorrects | wrong: It's a commonly cited, useful piece of context.
WHY: A break of structure is a descriptive early clue, not a guaranteed outcome.

[SPOT] Someone treats every single break of structure as a guaranteed, permanent trend reversal.
CHART: breakout
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Not every break of structure leads to a lasting trend change, some fail and the prior trend resumes.
They should look for an even bigger break to be sure.
They should ignore breaks of structure entirely.
WHY: A break of structure is commonly read as an early signal, not a guaranteed permanent shift.


[FILL] A break of structure is commonly treated as an early, not [___], signal.
confirmed
random
descriptive
WHY: It's read as an early clue that may or may not be confirmed by what follows.

[TF] A break of structure can fail, with the prior trend resuming afterward.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Not every structure break leads to a lasting change, some fail and the prior trend continues.

swings
colors
timeframes only

pullback
breakout
marubozu

[MATCH] Match the phase to its general description.
Pullback => Temporary move against the prevailing trend
Consolidation => Tighter, sideways pause, often after a stronger move
Break of structure => Price breaking the established swing pattern
WHY: A quick recap of three related but distinct phases covered in this unit.

[PICK] Which best captures how to responsibly describe an in-progress counter-trend move?

"This is definitely just a pullback" | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Some counter-moves do become full reversals.

UNIT 9 — Support & Resistance
NODE 1
TITLE: What Is Support?
CELEBRATION: You understand support levels!

INTRO
TITLE: Price's Floor
TEXT: Support is a price area where a chart has previously had trouble falling below.
CHART: supportBounce

[TF] Support is a price area where a chart has previously had trouble falling below.
ANSWER: True
WHY: That's the standard descriptive definition of support.

[FILL] Support is often thought of as a price [___].
floor
ceiling
wick
WHY: The floor analogy is a common way to remember what support describes.

[MARKLEVEL] Drag the line to the support this chart keeps bouncing from.
CHART: supportBounce
LEVEL: support
WHY: The floor price keeps reacting off of is the support level.

[MC] Why might a chart reader mark a support level on a chart?
To note a price area where price has reacted in the past, for reference.
To guarantee price will bounce there again in the future.
To change the color of the candles.
WHY: Support levels are historical reference points, not guarantees of future behavior.

[MATCH] Match the term to its common description.
Support => A price area where a chart has previously had trouble falling below
Bounce => Price reacting upward after reaching a support area
Break of support => Price falling clearly below a prior support area
WHY: These three terms are commonly used together to describe price behavior around support.

[FILL2] Support commonly forms where [1] has previously stepped in, near a price a chart has struggled to fall [2].
BLANK1: *buying | selling | random
BLANK2: *below | above | sideways
WHY: Support marks a level where buying pressure has previously slowed or reversed declines.

[ORDER] A simple way to mark support on a chart:
Look at recent price history for repeated lows
Mark price areas where price reacted more than once
Note that this is an estimate, not an exact line
Revisit and adjust as new price history comes in
WHY: Support is an informal, evolving reference area, not a fixed rule.

[PICK] Which best describes what a "support level" tells you, on its own?
CHART: supportBounce
CONCERN: Understanding the concept accurately
A historical price area where downward moves have previously struggled | detail: Descriptive, based on the past
A guarantee price will stop there again | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Past behavior at a level doesn't guarantee future behavior there.
A rule set by an exchange | detail: Incorrect | wrong: This is an informal chart-reading concept, not an official exchange rule.
WHY: Support describes historical tendencies, not fixed, guaranteed rules.

[SPOT] Someone claims a certain price level is "support" that can never be broken.
CHART: supportBounce
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
No support level is unbreakable; price levels are historical tendencies, not permanent walls.
They should have picked an even lower price level instead.
They should have used a shorter timeframe only.
WHY: Support levels get broken regularly, treating any level as absolute is a common misconception.


[FILL] A support level is commonly viewed as more notable with [___] touches.
more
fewer
zero
WHY: More reactions at a level commonly add to how notable it's viewed.

[TF] Support can eventually be broken and stop acting as a floor.
ANSWER: True
WHY: No support level is permanent, breaks happen regularly.

NODE 3
TITLE: Why Levels Form
CELEBRATION: You understand why support and resistance exist!

INTRO
TITLE: Memory in the Market
TEXT: Support and resistance are commonly explained by the idea that traders "remember" prior price reactions and behave similarly near those levels again.

[TF] Support and resistance levels form for no describable reason at all.
ANSWER: False
WHY: They're commonly explained by patterns in trader behavior around prior price reactions.

[FILL] A commonly cited reason levels "hold" is that traders may [___] how price reacted there before.
remember
forget
ignore
WHY: Prior reactions at a level are commonly thought to influence trader behavior there again.

[MC] What is a commonly cited explanation for why a prior high can act as resistance later?
Traders who missed selling near that high previously may be more inclined to sell there again.
The exchange enforces a rule preventing price from passing that point.
The chart's software automatically caps price at that level.
WHY: This is a commonly cited behavioral explanation, not an enforced rule.

[MATCH] Match the concept to a commonly cited explanation.
Support holding => Buyers stepping in again near a level that held before
Resistance holding => Sellers stepping in again near a level that held before
Level "memory" => Traders responding similarly to prices that mattered before
WHY: These are commonly cited behavioral explanations, not guaranteed mechanisms.

[FILL2] These explanations are [1], not [2] laws of how markets must behave.
BLANK1: *commonly cited theories | proven guarantees | random guesses
BLANK2: *guaranteed laws | commonly cited theories | proven facts
WHY: These are useful, widely discussed explanations, not iron-clad rules.

[ORDER] A simple way to think through why a level might matter:
Note that price reacted at this level before
Consider that traders may recall that reaction
Recognize this as a commonly cited theory, not a certainty
Watch how price actually behaves there this time
WHY: This keeps the explanation grounded as a theory rather than an assumed guarantee.

[PICK] Which best reflects a responsible view of why levels form?
CONCERN: Explaining support/resistance
"This is a commonly cited theory about trader behavior, not a guaranteed law" | detail: Balanced, accurate framing
"This is a guaranteed law of how markets must behave" | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: No such guarantee exists; it's a commonly cited theory.
"There's no reason at all. It's pure coincidence" | detail: Overcorrects | wrong: There are commonly cited, reasonable explanations, even without certainty.
WHY: The balanced view treats this as a reasonable theory, not an absolute law.

[SPOT] Someone insists there's a strict, universal rule guaranteeing why every level holds.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
There's no strict, universal rule. These are commonly cited theories about trader behavior, not guarantees.
They should look for an even older price level instead.
They should stop discussing why levels form at all.
WHY: Treating a commonly cited theory as an absolute rule overstates what's actually known.


[FILL] The behavioral explanation for levels is a [___], not a proven law.
theory
guarantee
rule
WHY: It's commonly discussed as a reasonable theory, not an enforced law.

[TF] Support and resistance levels are enforced by exchange rules.
ANSWER: False

[FILL] A support or resistance level that has been tested multiple times is often described as more [___].
significant
random
invisible
WHY: More touches and reactions are commonly linked to a level being viewed as more notable.

[MC] Why might multiple touches at the same level be noted by chart readers?

WHY: Repeated reactions are commonly cited as one reason a level draws more attention, without guaranteeing anything.

Multiple touches => A more established, more closely watched level
Many touches over time => Often described as a well-tested level
WHY: These descriptions reflect how touch count is commonly discussed, without implying certainty.

[FILL2] More touches can make a level feel more [1], but this still doesn't [2] it will hold again.
BLANK1: *notable | random | invisible
BLANK2: *guarantee | describe | random
WHY: Notability and guaranteed future behavior are two different things.

[ORDER] A simple way to evaluate how "tested" a level is:

Note whether it reacted (bounced/rejected) each time

Form a view on how notable it currently seems

[PICK] Which best describes a level tested by price five separate times?
CHART: supportBounce
CONCERN: Comparing levels by touch count
A more established, closely watched level, though not a guarantee | detail: More touches, more notability, still no certainty

A level that's now meaningless due to overuse | detail: Overcorrects | wrong: More touches are commonly viewed as adding notability, not removing it.
WHY: More touches add notability in how a level is commonly discussed, without guaranteeing what happens next.

[SPOT] Someone assumes a level touched five times is now mathematically guaranteed to hold a sixth time.
CHART: supportBounce
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
More touches can add notability, but they never guarantee the level will hold again.
They should wait for even more touches before considering it at all.
They should stop counting touches entirely.
WHY: Notability from repeated touches is not the same as a guarantee about future behavior.


[FILL] Touch count is one simple way to compare the relative [___] of levels.
notability
color
volume
WHY: Comparing touch counts is a simple, descriptive way to judge relative notability.

[TF] A level with many touches is mathematically certain to hold on the next test.
ANSWER: False

[FILL] When price clearly moves above a prior resistance level, it's often called a [___].
breakout
pullback
doji

[MC] What does the term "breakdown" commonly describe?

[MATCH] Match the term to its description.

WHY: These three terms are commonly used together when describing this kind of event.

WHY: Not every breakout continues, some reverse back, commonly called a "false breakout."

after

without regard to

WHY: Breakdown is simply the downside counterpart to breakout.

NODE 6
TITLE: Fakeouts
CELEBRATION: You can recognize a false breakout!

INTRO
TITLE: When the Breakout Fails
TEXT: A fakeout (false breakout) happens when price briefly moves past a level, then reverses back, the opposite of a confirmed break.
CHART: resistanceRejection

[TF] A false breakout means price moved past a level and then reversed back, instead of continuing.
ANSWER: True
WHY: That reversal back into the prior range is what defines a false breakout.

[FILL] A breakout that quickly reverses back is often called a [___] breakout.
false
confirmed
strong
WHY: "False" describes the failed follow-through.

[MC] Why do chart readers commonly pay attention to false breakouts?
They're a commonly cited reason why waiting for confirmation matters, rather than reacting instantly.
They guarantee a certain outcome every time they occur.
They mean the chart's data is inaccurate.
WHY: False breakouts are simply one commonly described risk of reacting to a level break too early.

[MATCH] Match the outcome to its label.
Price breaks a level and keeps moving that direction => Confirmed breakout
Price breaks a level then quickly reverses back => False breakout ("fakeout")
Price returns to test a broken level from the other side => Retest
WHY: These are standard descriptive labels for different outcomes after a level is broken.

[FILL2] A false breakout is described [1] the fact, once price has already [2] back.
BLANK1: *after | before | during
BLANK2: *reversed | continued | doubled
WHY: You can only classify a breakout as "false" once you've seen what happened next.

[ORDER] A cautious way to think about a fresh breakout:
Note that price has just moved past a level
Recognize the move could continue or reverse
Wait to see whether the move holds over subsequent candles
Only then describe it as confirmed or false
WHY: Patience before labeling a breakout is a commonly cited way to reduce being misled by an early false move.

[PICK] Which best describes a false breakout?
CHART: resistanceRejection
CONCERN: Reading breakout follow-through
Price moved past a level but then reversed back into the range | detail: The definition of a false breakout
Price moved past a level and kept going for months | detail: Doesn't match | wrong: That would be a confirmed breakout.
A candle with a small body and long wicks | detail: Unrelated | wrong: That describes a doji, not a false breakout.
WHY: The reversal back is the defining feature of a false breakout.

[SPOT] Someone assumes every breakout must be false and never trusts one at all.
CHART: resistanceRejection
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Some breakouts do continue, treating every single one as false is just as much an overstatement as treating every one as guaranteed.
They should have only looked at longer wicks.
They should have used a completely different chart type.
WHY: Both extremes, assuming every breakout works, or assuming none do, overstate certainty in either direction.


[FILL] A false breakout can only be labeled [___] price has already reversed back.
after
before
without
WHY: You need to see the reversal happen before calling it a false breakout.

[TF] False breakouts are a reason many chart readers wait for confirmation before reacting.
ANSWER: True
WHY: The risk of a false breakout is commonly cited as a reason to wait for confirmation.

NODE 7
TITLE: Retests
CELEBRATION: You understand retests of broken levels!

INTRO
TITLE: Coming Back for a Second Look
TEXT: After a level breaks, price sometimes returns to that same level from the other side before continuing, commonly called a retest.
CHART: breakout

[TF] A retest means price returns to a broken level from the other side.
ANSWER: True
WHY: That's the definition, revisiting the level after it was already broken.

[FILL] Old resistance that gets broken is sometimes described as becoming new [___].
support
resistance
volume
WHY: A commonly cited idea is that a broken ceiling can act as a new floor, and vice versa.

[MC] What is a retest, in simple terms?
Price returning to touch a previously broken level before (potentially) continuing.
A brand new all-time high.
A chart with no candles at all.
WHY: A retest is specifically about revisiting a level that was already broken.

[MATCH] Match the term to its description.
Breakout => Price first clears a level
Retest => Price returns to touch that same level again
Old resistance becomes support => A commonly cited idea about broken ceilings
WHY: These terms are frequently used together when describing this kind of price sequence.

[FILL2] A retest happens [1] the original breakout, when price revisits the [2] level.
BLANK1: *after | before | during
BLANK2: *same | opposite | random
WHY: The order matters, a retest only makes sense once a level has already broken.

[ORDER] A simple way to describe a breakout-and-retest sequence:
Price breaks clearly past a level
Price pulls back to touch that same level again
Price either holds there and continues, or breaks back through
The sequence is described based on what actually happened
WHY: Like breakouts, retests are described after the fact based on how price actually behaved.

[PICK] Which best matches the idea that "old resistance can become new support"?
CHART: breakout
CONCERN: Understanding retest terminology
A broken ceiling sometimes acts as a floor on a later retest | detail: A commonly cited descriptive idea
A guarantee that every broken level will hold on retest | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Retests can also fail and break back through.
A rule enforced by exchanges | detail: Incorrect | wrong: This is informal chart-reading terminology, not an official rule.
WHY: This is a commonly cited pattern, not a guaranteed rule.

[SPOT] Someone assumes every retest of a broken level will always hold and bounce.
CHART: breakout
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Retests sometimes fail and price breaks back through. It isn't guaranteed to hold.
They should have used a shorter timeframe only.
They should have ignored the retest entirely.
WHY: Like other chart concepts here, a retest describes a common pattern, not a certainty.


[FILL] A retest only makes sense once a level has already [___].
broken
formed
disappeared
WHY: Retesting implies revisiting a level after it was already broken.

[TF] A retest is guaranteed to hold and never break back through.
ANSWER: False
WHY: Retests can also fail, with price breaking back through the level.

NODE 8
TITLE: Range Trading (Intro)
CELEBRATION: You understand the basic idea of a trading range!

INTRO
TITLE: Bouncing Between the Lines
TEXT: A trading range describes a period where price bounces between a fairly consistent support and resistance level.
CHART: sideways

[TF] A trading range describes price bouncing between a fairly consistent support and resistance level.
ANSWER: True
WHY: That's the standard descriptive definition of a trading range.

[FILL] The upper boundary of a trading range is commonly the level acting as [___].
resistance
support
volume
WHY: Resistance forms the upper boundary in a typical range description.

[MC] What commonly happens at the edges of an established trading range?
Price has commonly reacted (bounced or rejected) at those edges before.
Price is guaranteed to break out immediately.
The chart data resets at each edge.
WHY: The edges of a range are, by definition, levels where price has reacted previously.

[MATCH] Match the range concept to its description.
Range floor => The support side of the range
Range ceiling => The resistance side of the range
Range breakout => Price clearly leaving the established range
WHY: These terms describe the basic structure of a trading range.

[FILL2] A trading range is bounded by a [1] level below and a [2] level above.
BLANK1: *support | resistance | breakout
BLANK2: *resistance | support | breakout
WHY: Support and resistance together form the floor and ceiling of a range.

[ORDER] A simple way to identify a trading range:
Look for repeated reactions near a similar low
Look for repeated reactions near a similar high
Confirm price has bounced between these levels more than once
Label that zone as a trading range
WHY: Multiple touches on both sides is what distinguishes a range from a brief pause.

[PICK] Which best describes a trading range, as commonly used?
CHART: sideways
CONCERN: Understanding this specific term
Price bouncing between a fairly consistent support and resistance level | detail: A bounded, sideways pattern
A guarantee price will break out imminently | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Ranges can persist for a long time before resolving.
The exact same thing as a single candlestick pattern | detail: Conflates concepts | wrong: A range spans many candles, not one shape.
WHY: A trading range specifically describes bounded, repeated bouncing between two levels.

[SPOT] Someone assumes an established trading range will hold forever and never resolve into a breakout.
CHART: sideways
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Ranges are commonly followed eventually by a breakout in one direction or another. They don't last forever.
They should have widened the range indefinitely instead.
They should have only looked at a shorter timeframe.
WHY: Treating any market phase as permanent overstates what is really just a temporary description.


[FILL] A trading range is bounded by consistent support [___] resistance levels.
and
or
without
WHY: Both a floor (support) and a ceiling (resistance) together define a range.

[TF] A trading range can persist for a long time before finally breaking out.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Ranges are commonly described as lasting a while before eventually resolving.

NODE 9
TITLE: Combining Levels with Trend
CELEBRATION: You can combine levels and trend context together!

INTRO
TITLE: Reading Levels in Context
TEXT: Support and resistance are commonly read alongside the broader trend, not in isolation from it.
CHART: uptrend

[TF] Support and resistance levels should always be read completely independently of the overall trend.
ANSWER: False
WHY: Levels are commonly read alongside trend context for a fuller picture, not in total isolation.

[FILL] A support level within an established uptrend is sometimes described as aligning with the [___] trend.
broader
opposite
random
WHY: Levels are commonly discussed in relation to the broader trend they sit within.

[MC] Why might a support level be viewed differently during an uptrend versus a downtrend?
The broader trend context can add to how a level is commonly interpreted.
Support only exists during uptrends and never during downtrends.
Trend and support have no relationship to each other at all.
WHY: Combining level and trend context is commonly described as giving a fuller picture than either alone.

[MATCH] Match the combined context to a commonly cited description.
Support within an uptrend => Sometimes read as aligning with the broader upward structure
Resistance within a downtrend => Sometimes read as aligning with the broader downward structure
Level against the trend => Read with extra caution, given the conflicting context
WHY: These are common (not certain) ways trend and levels are discussed together.

[FILL2] Combining [1] and [2] together is commonly described as giving a fuller picture than either alone.
BLANK1: *levels | doji | volume
BLANK2: *trend | wicks | color
WHY: Neither concept alone captures everything; combining them is the commonly cited habit.

[ORDER] A simple way to combine levels and trend context:
Identify the broader trend direction first
Identify nearby support or resistance levels
Note whether the level aligns with or goes against the trend
Factor both pieces into your overall description
WHY: This habit builds a fuller, combined read rather than relying on just one concept.

[PICK] Which best reflects a balanced way to combine trend and levels?
CHART: uptrend
CONCERN: Combining these concepts responsibly
Considering both together for a fuller descriptive picture | detail: Neither alone tells the whole story
Only ever considering levels, ignoring trend entirely | detail: Incomplete | wrong: This ignores useful context the trend can add.
Only ever considering trend, ignoring levels entirely | detail: Incomplete | wrong: This ignores useful context levels can add.
WHY: Combining both is more descriptive than relying on either one alone.

[SPOT] Someone reads a resistance level the exact same way regardless of whether the chart is in an uptrend or downtrend.
CHART: uptrend
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Ignoring the broader trend context misses useful information that's commonly combined with level analysis.
They should ignore resistance levels entirely instead.
They should only ever look at trend, never levels.
WHY: Combining trend and level context together is more descriptive than looking at either alone.


[FILL] Combining levels with trend gives a [___] picture than either alone.
fuller
narrower
random
WHY: Together, levels and trend context are commonly described as more complete than either alone.

[TF] A level that goes against the broader trend is commonly read with extra caution.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Conflicting context (level vs trend) is commonly treated as worth extra caution.

NODE 10
TITLE: Levels Drill, Support or Resistance?
CELEBRATION: You can classify levels across a mixed set of charts!

INTRO
TITLE: Practice Round
TEXT: Practice identifying where support and resistance levels sit relative to price.

[MC] Where is the support level in this chart, in relation to price?
CHART: supportBounce
Below, where price has bounced more than once
Above, where price hasn't yet traded
There is no support shown in this chart
WHY: Support is the price floor where a chart has previously had trouble falling below.

[MC] Where is the resistance level in this chart, in relation to price?
CHART: resistanceRejection
Above, where price has struggled to close beyond
Below, where price has never traded
There is no resistance shown in this chart
WHY: Resistance is the price ceiling where a chart has previously had trouble rising above.

[MC] What does the final candle in this chart illustrate about the nearby level?
CHART: breakout
A breakout clearing above the prior level on strong momentum
Price staying entirely within the prior range
Price falling below the prior level
WHY: The final candles here clear the prior level and hold above it, matching a breakout.

[TF] A support level and a resistance level can be the exact same price, just viewed from different sides.
ANSWER: True
WHY: A broken resistance level commonly becomes a new support level from the other side, and vice versa.

[MATCH] Match the level type to its position relative to price.
Support => Below current price, acting as a floor
Resistance => Above current price, acting as a ceiling
Broken level (flipped) => Can act as the opposite type after being broken
WHY: A quick recap of how these levels relate to price position.

[PICK] What is the main goal of practicing level identification like this?
CONCERN: Understanding the purpose of this drill
Building a consistent habit for spotting these zones on any chart | detail: A practical, repeatable skill
Guaranteeing exactly where price will react next | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Levels are historical references, not guarantees.
Replacing the need to look at the trend entirely | detail: Incomplete | wrong: Levels are read alongside trend, not instead of it.
WHY: This drill builds a consistent, descriptive habit, not predictive certainty.


[FILL] These drills build a consistent, [___] habit for spotting levels.
descriptive
predictive
random
WHY: The goal is descriptive skill, not predictive certainty.

[TF] Support and resistance can each act as the other after a level breaks.
ANSWER: True
WHY: A broken resistance can become support (and vice versa), this flip is commonly discussed.

[MATCH] Match the drill focus to its skill.
Spotting support => Identifying a floor level
Spotting resistance => Identifying a ceiling level
Spotting a breakout => Noticing price clearing a level
WHY: These are the three core skills this drill practices together.

NODE 11
TITLE: Support & Resistance Review
CELEBRATION: You've completed the Support & Resistance unit!

INTRO
TITLE: Bringing It All Together
TEXT: A final review of support, resistance, breakouts, fakeouts, and retests, all descriptive tools for reading key price zones.

[TF] Everything covered in this unit describes historical price behavior, not guaranteed future outcomes.
ANSWER: True
WHY: That's the common thread across every concept in this unit.

[FILL] A price floor where declines have previously struggled is called [___].
support
resistance
volume
WHY: Reviewing the basic definition from earlier in this unit.

[MC] What do support, resistance, and breakouts all have in common as terms?
They all describe patterns in price history around key levels, not certainties about the future.
They all refer to a single specific candlestick shape.
They only apply to one specific asset.
WHY: Each is a descriptive label for behavior around price levels.

[MATCH] Match the concept to its one-line summary.
Support/Resistance => Price areas where reactions have happened before
Breakout/Breakdown => Price clearly moving past a level
Fakeout/Retest => Ways a level break can play out afterward
WHY: A quick recap of the core vocabulary from this unit.

[FILL2] Reading levels well means combining [1] with a healthy dose of [2] about certainty.
BLANK1: *description | randomness | guessing
BLANK2: *skepticism | confidence | ignorance
WHY: Useful chart reading pairs descriptive skill with realistic expectations.

[ORDER] The general path this unit followed:
Learn the basic definitions of support and resistance
Learn why levels are commonly thought to form
Learn about breakouts, fakeouts, and retests
Practice combining levels with trend, while staying skeptical of guarantees
WHY: Each lesson built on the one before it toward a fuller picture.

[PICK] Which best summarizes the overall theme of this unit?
CONCERN: Reviewing the big picture
Support and resistance are descriptive tools, not predictive guarantees | detail: The central theme throughout
Support and resistance always predict the future with certainty | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: This directly contradicts what every lesson covered.
Support and resistance are meaningless and should be ignored | detail: Overcorrects | wrong: They're useful descriptive tools, just not guarantees.
WHY: This balanced view has been the throughline of every lesson in this unit.

[SPOT] Someone finishes this unit and claims they can now predict every future price reaction with certainty.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Nothing in this unit was about certainty. It was about describing key price zones more clearly.
They should have skipped straight to more advanced lessons.
They should have only studied one single chart.
WHY: This unit builds reading literacy, not a predictive guarantee, that distinction matters.


[FILL] This unit treats levels as [___] tools, not guarantees.
descriptive
predictive
guaranteed
WHY: Descriptive, not predictive, framing has been the throughline of this unit.

[TF] Fakeouts and retests are both ways a level break can play out after the fact.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Both describe possible outcomes once a level has been broken.

UNIT 10 — Double Tops & Bottoms
NODE 1
TITLE: What Is a Double Top?
CELEBRATION: You can spot a double top on a chart!

INTRO
TITLE: Two Peaks at the Same Level
TEXT: A double top is commonly described as price rising to a level, pulling back, rising to about the <b>same</b> level again, then turning down, an "M" shape. It's read as a possible reversal after an up-move.
CHART: doubleTop

[MC] The chart above shows two peaks stalling at about the same price. This shape is commonly called a...
Double top.
Double bottom.
Breakout.
WHY: Two peaks at the same level forming an "M" is the double top shape.

[TF] A double top is commonly described as forming after an up-move, at resistance.
CHART: doubleTop
ANSWER: True
WHY: The two peaks stall at the same resistance level after price has risen into it.

[FILL] The two peaks of a double top reach roughly the [___] price level.
same
opposite
random
WHY: The defining feature is two peaks at about the same level.

[MATCH] Match each part of the double top to what it is.
The two peaks => Highs that stall at the same resistance
The dip between them => A pullback before the second attempt
The turn down after => Price failing to break higher a second time
WHY: These three parts together make the "M" shape.

[PICK] What does a double top describe, on its own?
CHART: doubleTop
CONCERN: Reading the pattern descriptively
Price tested a level twice and failed to break above it | detail: A descriptive read, not a guarantee
A guaranteed crash from here | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: A pattern describes; it doesn't guarantee the next move.
That both peaks are green candles | detail: Unrelated | wrong: Candle color isn't what defines the pattern.
WHY: A double top describes a level tested twice and rejected. Context, not certainty.

[SPOT] Someone sees one peak and immediately calls it a "double top."
CHART: resistanceRejection
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
A double top needs two peaks at about the same level, a single peak isn't one yet.
A double top only needs a single peak to count.
The number of peaks doesn't matter for a double top.
WHY: The pattern is defined by two peaks at a similar level, not one.


[FILL] A double top forms after an [___] move, at resistance.
up
down
sideways
WHY: The double top's "M" shape forms after price has risen into resistance.

[TF] A double top's two peaks must be exactly identical to the cent.
ANSWER: False
WHY: "About the same" level is the practical standard, not perfect equality.

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the first peak of the double top.
CHART: doubleTop
TARGET: 2
WHY: The 2nd candle is the first peak, testing resistance for the first time.

[TAPCANDLES] Tap both peaks that test the same resistance level.
CHART: doubleTop
TARGETS: 2, 4
WHY: Candles 2 and 4 are the two peaks stalling at about the same level, the "M" shape.

NODE 2
TITLE: What Is a Double Bottom?
CELEBRATION: You can spot a double bottom on a chart!

INTRO
TITLE: Two Troughs at the Same Level
TEXT: A double bottom is the mirror image: price falls to a level, bounces, falls to about the <b>same</b> level again, then turns up, a "W" shape, read as a possible reversal after a down-move.
CHART: doubleBottom

[MC] The chart above shows two troughs holding at about the same price. This shape is commonly called a...
Double bottom.
Double top.
Breakdown.
WHY: Two troughs at the same level forming a "W" is the double bottom shape.

[TF] A double bottom is commonly described as forming after a down-move, at support.
CHART: doubleBottom
ANSWER: True
WHY: The two troughs hold at the same support level after price has fallen into it.

[FILL] The two troughs of a double bottom reach roughly the [___] price level.
same
opposite
random
WHY: The defining feature is two troughs at about the same level.

[PICK] What does a double bottom describe, on its own?
CHART: doubleBottom
CONCERN: Reading the pattern descriptively
Price tested a level twice and held above it both times | detail: A descriptive read
A guaranteed rally from here | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: A pattern describes; it doesn't guarantee the outcome.
That the chart data is broken | detail: Incorrect | wrong: A double bottom is a normal, valid shape.
WHY: A double bottom describes support tested twice and held. Context, not certainty.

[TF] The chart above shows an "M" shape.
CHART: doubleBottom
ANSWER: False
WHY: A double bottom is a "W" shape; the "M" is a double top.

[SPOT] Someone calls the chart a double top because "it has two turning points."
CHART: doubleBottom
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Two troughs at support form a double bottom (a "W"), not a double top.
Any two turning points always make a double top.
Double tops and double bottoms are the same thing.
WHY: Direction matters, troughs at support is a double bottom, peaks at resistance is a double top.


[FILL] A double bottom forms after a [___] move, at support.
down
up
sideways
WHY: The "W" shape forms after price has fallen into support.

[TF] A double bottom's two troughs must be exactly identical to the cent.
ANSWER: False
WHY: "About the same" level is the practical standard, not perfect equality.

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the first trough of the double bottom.
CHART: doubleBottom
TARGET: 2
WHY: The 2nd candle is the first trough, testing support for the first time.

[TAPCANDLES] Tap both troughs that test the same support level.
CHART: doubleBottom
TARGETS: 2, 4
WHY: Candles 2 and 4 are the two troughs holding at about the same level, the "W" shape.

NODE 3
TITLE: The Neckline
CELEBRATION: You understand the neckline!

INTRO
TITLE: The Level That Confirms
TEXT: The <b>neckline</b> is the level at the dip of a double top (or the peak of a double bottom). The pattern is commonly said to "complete" only if price breaks that neckline.
CHART: doubleTop

[FILL] In a double top, the neckline sits at the [___] between the two peaks.
dip
top
midpoint of the wicks
WHY: The neckline runs along the low of the pullback between the two peaks.

[MC] Why is the neckline important when reading a double top?
The pattern is commonly said to complete only when price breaks the neckline.
The neckline guarantees the direction of the next candle.
The neckline is unrelated to the pattern.
WHY: Breaking the neckline is the commonly cited confirmation step, descriptively.

[TF] A double top is commonly said to be "confirmed" the instant the second peak forms, before any neckline break.
ANSWER: False
WHY: Confirmation is commonly tied to the neckline breaking, not just the second peak appearing.

[MATCH] Match the pattern to where its neckline sits.
Double top => Along the dip between the two peaks
Double bottom => Along the bounce peak between the two troughs
Neckline break => The commonly cited confirmation of either pattern
WHY: The neckline is the shared "confirmation level" for both patterns.

[PICK] Which best describes the neckline's role?
CHART: doubleBottom
CONCERN: Understanding confirmation
A level whose break is commonly read as confirming the pattern | detail: A descriptive confirmation step
A guarantee the pattern will play out | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: A break is a clue, not a guarantee.
The exact future price target | detail: Incorrect | wrong: The neckline is a level, not a price prediction.
WHY: The neckline is the confirmation level, described, not a certainty.

[SPOT] Someone treats a double top as complete even though price never broke the neckline.
CHART: doubleTop
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Without a neckline break, the pattern isn't commonly considered confirmed yet.
The neckline never matters for a double top.
A double top is always complete at the first peak.
WHY: The neckline break is the commonly cited confirmation, so calling it complete early skips that step.


[FILL] In a double bottom, the neckline sits at the [___] between the two troughs.
bounce peak
lowest point
midpoint of the wicks
WHY: The neckline in a double bottom runs along the bounce high between the two troughs.

[TF] The neckline is the same idea in both a double top and a double bottom, just mirrored.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Both patterns share the neckline-confirmation idea, just on opposite sides.

[MC] What happens if price never breaks the neckline after the second peak or trough?
The pattern isn't commonly considered confirmed yet.
The pattern is automatically confirmed anyway.
The neckline moves to a random new level.
WHY: Confirmation is tied specifically to the neckline break.

NODE 4
TITLE: Double Top vs. Resistance Rejection
CELEBRATION: You can tell these two apart!

INTRO
TITLE: One Touch or Two?
TEXT: A single rejection at resistance touches the level <b>once</b>. A double top touches about the <b>same</b> level <b>twice</b>, with a dip in between. Reading how many times price tested the level is the skill here.

[MC] The chart above shows price stalling at resistance and turning down after a single touch. This is best described as...
A resistance rejection.
A double top.
A double bottom.
CHART: resistanceRejection
WHY: One touch of resistance is a rejection; a double top needs two touches at the same level.

[MC] The chart above shows two peaks at the same level with a dip between them. This is best described as...
A double top.
A single resistance rejection.
A breakout.
CHART: doubleTop
WHY: Two touches of the same level, forming an "M", is a double top.

[TF] The main difference between a single resistance rejection and a double top is how many times the level was tested.
ANSWER: True
WHY: One test = rejection; two tests at the same level = double top.

[FILL2] A double top tests the level [1], while a single rejection tests it [2].
BLANK1: *twice | once | never
BLANK2: *once | twice | often
WHY: Counting the tests of the level is what separates the two reads.

[PICK] Reading the chart above, which description fits?
CHART: doubleTop
CONCERN: Counting tests of the level
Two tests of the same resistance, a double top | detail: Two touches, "M" shape
A single test of resistance | detail: Undercounts | wrong: There are two peaks here, not one.
A support bounce | detail: Wrong side | wrong: This is at resistance (top), not support.
WHY: Two peaks at the same resistance is a double top, not a single rejection.

[SPOT] Someone labels a two-peak "M" at resistance a "single rejection."
CHART: doubleTop
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Two peaks at the same level is a double top, not a single rejection. They undercounted the tests.
The number of touches never matters.
A double top and a single rejection are identical.
WHY: The count of tests at the level is exactly what distinguishes the two.


[FILL] Counting how many times a level was tested is the key to telling these two [___] apart.
patterns
colors
timeframes
WHY: Test count (one vs two) is exactly what separates a rejection from a double top.

[TF] A single resistance rejection can still be a valid, meaningful chart event on its own.
ANSWER: True
WHY: A single rejection is still meaningful. It's just not a double top by itself.


[FILL] A single touch of a level is a rejection; a second touch at the same level upgrades it to a [___].
double top
triple bottom
random pattern
WHY: The second test at the same level is what makes it a double top rather than a single rejection.

NODE 5
TITLE: Double Bottom vs. Support Bounce
CELEBRATION: You can tell these two apart!

INTRO
TITLE: One Bounce or Two?
TEXT: A single support bounce touches support <b>once</b>. A double bottom touches about the same support <b>twice</b>, with a bounce in between, the "W".

[MC] The chart above shows price dipping to support and bouncing after a single touch. This is best described as...
A support bounce.
A double bottom.
A double top.
CHART: supportBounce
WHY: One touch of support is a bounce; a double bottom needs two touches at the same level.

[MC] The chart above shows two troughs at the same level with a bounce between them. This is best described as...
A double bottom.
A single support bounce.
A breakdown.
CHART: doubleBottom
WHY: Two touches of the same support, forming a "W", is a double bottom.

[TF] A double bottom and a single support bounce differ mainly in how many times support was tested.
ANSWER: True
WHY: One test = bounce; two tests at the same level = double bottom.

[FILL2] A double bottom tests support [1], while a single bounce tests it [2].
BLANK1: *twice | once | never
BLANK2: *once | twice | always
WHY: Counting the tests of support is what separates the two reads.

[PICK] Reading the chart above, which description fits?
CHART: doubleBottom
CONCERN: Counting tests of the level
Two tests of the same support, a double bottom | detail: Two touches, "W" shape
A single support bounce | detail: Undercounts | wrong: There are two troughs here, not one.
A resistance rejection | detail: Wrong side | wrong: This is at support (bottom), not resistance.
WHY: Two troughs at the same support is a double bottom, not a single bounce.

[SPOT] Someone calls a two-trough "W" a "single bounce."
CHART: doubleBottom
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Two troughs at the same level is a double bottom, not a single bounce. They undercounted the tests.
Counting touches never matters at support.
A double bottom and a single bounce are identical.
WHY: The count of tests at support is what distinguishes the two.


[FILL] Counting how many times support was tested is the key to telling these two [___] apart.
patterns
colors
timeframes
WHY: Test count (one vs two) is exactly what separates a bounce from a double bottom.

[TF] A single support bounce can still be a valid, meaningful chart event on its own.
ANSWER: True
WHY: A single bounce is still meaningful. It's just not a double bottom by itself.


[FILL] A single touch of support is a bounce; a second touch at the same level upgrades it to a [___].
double bottom
triple top
random pattern
WHY: The second test at the same level is what makes it a double bottom rather than a single bounce.

NODE 6
TITLE: Reading the Two Peaks
CELEBRATION: You can judge whether the peaks match!

INTRO
TITLE: Are They Really Equal?
TEXT: A cleaner double top has two peaks at <b>about</b> the same height. If the second peak is much higher or lower, the "double" read is weaker. Judging roughly-equal highs is the skill here.
CHART: doubleTop

[TF] A double top is commonly described as having two peaks at roughly the same level.
CHART: doubleTop
ANSWER: True
WHY: Roughly-equal peaks are what make it a "double" top.

[MC] If the second peak is much higher than the first, the double top read is...
Weaker, the peaks aren't really at the same level.
Stronger, higher is always better.
Unchanged, peak height never matters.
WHY: The pattern relies on the two peaks being at about the same level.

[FILL] For a clean double top, the two peaks should be at [___] the same height.
about
exactly zero
wildly different
WHY: "About the same" height is the practical standard, not perfectly identical.

[PICK] The two peaks on the chart above are at about the same level. What does that support?
CHART: doubleTop
CONCERN: Judging the peaks
A cleaner double top read, since the level was tested twice at the same height | detail: Roughly-equal peaks
A guaranteed reversal | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Equal peaks strengthen the read, not guarantee an outcome.
That the pattern is invalid | detail: Backwards | wrong: Equal peaks support the pattern, not invalidate it.
WHY: Roughly-equal peaks strengthen the double top read, descriptively.

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the second peak of the double top.
CHART: doubleTop
TARGET: 4
WHY: The fourth candle is the second peak, stalling at the same resistance as the first.

[SPOT] Someone calls it a clean double top even though the second peak is far higher than the first.
CHART: breakout
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
If the second high is far above the first, the peaks aren't at the same level. It's a weaker "double" read (and may just be a breakout).
Peak heights never matter for a double top.
A higher second peak always makes a stronger double top.
WHY: The "double" read depends on the two peaks being at about the same level.


[FILL] Judging whether two peaks are "roughly equal" is a matter of [___], not exact math.
degree
guaranteed certainty
random guessing
WHY: "About the same" is a practical, descriptive judgment, not a strict formula.

[TF] A second peak noticeably lower than the first also weakens the double top read.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Either a much higher OR much lower second peak weakens the "roughly equal" read.


[FILL] The "double" in double top refers to the number of times the [___] was tested.
level
candle color
timeframe
WHY: "Double" specifically counts the tests of the resistance level.

[TF] A double top with two clearly unequal peaks is generally considered a cleaner pattern than one with equal peaks.
ANSWER: False
WHY: Roughly equal peaks make for a cleaner double top read, not unequal ones.

NODE 7
TITLE: Reading the Two Troughs
CELEBRATION: You can judge whether the troughs match!

INTRO
TITLE: Are They Really Equal?
TEXT: The same idea applies to a double bottom: two troughs at <b>about</b> the same low. A second trough much lower weakens the "double" read.
CHART: doubleBottom

[MC] If the second trough is much lower than the first, the double bottom read is...
Weaker, the troughs aren't at the same level.
Stronger, lower is always better.
Unchanged, trough depth never matters.
WHY: The pattern relies on the two troughs being at about the same level.

[FILL] For a clean double bottom, the two troughs should be at [___] the same low.
about
wildly different
exactly zero
WHY: "About the same" low is the practical standard.

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the second trough of the double bottom.
CHART: doubleBottom
TARGET: 4
WHY: The fourth candle is the second trough, holding at the same support as the first.

[PICK] The two troughs on the chart above hold at about the same level. What does that support?
CHART: doubleBottom
CONCERN: Judging the troughs
A cleaner double bottom read, since support held twice at the same level | detail: Roughly-equal troughs
A guaranteed rally | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Equal troughs strengthen the read, not guarantee it.
That the pattern is invalid | detail: Backwards | wrong: Equal troughs support the pattern.
WHY: Roughly-equal troughs strengthen the double bottom read, descriptively.

[SPOT] Someone calls it a clean double bottom even though the second trough is far below the first.
CHART: breakdown
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
If the second low is far below the first, the troughs aren't at the same level, a weaker "double" read (and may just be a breakdown).
Trough depth never matters.
A lower second trough always strengthens the pattern.
WHY: The "double" read depends on the two troughs being at about the same level.


[FILL] Judging whether two troughs are "roughly equal" is a matter of [___], not exact math.
degree
guaranteed certainty
random guessing
WHY: "About the same" is a practical, descriptive judgment, not a strict formula.

[TF] A second trough noticeably higher than the first also weakens the double bottom read.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Either a much higher OR much lower second trough weakens the "roughly equal" read.


[FILL] The "double" in double bottom refers to the number of times the [___] was tested.
level
candle color
timeframe
WHY: "Double" specifically counts the tests of the support level.

[TF] A double bottom with two clearly unequal troughs is generally considered a cleaner pattern than one with equal troughs.
ANSWER: False
WHY: Roughly equal troughs make for a cleaner double bottom read, not unequal ones.

NODE 8
TITLE: When the Pattern Fails
CELEBRATION: You understand failed double patterns!

INTRO
TITLE: Not Every M or W Follows Through
TEXT: Sometimes price forms two peaks but then breaks <b>up</b> through resistance instead of down, the double top "fails". Patterns are clues, never guarantees.
CHART: breakout

[TF] Every double top is guaranteed to lead to a reversal down.
ANSWER: False
WHY: Patterns are descriptive clues; a double top can fail if price breaks up instead.

[MC] A double top "fails" when...
Price breaks up through the resistance instead of turning down.
The two peaks are at the same level.
A neckline forms between the peaks.
WHY: Breaking up through the level is the opposite of the expected reversal, so the pattern fails.

[FILL] Because patterns are clues and not guarantees, a double top can still [___].
fail
guarantee
predict exactly
WHY: Any pattern can fail. That's why it's read as a clue, not a certainty.

[PICK] Why treat a double top as a clue rather than a certainty?
CHART: doubleTop
CONCERN: Managing expectations
Because price can still break the level instead of reversing | detail: Patterns fail sometimes
Because patterns always work | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: This is exactly the overconfidence to avoid.
Because the pattern controls the market | detail: Incorrect | wrong: A pattern describes; it doesn't control price.
WHY: Any pattern can fail, so it's read as a probability-shaped clue, not a guarantee.

[SPOT] Someone assumes a double top means a reversal is certain and ignores that price broke upward.
CHART: breakout
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Price broke up through the level, the double top failed. Treating it as certain ignored that outcome.
A double top can never fail.
Breaking upward confirms the double top.
WHY: When price breaks up instead of down, the double top has failed. Patterns aren't guarantees.


[FILL] A failed double top is one commonly cited reason to treat patterns as [___], not certainties.
clues
guarantees
laws
WHY: Failure is exactly why patterns are read as probability-shaped clues.

[TF] A double bottom can also fail, in the opposite way (breaking down through support).
ANSWER: True
WHY: Just as a double top can fail by breaking up, a double bottom can fail by breaking down.


[FILL] Whether a double top "fails" or "confirms" is only known [___] the fact.
after
before
without regard to
WHY: You need to see how price actually resolves to know if the pattern held or failed.

[TF] A failed pattern is still useful to recognize, even though it didn't play out as expected.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Recognizing failures builds a more honest, complete understanding of how patterns behave.

A single rejection, not a double pattern.
A double top.
A double bottom.

[FILL] This drill practices spotting [___] before naming a pattern.
touch count
candle color
company name
WHY: Counting touches at a level is the first step to correctly naming these patterns.

pattern

NODE 10
TITLE: What the Pattern Suggests
CELEBRATION: You can read what double patterns suggest!

INTRO
TITLE: From Shape to Meaning
TEXT: Read what each completed pattern <b>suggests</b>, always descriptively, never as a buy/sell instruction.

[PICK] A double bottom on the chart above has held support twice. Descriptively, that suggests...
Sellers failed to push below the level twice, buyers defended it | detail: A read of the two tests
A guaranteed rally | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Descriptive, not a promise.
Nothing can be said about the level | detail: Undersells it | wrong: Two holds at a level is meaningful context.
CHART: doubleBottom
WHY: Two successful defenses of support is descriptive context, not a certainty.

[PICK] A double top on the chart above has failed at resistance twice. Descriptively, that suggests...
Buyers failed to push above the level twice, sellers defended it | detail: A read of the two tests
A guaranteed crash | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Descriptive, not a promise.
That resistance no longer exists | detail: Backwards | wrong: The level held twice, so it's still relevant.
CHART: doubleTop
WHY: Two failed pushes above resistance is descriptive context, not a certainty.

[TF] Reading "what a pattern suggests" is about description, not a signal to act.
ANSWER: True
WHY: This whole course stays descriptive, patterns inform reading, not trade commands.

[MATCH] Match each pattern to its descriptive read.
Double top => Resistance defended twice; buyers failed above it
Double bottom => Support defended twice; sellers failed below it
Failed pattern => Price broke the level instead of reversing
WHY: Each is a descriptive read of how the level behaved.

[SPOT] Someone turns "a double bottom suggests buyers defended support" into "definitely buy now."
CHART: doubleBottom
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
That converts a descriptive read into a certainty and a trade command. The pattern is context, not a guaranteed signal.
The descriptive read and a guaranteed buy are the same thing.
Patterns are always trade commands.
WHY: A descriptive read isn't a guaranteed signal; treating it as one overstates certainty.


[FILL] "What a pattern suggests" always stays [___], never a certainty.
descriptive
guaranteed
random
WHY: This unit consistently frames pattern reads as descriptive, not certain.

[TF] A failed double top suggests something different than a confirmed one.
ANSWER: True
WHY: A confirmed pattern suggests the reversal played out; a failed one suggests it didn't.


[FILL] Reading "what a pattern suggests" adds [___] on top of simply naming the shape.
interpretation
guesswork
volume
WHY: Interpreting the pattern's implication goes beyond just identifying its shape.
