UNIT 11 — Head & Shoulders
NODE 1
TITLE: What Is a Head & Shoulders?
CELEBRATION: You can spot a head & shoulders on a chart!

INTRO
TITLE: Three Peaks, Middle Highest
TEXT: A head & shoulders is commonly described as three peaks: a <b>left shoulder</b>, a higher <b>head</b>, then a <b>right shoulder</b> at about the shoulder's height. It's read as a possible reversal after an up-move.
CHART: headShoulders

[MC] The chart above shows three peaks with the middle one highest. This shape is commonly called a...
Head & shoulders.
Double top.
Ascending triangle.
WHY: Three peaks with a higher middle "head" is the head & shoulders shape.

[FILL] In a head & shoulders, the middle and highest peak is called the [___].
head
neckline
shoulder
WHY: The tallest, central peak is the "head".

[TF] A head & shoulders is commonly described as forming after an up-move.
CHART: headShoulders
ANSWER: True
WHY: Like other reversal patterns, it appears after price has risen into it.

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the head (the highest peak).
CHART: headShoulders
TARGET: 4
WHY: The 4th candle is the head, the tallest of the three peaks.

[PICK] What does a head & shoulders describe, on its own?
CHART: headShoulders
CONCERN: Reading the pattern descriptively
Three peaks with a higher middle, a possible reversal shape | detail: Descriptive, not a guarantee
A guaranteed crash | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: A pattern describes; it doesn't guarantee the move.
That the middle peak is the lowest | detail: Backwards | wrong: The head (middle) is the highest, not the lowest.
WHY: A head & shoulders describes a three-peak shape. Context, not certainty.

[SPOT] Someone calls a two-peak chart a "head & shoulders."
CHART: doubleTop
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
A head & shoulders needs three peaks (with a higher middle), two peaks is a double top.
Two peaks is enough for a head & shoulders.
The number of peaks doesn't matter.
WHY: Head & shoulders is a three-peak pattern; two peaks is a double top.


[FILL] A head & shoulders is a [___]-peak pattern.
three
two
four
WHY: Left shoulder, head, right shoulder, three peaks total.

[TF] The head is the middle peak of the three.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Left shoulder, then the (highest) head, then the right shoulder, the head sits in the middle.

[MC] Which best matches the order of peaks in a head & shoulders, left to right?
Left shoulder, head, right shoulder.
Head, left shoulder, right shoulder.
Right shoulder, head, left shoulder.
WHY: The name itself describes the left-to-right order: shoulder, head, shoulder.

NODE 2
TITLE: The Three Peaks
CELEBRATION: You can name each peak of a head & shoulders!

INTRO
TITLE: Left Shoulder, Head, Right Shoulder
TEXT: The three peaks each have a name: <b>left shoulder</b>, <b>head</b> (highest), and <b>right shoulder</b> (about the same height as the left). Reading them in order is the skill.
CHART: headShoulders

[MATCH] Match each peak to its description.
Left shoulder => The first peak, lower than the head
Head => The middle, highest peak
Right shoulder => The third peak, about the left shoulder's height

[FILL] The right shoulder is commonly at about the [___] height as the left shoulder.
same
double
zero
WHY: Roughly-equal shoulders are part of a clean head & shoulders.

[MC] Which peak is the tallest in a head & shoulders?
The head (the middle peak).
The left shoulder.
The right shoulder.

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the head.
CHART: headShoulders
TARGET: 4

The right shoulder is always the tallest.
The head should be the shortest peak.
WHY: The head is the highest peak; shoulders sit lower and are roughly equal.


[FILL] The left and right shoulders are commonly at [___] height as each other.
about the same
wildly different
exactly double the
WHY: Roughly equal shoulder heights are part of a clean head & shoulders.

[TF] The head is commonly the shortest of the three peaks.
ANSWER: False

NODE 3
TITLE: The Neckline
CELEBRATION: You understand the neckline!

INTRO
TITLE: The Line Under the Pattern
TEXT: The <b>neckline</b> connects the two troughs between the peaks. A head & shoulders is commonly said to "complete" only when price breaks the neckline.

LEVEL: support

TARGET_START: 3
TARGET_END: 5

The neckline guarantees the next candle's direction.
The neckline is unrelated to the pattern.
WHY: Breaking the neckline is the commonly cited confirmation step, descriptively.

[FILL] The neckline connects the two [___] between the peaks.
troughs
peaks
wicks
WHY: The neckline runs along the lows (troughs), not the peaks.

[TF] A head & shoulders is commonly called confirmed the moment the right shoulder forms, before any neckline break.
ANSWER: False

[PICK] Which best describes the neckline's role?
CHART: headShoulders
CONCERN: Understanding confirmation
A level whose break is commonly read as confirming the pattern | detail: A descriptive confirmation step
A guarantee the pattern plays out | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: A break is a clue, not a guarantee.
The exact future price target | detail: Incorrect | wrong: The neckline is a level, not a prediction.
WHY: The neckline is the confirmation level, described, not a certainty.


[FILL] The neckline connects the two [___] between the peaks.
troughs
peaks
wicks
WHY: The neckline runs along the lows between the shoulders and head, not the peaks.

[TF] Price breaking the neckline is commonly read as confirming the pattern.
ANSWER: True
WHY: The neckline break is the commonly cited confirmation step.

[MC] What is the neckline, in simple terms?
The level connecting the troughs between the three peaks.
The tallest peak itself.
A random line unrelated to the pattern.
WHY: The neckline specifically connects the two troughs framing the head.

INTRO
TITLE: The Upside-Down Version
TEXT: An <b>inverse</b> head & shoulders is the mirror image: three troughs, with the middle (head) lowest, after a down-move. It's read as a possible reversal upward.
CHART: inverseHeadShoulders

[MC] The chart above shows three troughs with the middle one lowest. This is commonly called an...
Inverse head & shoulders.
Head & shoulders (regular).
Double bottom.
WHY: Three troughs with a lower middle "head" is the inverse head & shoulders.

[TF] An inverse head & shoulders commonly forms after a down-move.
CHART: inverseHeadShoulders
ANSWER: True
WHY: The inverse version appears after price has fallen into it.

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the head (the lowest trough).
CHART: inverseHeadShoulders
TARGET: 4
WHY: The 4th candle is the head, the lowest of the three troughs.

[MARKLEVEL] Drag the line to the neckline (along the two peaks).
CHART: inverseHeadShoulders
LEVEL: resistance
WHY: In an inverse head & shoulders, the neckline runs along the peaks between the troughs.

[TRENDLINE] Drag to connect the two peaks that form the neckline.
CHART: inverseHeadShoulders
TARGET_START: 3
TARGET_END: 5
WHY: Candles 3 and 5 are the peaks on either side of the head, connected by the neckline.

[PICK] How does an inverse head & shoulders differ from the regular one?
CHART: inverseHeadShoulders
CONCERN: Telling the two apart
It's three troughs with the lowest in the middle, after a down-move | detail: The mirror image
It's identical to the regular version | detail: Incorrect | wrong: One is peaks-up, the other troughs-down.
It has only one trough | detail: Undercounts | wrong: It has three troughs, not one.
WHY: The inverse is the upside-down mirror, three troughs with a lower head.

[SPOT] Someone calls an inverse head & shoulders a "regular head & shoulders".
CHART: inverseHeadShoulders
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
This is three troughs with a lower middle (inverse), not three peaks with a higher middle.
The two patterns are the same thing.
Troughs and peaks don't change the name.
WHY: Peaks-up is the regular pattern; troughs-down is the inverse.


[FILL] An inverse head & shoulders has three [___] instead of three peaks.
troughs
wicks
necklines
WHY: The inverse version flips peaks into troughs.

[TF] An inverse head & shoulders is commonly read as a possible reversal upward.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Forming after a down-move, it's commonly read as a possible upward reversal.

[MC] In an inverse head & shoulders, which trough is the lowest?
The head (middle trough).
The left shoulder.
The right shoulder.
WHY: The head is the lowest of the three troughs in the inverse version.

NODE 5
TITLE: Reading Symmetry
CELEBRATION: You can judge a head & shoulders' symmetry!

INTRO
TITLE: Are the Shoulders Balanced?
TEXT: A cleaner head & shoulders has two shoulders at <b>about</b> the same height, with the head clearly above both. Lopsided shoulders weaken the read.
CHART: headShoulders

[MC] For a clean head & shoulders, the two shoulders should be...
At about the same height, both below the head.
Wildly different heights.
Both taller than the head.
WHY: Roughly-equal shoulders below a clear head make the cleanest pattern.

[TAPCANDLES] Tap BOTH shoulders.
CHART: headShoulders
TARGETS: 2, 6
WHY: The 2nd and 6th candles are the two shoulders, read whether they're about equal.

[FILL] The head should be clearly [___] both shoulders.
above
below
equal to
WHY: The head is the highest peak, above both shoulders.

[TF] If the "head" is actually lower than a shoulder, the head & shoulders read is weaker (or invalid).
ANSWER: True
WHY: The head must be the highest peak; if it isn't, it's not a clean head & shoulders.

[SPOT] Someone calls it a clean head & shoulders even though the middle peak is lower than both outer peaks.
CHART: doubleTop
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
If the middle peak isn't the highest, it isn't a head & shoulders. The head must top both shoulders.
The head can be the lowest peak.
Peak heights never matter.
WHY: The defining feature is a head that stands above both shoulders.


[FILL] Judging shoulder symmetry is about [___], not exact precision.
degree
guaranteed certainty
random guessing
WHY: "About the same" height is a practical, descriptive judgment.

[TF] A lopsided head & shoulders (very unequal shoulders) is read as a weaker version of the pattern.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Unequal shoulders weaken the "clean" pattern read, though it can still count loosely.

NODE 6
TITLE: Head & Shoulders vs. Double Top
CELEBRATION: You can tell these two apart!

INTRO
TITLE: Count the Peaks
TEXT: A double top has <b>two</b> peaks at the same level. A head & shoulders has <b>three</b> peaks, with a higher middle. Counting the peaks tells them apart.

[MC] The chart above has two peaks at the same level. This is best described as...
A double top.
A head & shoulders.
An inverse head & shoulders.
CHART: doubleTop
WHY: Two equal peaks is a double top; three peaks (higher middle) would be head & shoulders.

[MC] The chart above has three peaks with a higher middle. This is best described as...
A head & shoulders.
A double top.
A single rejection.
CHART: headShoulders
WHY: Three peaks with a taller middle is a head & shoulders.

[TF] The main difference between a double top and a head & shoulders is the number of peaks.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Two peaks = double top; three peaks (higher middle) = head & shoulders.

[FILL2] A double top has [1] peaks; a head & shoulders has [2].
BLANK1: *two | three | four
BLANK2: *three | two | one
WHY: Counting the peaks separates the two patterns.

[PICK] Reading the chart above, which fits?
CHART: headShoulders
CONCERN: Counting the peaks
Three peaks with a higher middle, a head & shoulders | detail: Three-peak pattern
Two peaks, a double top | detail: Undercounts | wrong: There are three peaks here, not two.
No pattern at all | detail: Misses it | wrong: Three peaks with a higher middle is a recognizable pattern.
WHY: Three peaks with a higher middle is a head & shoulders, not a double top.

[SPOT] Someone labels a clear three-peak (higher middle) chart a "double top."
CHART: headShoulders
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Three peaks with a higher middle is a head & shoulders. They undercounted the peaks.
Peak count never matters.
A double top and head & shoulders are identical.
WHY: The peak count (and the higher middle) is exactly what distinguishes them.


[FILL] Counting the [___] is the fastest way to tell a double top from a head & shoulders.
peaks
candle colors
timeframes
WHY: Two peaks = double top; three peaks with a higher middle = head & shoulders.

[TF] A head & shoulders and a double top can never be confused with each other.
ANSWER: False
WHY: Without carefully counting peaks, the two patterns can be mixed up. That's exactly the skill this lesson builds.

NODE 7
TITLE: Confirmation
CELEBRATION: You understand head & shoulders confirmation!

INTRO
TITLE: The Neckline Break
TEXT: A head & shoulders is commonly said to "confirm" only when price breaks the neckline after the right shoulder. Until then, it's a forming pattern, not a completed one.
CHART: headShoulders

[MC] When is a head & shoulders commonly considered confirmed?
When price breaks the neckline after the right shoulder.
The instant the left shoulder forms.
Never. It can't be confirmed.
WHY: The neckline break after the right shoulder is the commonly cited confirmation.

[TF] Before the neckline breaks, a head & shoulders is commonly described as still forming, not confirmed.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Confirmation is tied to the neckline break, so before that it's a forming pattern.

[ORDER] The commonly described sequence of a head & shoulders:
Left shoulder forms
Head forms (higher)
Right shoulder forms (about the shoulder height)
Price breaks the neckline (confirmation)
WHY: This order, shoulders, head, shoulder, then neckline break, is how the pattern is commonly read.

[PICK] Why wait for the neckline break before calling it confirmed?
CHART: headShoulders
CONCERN: Managing confirmation
Because until the neckline breaks, the pattern can still fail | detail: Confirmation reduces (not removes) uncertainty
Because the pattern is always confirmed at the head | detail: Too early | wrong: The head forming isn't the confirmation step.
Because confirmation guarantees the outcome | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Even a confirmed pattern can fail.
WHY: The neckline break is the commonly cited confirmation; before it, the pattern can still fail.


[FILL] Before the neckline breaks, a head & shoulders is still [___].
forming
confirmed
guaranteed
WHY: It remains a forming (not yet confirmed) pattern until the neckline breaks.

[TF] Confirmation happens the moment the head forms, before the right shoulder.
ANSWER: False
WHY: Confirmation is tied to the neckline breaking after the right shoulder, not to the head alone.

[MC] What must happen for a head & shoulders to be considered confirmed?
Price must break the neckline after the right shoulder forms.
The left shoulder must be taller than the head.
Nothing. It's confirmed immediately once three peaks appear.
WHY: The neckline break after the right shoulder is the commonly cited confirmation step.

NODE 8
TITLE: When It Fails
CELEBRATION: You understand failed head & shoulders!

INTRO
TITLE: Not Every Pattern Follows Through
TEXT: Sometimes a head & shoulders forms but price never breaks the neckline, or breaks back the other way. Patterns are clues, never guarantees.
CHART: headShoulders

[TF] Every head & shoulders is guaranteed to lead to a reversal.
ANSWER: False
WHY: Patterns are descriptive clues; a head & shoulders can fail if the neckline doesn't break as expected.

[MC] A head & shoulders "fails" when...
Price doesn't break the neckline as expected (or breaks back the other way).
The head is the highest peak.
A neckline connects the troughs.
WHY: Not following through at the neckline is what makes the pattern fail.

[FILL] Because patterns are clues, a head & shoulders can still [___].
fail
guarantee
predict exactly
WHY: Any pattern can fail. That's why it's read as a clue.

[PICK] Why treat a head & shoulders as a clue rather than a certainty?
CHART: headShoulders
CONCERN: Managing expectations
Because price can still fail to break the neckline | detail: Patterns fail sometimes
Because patterns always work | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: This is the overconfidence to avoid.
Because the pattern controls price | detail: Incorrect | wrong: A pattern describes; it doesn't control the market.
WHY: Any pattern can fail, so it's read as a probability-shaped clue, not a guarantee.


[FILL] A head & shoulders can fail if price never breaks the [___].
neckline
head
left shoulder
WHY: Failing to break the neckline (or breaking back the other way) is how the pattern fails.

[TF] A failed head & shoulders is still useful to recognize in hindsight.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Recognizing failures builds a more complete, honest understanding of the pattern.

[MC] Why is a head & shoulders read as a clue rather than a certainty?
Because it can fail to confirm, just like any chart pattern.
Because it never fails once three peaks form.
Because the neckline guarantees the outcome.
WHY: Like other patterns in this course, it's a probability-shaped clue, not a guarantee.

NODE 9
TITLE: Identification Drill
CELEBRATION: You can identify head & shoulders parts fast!

INTRO
TITLE: Read the Charts
TEXT: Identify the pattern and its parts across a few charts.

[MC] Which pattern is shown on the chart above?
CHART: headShoulders
Head & shoulders.
Inverse head & shoulders.
Double bottom.
WHY: Three peaks with a higher middle is a head & shoulders.

[MC] Which pattern is shown on the chart above?
CHART: inverseHeadShoulders
Inverse head & shoulders.
Head & shoulders (regular).
Double top.
WHY: Three troughs with a lower middle is an inverse head & shoulders.

CHART: headShoulders

WHY: The 4th candle is the head, the tallest peak.

[TAPCANDLES] Tap BOTH shoulders.
CHART: headShoulders
TARGETS: 2, 6
WHY: The 2nd and 6th candles are the left and right shoulders.

[MARKLEVEL] Drag the line to the neckline.
CHART: headShoulders
LEVEL: support
WHY: The neckline runs along the two troughs.


[FILL] This drill practices spotting the pattern's [___] quickly.
parts
color
volume
WHY: Naming the head, shoulders, and neckline quickly is the point of this drill.

[TF] Fast identification drills build quicker pattern recognition over time.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Repetition across mixed charts speeds up recognition.

NODE 10
TITLE: What It Suggests
CELEBRATION: You can read what a head & shoulders suggests!

INTRO
TITLE: From Shape to Meaning
TEXT: Read what a completed head & shoulders <b>suggests</b>, descriptively, never as a buy/sell command.

[PICK] A head & shoulders has completed on the chart above (neckline broke). Descriptively, that suggests...
Buyers made a higher high (head) but couldn't sustain it, and the level gave way | detail: A read of the structure
A guaranteed crash | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Descriptive, not a promise.
That resistance no longer exists | detail: Backwards | wrong: The structure is exactly what's being read.
CHART: headShoulders
WHY: The failed higher high plus a neckline break is descriptive context, not a certainty.

[PICK] An inverse head & shoulders has completed on the chart above. Descriptively, that suggests...
Sellers made a lower low (head) but couldn't sustain it, and the level gave way upward | detail: A read of the structure
A guaranteed rally | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Descriptive, not a promise.
Nothing can be read from it | detail: Undersells it | wrong: The structure is meaningful context.
CHART: inverseHeadShoulders
WHY: The failed lower low plus an upward neckline break is descriptive context.

[TF] Reading "what a pattern suggests" is description, not a signal to act.
ANSWER: True
WHY: This course stays descriptive, patterns inform reading, not trade commands.

[SPOT] Someone turns "a head & shoulders suggests a failed higher high" into "definitely short it now."
CHART: headShoulders
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
That converts a descriptive read into a certainty and a trade command. The pattern is context, not a guaranteed signal.
A descriptive read and a trade command are the same.
Patterns are always trade commands.
WHY: A descriptive read isn't a guaranteed signal; treating it as one overstates certainty.


[FILL] "What it suggests" stays [___], never a certainty.
descriptive
guaranteed
random
WHY: This course consistently frames pattern reads as descriptive, not certain.

[TF] A head & shoulders suggests something different depending on whether the neckline has broken yet.
ANSWER: True
WHY: A forming pattern (neckline unbroken) suggests something different than a confirmed one.

[MC] What should "what it suggests" never be read as?
A guaranteed trade signal.
A descriptive clue.
Useful context.
WHY: This course treats pattern suggestions as descriptive context, never a guaranteed signal.

NODE 11
TITLE: Mixed Recognition

INTRO

TEXT: A full mix, identify the pattern and its parts.

Inverse head & shoulders.
Head & shoulders (regular).
Double top.
WHY: Three troughs with a lower middle is an inverse head & shoulders.

CHART: headShoulders
ANSWER: True
WHY: That's the head & shoulders shape.

[MATCH] Match each pattern to its one-line description.
Head & shoulders => Three peaks, higher middle
Inverse head & shoulders => Three troughs, lower middle

WHY: Recognizing shape plus the confirmation level is the intermediate skill.

[FILL] Mixed recognition combines naming the shape with reading its [___].
level context
candle color

[TF] Practicing mixed recognition helps reinforce everything covered earlier in the unit.
ANSWER: True

NODE 12
TITLE: Head & Shoulders Review
CELEBRATION: You've mastered head & shoulders!

INTRO
TITLE: Bringing It Together
TEXT: A final review of head & shoulders, the inverse, the neckline, and confirmation.

[TF] A head & shoulders is three peaks (higher middle); the inverse is three troughs (lower middle).
ANSWER: True
WHY: That's the core of both patterns.

[FILL] The level connecting the two troughs, whose break confirms the pattern, is the [___].
neckline
head
shoulder
WHY: Reviewing the confirmation level.

[MC] What do a head & shoulders and its inverse have in common?
Both are three-peak (or three-trough) reversal shapes confirmed by a neckline break.
Both guarantee a reversal.
Both have only one peak.
WHY: Three turning points plus a neckline break is the shared idea; neither guarantees an outcome.

[MATCH] Match each term to its meaning.
Head => The tallest (or lowest) middle peak/trough
Shoulders => The outer two peaks/troughs, roughly equal
Neckline => The level whose break confirms the pattern
WHY: A quick recap of the unit's vocabulary.

[FILL2] Head & shoulders patterns are read as [1] clues confirmed by a neckline break, not [2] outcomes.
BLANK1: *descriptive | guaranteed | random
BLANK2: *guaranteed | descriptive | possible
WHY: The unit treats the pattern as a descriptive, confirmation-based clue.

[PICK] Which best summarizes this unit?
CHART: headShoulders
CONCERN: Reviewing the big picture
A three-peak reversal shape confirmed by a neckline break, useful context, not a guaranteed signal | detail: The central theme
A pattern that guarantees the next move | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: This contradicts the unit.
A replacement for reading levels | detail: Backwards | wrong: It's read together with the neckline.
WHY: This descriptive, confirmation-aware reading is the throughline of the unit.

[SPOT] Someone finishes this unit and starts calling every three-bar wiggle a "head & shoulders."
CHART: sideways
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
A head & shoulders needs three clear peaks with a higher middle and a neckline, not any small wiggle.
Any three candles form a head & shoulders.
The neckline and peak heights don't matter.
WHY: The pattern has specific requirements (three peaks, higher middle, neckline); random wiggles don't qualify.


[FILL] This unit treats head & shoulders as a [___] pattern, confirmed by the neckline.
descriptive
guaranteed
random
WHY: Descriptive, neckline-confirmed reading has been the throughline of this unit.

[MC] What's the single biggest skill this unit builds?
Reading a three-peak (or three-trough) structure and its confirmation level together.
Memorizing one exact price forever.
Ignoring the neckline entirely.
WHY: Combining structure and confirmation is the core skill from this unit.

UNIT 12 — Triangles
NODE 1
TITLE: What Is an Ascending Triangle?
CELEBRATION: You can spot an ascending triangle!

INTRO
TITLE: Flat Top, Rising Lows
TEXT: An ascending triangle is commonly described as a <b>flat resistance</b> on top with <b>rising lows</b> underneath, price squeezing up toward the level. It's read as a possible continuation upward.
CHART: ascendingTriangle

[MC] The chart above has a flat top and rising lows. This is commonly called an...
Ascending triangle.
Descending triangle.
Double top.
WHY: A flat resistance with rising lows is the ascending triangle shape.

[MARKLEVEL] Drag the line to the flat resistance the highs keep testing.
CHART: ascendingTriangle
LEVEL: resistance
WHY: The flat top of an ascending triangle is the resistance being tested repeatedly.

[TAPCANDLES] Tap every candle that closes at one of the rising lows.
CHART: ascendingTriangle
TARGETS: 1, 2, 4
WHY: Candles 1, 2, and 4 mark the sequence of rising lows squeezing up under the flat top.

[TF] An ascending triangle has rising lows underneath a flat resistance.
CHART: ascendingTriangle
ANSWER: True
WHY: Rising lows squeezing up under a flat top define the ascending triangle.

[FILL] The lows of an ascending triangle are commonly [___] while the top stays flat.
rising
falling
flat
WHY: Rising lows plus a flat top is the defining structure.

[PICK] What does an ascending triangle describe, on its own?
CHART: ascendingTriangle
CONCERN: Reading the pattern descriptively
Buyers pushing lows higher while sellers hold a flat level, a squeeze | detail: Descriptive, not a guarantee
A guaranteed breakout up | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Triangles can break either way; nothing is guaranteed.
That the top is falling | detail: Backwards | wrong: The top is flat, not falling.
WHY: An ascending triangle describes a squeeze. Context, not certainty.


[FILL] An ascending triangle has a flat [___] on top.
resistance
support
neckline
WHY: The flat top of an ascending triangle is the resistance being tested.

[TF] An ascending triangle's lows are commonly described as rising.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Rising lows squeezing up under a flat top is the ascending triangle shape.

[MC] What is squeezing together in an ascending triangle?
Price, between a flat resistance and rising lows.
Two flat, unmoving lines.
Nothing. It's a random shape.
WHY: Price is squeezed between the flat top and the rising lows.

NODE 2
TITLE: What Is a Descending Triangle?
CELEBRATION: You can spot a descending triangle!

INTRO
TITLE: Flat Bottom, Falling Highs
TEXT: A descending triangle is the mirror: a <b>flat support</b> on the bottom with <b>falling highs</b> above, price squeezing down toward the level. It's read as a possible continuation downward.
CHART: descendingTriangle

[MC] The chart above has a flat bottom and falling highs. This is commonly called a...
Descending triangle.
Ascending triangle.
Double bottom.
WHY: A flat support with falling highs is the descending triangle shape.

[MARKLEVEL] Drag the line to the flat support the lows keep testing.
CHART: descendingTriangle
LEVEL: support
WHY: The flat bottom of a descending triangle is the support being tested repeatedly.

[TAPCANDLES] Tap every candle that closes at one of the falling highs.
CHART: descendingTriangle
TARGETS: 1, 2, 4
WHY: Candles 1, 2, and 4 mark the sequence of falling highs squeezing down over the flat bottom.

[FILL] The highs of a descending triangle are commonly [___] while the bottom stays flat.
falling
rising
flat
WHY: Falling highs plus a flat bottom is the defining structure.

[TF] The chart above shows a flat top with rising lows.
CHART: descendingTriangle
ANSWER: False
WHY: A descending triangle has a flat BOTTOM with falling highs, the opposite.

[SPOT] Someone calls a flat-bottom, falling-highs chart an "ascending triangle."
CHART: descendingTriangle
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Flat bottom with falling highs is a descending triangle; ascending has a flat top with rising lows.
The two triangles are the same.
The flat side doesn't matter.
WHY: Which side is flat (top vs bottom) separates ascending from descending.


[FILL] A descending triangle has a flat [___] on the bottom.
support
resistance
neckline
WHY: The flat bottom of a descending triangle is the support being tested.

[TF] A descending triangle's highs are commonly described as falling.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Falling highs squeezing down onto a flat bottom is the descending triangle shape.

[MC] What is squeezing together in a descending triangle?
Price, between falling highs and a flat support.
Two flat, unmoving lines.
Nothing. It's a random shape.
WHY: Price is squeezed between the falling highs and the flat bottom.

NODE 3
TITLE: What Is a Symmetrical Triangle?
CELEBRATION: You can spot a symmetrical triangle!

INTRO
TITLE: Both Sides Converging
TEXT: A symmetrical triangle has <b>lower highs and higher lows</b> converging toward a point. Neither side is flat. It's read as a squeeze that can break either way.
CHART: symmetricalTriangle

[MC] The chart above has lower highs AND higher lows converging. This is commonly called a...
Symmetrical triangle.
Ascending triangle.
Channel.
WHY: Both sides converging (no flat side) is a symmetrical triangle.

[FILL] A symmetrical triangle has lower highs and [___] lows converging together.
higher
lower
flat
WHY: Lower highs and higher lows narrowing is the symmetrical shape.

[TF] A symmetrical triangle has one perfectly flat side.
CHART: symmetricalTriangle
ANSWER: False
WHY: Neither side is flat. Both converge. A flat side would make it ascending or descending.

[PICK] How does a symmetrical triangle differ from an ascending one?
CHART: symmetricalTriangle
CONCERN: Telling triangles apart
Both sides converge; an ascending triangle has a flat top | detail: No flat side vs a flat side
They are identical | detail: Incorrect | wrong: One has a flat side, the other doesn't.
A symmetrical triangle only has rising lows | detail: Incomplete | wrong: It has lower highs too, both converging.
WHY: A symmetrical triangle converges on both sides; ascending/descending have one flat side.


[FILL] A symmetrical triangle has [___] flat side(s).
zero
one
two
WHY: Both sides converge in a symmetrical triangle. Neither is flat.

[TF] A symmetrical triangle can break either upward or downward.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Without a flat side biasing one direction, a symmetrical triangle can resolve either way.

[MC] What distinguishes a symmetrical triangle from ascending/descending ones?
Neither side is flat. Both converge toward a point.
It always breaks upward.
It has no lower boundary at all.
WHY: Lacking a flat side is what makes it "symmetrical" rather than ascending or descending.

NODE 4
TITLE: The Flat Side Is the Level
CELEBRATION: You can find the level in a triangle!

INTRO
TITLE: Read the Flat Side
TEXT: In ascending and descending triangles, the <b>flat side</b> is a support or resistance being tested again and again. Marking that level is a key reading skill.

[TAPCANDLES] Tap the candles that touch the flat resistance on top.
CHART: ascendingTriangle
TARGETS: 1, 3, 5
WHY: The 1st, 3rd, and 5th candles push up into the flat resistance.

[MARKLEVEL] Drag the line to the resistance in this ascending triangle.
CHART: ascendingTriangle
LEVEL: resistance
WHY: The repeated highs mark the flat resistance level.

[MARKLEVEL] Drag the line to the support in this descending triangle.
CHART: descendingTriangle
LEVEL: support
WHY: The repeated lows mark the flat support level.

[MC] In an ascending triangle, the flat side is a...
resistance level being tested by the highs.
support level being tested by the lows.
random line.
WHY: The flat top of an ascending triangle is a resistance being retested.

[TF] In a descending triangle, the flat side is a support level.
ANSWER: True
WHY: The flat bottom of a descending triangle is a support being retested.


[FILL] The flat side of an ascending or descending triangle is being tested [___] over time.
repeatedly
once only
never
WHY: Repeated tests of the flat side is what makes it a meaningful level.

[TF] Marking the flat side of a triangle is the same skill as marking any other support/resistance level.
ANSWER: True
WHY: The flat side of a triangle is simply a support or resistance level, read the same way.

NODE 5
TITLE: Which Way Does It Break?
CELEBRATION: You can read triangles without over-predicting!

INTRO
TITLE: Bias, Not Certainty
TEXT: Ascending triangles are commonly <b>associated</b> with an upward break and descending with a downward break. But triangles can break either way. It's a bias, never a guarantee.
CHART: ascendingTriangle

[TF] An ascending triangle guarantees an upward breakout.
ANSWER: False
WHY: It's commonly associated with an upward break, but nothing is guaranteed. It can break either way.

[MC] What's the honest way to read an ascending triangle's direction?
It's commonly associated with an upward break, but can still break down.
It always breaks up, guaranteed.
It always breaks down.
WHY: A commonly-cited bias isn't a guarantee; triangles can break either way.

[FILL] A triangle's expected break direction is a [___], not a certainty.
bias
guarantee
rule
WHY: Descriptive bias, not a promise, is the honest read.

[SPOT] Someone bets everything on an ascending triangle "definitely" breaking up.
CHART: ascendingTriangle
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
An ascending triangle is associated with an upward break but doesn't guarantee it, treating it as certain overstates the read.
Ascending triangles always break up.
Break direction is fully predictable.
WHY: A bias isn't a guarantee; triangles can and do break either way.


[FILL] A triangle's expected break direction is described as a [___], not a guarantee.
bias
certainty
rule
WHY: Even a commonly associated direction is just a bias, not a promise.

[TF] A descending triangle can still break upward, despite the commonly cited downward bias.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Bias describes a tendency, not a guarantee, any triangle can break either way.

pattern

NODE 7
TITLE: Triangles Review
CELEBRATION: You've mastered triangles!

INTRO
TITLE: Bringing It Together
TEXT: A final review of ascending, descending, and symmetrical triangles.

Descending => Flat bottom, falling highs

WHY: The flat side (or lack of one) names each triangle.

UNIT 13 — Flags & Pennants
NODE 1
TITLE: What Is a Bull Flag?
CELEBRATION: You can spot a bull flag!

INTRO
TITLE: Pole, Then a Pause
TEXT: A bull flag is a strong up move (the <b>pole</b>) followed by a small, gentle pullback (the <b>flag</b>) that drifts down or sideways, then price often continues up. It's a continuation pattern.
CHART: bullFlag

[MC] The chart above shows a sharp rise, a small drift down, then more rise. This is commonly called a...
Bull flag.
Bear flag.
Double top.
WHY: A strong up pole plus a small downward flag is a bull flag.

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the start of the pole (the first strong up candle).
CHART: bullFlag
TARGET: 1
WHY: The 1st candle begins the strong up move, the pole.

[CONSOLIDATION] Drag across the candles that make up the flag (the small pause after the pole).
CHART: bullFlag
TARGET_LO: 3
TARGET_HI: 4
WHY: Candles 3 and 4 are the small downward drift after the pole, the flag itself.

[FILL] In a bull flag, the strong initial up-move is called the [___].
pole
flag
neckline
WHY: The pole is the sharp initial move; the flag is the small pause after it.

[TF] A bull flag is commonly described as a continuation pattern.
CHART: bullFlag
ANSWER: True
WHY: The small pause after the pole often continues in the pole's direction.

[PICK] What does a bull flag describe, on its own?
CHART: bullFlag
CONCERN: Reading the pattern descriptively
A strong move, a small pause, and often continuation the same way | detail: Descriptive continuation read
A guaranteed continuation | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: It's a common tendency, not a guarantee.
A reversal down | detail: Backwards | wrong: A bull flag is a continuation-up pattern, not a reversal.
WHY: A bull flag describes a pause in an up-move that often continues. Context, not certainty.


[FILL] The strong initial move in a bull flag is called the [___].
pole
flag
neckline
WHY: "Pole" is the sharp initial up-move before the small pause.

[TF] A bull flag's pause commonly drifts down or sideways, against the pole.
ANSWER: True
WHY: The flag portion drifts gently against the pole's direction before often continuing.

[MC] What commonly happens after a clean bull flag's pause?
Price often continues in the pole's original (upward) direction.
Price always reverses down permanently.
Nothing, the chart stops updating.
WHY: Continuation of the pole's direction is the commonly cited tendency after a flag.

INTRO
TITLE: The Mirror Image
TEXT: A bear flag is a strong down move (the pole) followed by a small drift up (the flag), then price often continues down. It's the bull flag's mirror.
CHART: bearFlag

[MC] The chart above shows a sharp drop, a small drift up, then more drop. This is commonly called a...
Bear flag.
Bull flag.
Double bottom.
WHY: A strong down pole plus a small upward flag is a bear flag.

CHART: bearFlag

WHY: The 1st candle begins the strong down move, the pole.

[CONSOLIDATION] Drag across the candles that make up the flag (the small pause after the pole).
CHART: bearFlag
TARGET_LO: 3
TARGET_HI: 4
WHY: Candles 3 and 4 are the small upward drift after the pole, the flag itself.

[TF] A bear flag drifts slightly UP against a strong down-move.
CHART: bearFlag
ANSWER: True
WHY: The flag drifts gently against the pole's direction, up, in a bear flag.

[FILL] A bear flag is commonly read as a [___] pattern.
continuation
reversal
random
WHY: Like the bull flag, it's a continuation of the pole's direction.

CHART: bearFlag

pole
flag
neckline

ANSWER: True
WHY: The flag portion drifts gently against the pole's direction (up, in a bear flag) before often continuing.

[MC] What commonly happens after a clean bear flag's pause?

[MC] A pennant's pause looks like a small...
symmetrical triangle (converging).
double top.
head & shoulders.

Pennant => A small converging triangle
Both => A brief pause after a strong pole

[TF] A pennant is a reversal pattern, unlike a flag.

symmetrical triangle
double top
neckline

UNIT 14 — Wedges & Channels
NODE 1
TITLE: What Is a Rising Wedge?
CELEBRATION: You can spot a rising wedge!

INTRO
TITLE: Rising, But Narrowing
TEXT: A rising wedge has <b>higher highs and higher lows</b>, but the lines <b>converge</b> (the lows rise faster). Despite rising, it's commonly read as a possible reversal <b>down</b>.
CHART: risingWedge

[MC] The chart above rises but the two lines converge. This is commonly called a...
Rising wedge.
Falling wedge.
Ascending channel.

CHART: risingWedge
ANSWER: True
WHY: Even though it rises, the converging structure is commonly read as weakening, a possible down reversal.

[FILL] A rising wedge has higher highs and higher lows, but the lines [___].
converge
stay parallel
diverge
WHY: Converging (narrowing) lines are what make it a wedge, not a channel.

[PICK] What makes a rising wedge different from a plain uptrend?
CHART: risingWedge
CONCERN: Reading the structure
Its lines converge (narrow) rather than staying parallel | detail: Convergence is the key
It has no highs or lows | detail: Incorrect | wrong: It has both; they just converge.
It always goes straight up forever | detail: Overstates | wrong: The converging shape is the point.
WHY: Convergence distinguishes a wedge from a parallel trend.


[FILL] A rising wedge's lines [___] rather than staying parallel.
converge
diverge
stay flat
WHY: Converging (narrowing) lines are what define a wedge, unlike a parallel channel.

WHY: The converging structure is commonly read as weakening, even though price is rising.

lower highs
higher lows
flat tops

[PICK] What does a trendline describe?

A guarantee price will follow it forever | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: A trendline is a guide, not a promise.
The exact future price | detail: Incorrect | wrong: It marks structure, not a prediction.

horizontal

wicks
WHY: The line connects the higher lows beneath price.

[TF] If price makes a lower low that breaks below the uptrend line, that's commonly read as the line breaking.
ANSWER: True
WHY: A lower low breaking the line is commonly read as the uptrend structure weakening.


[FILL] An uptrend line stays intact as long as pullbacks hold [___] the line.
above
below
exactly on
WHY: Holding above the line keeps the uptrend structure intact.

[TF] A pullback that breaks below the uptrend line is commonly read as a possible weakening of structure.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Breaking the line is commonly noted as an early sign the uptrend may be shifting.

[MC] What happens if a pullback breaks clearly below the uptrend line?
It's commonly read as a possible early sign of weakening structure.
It automatically confirms a new, permanent downtrend.
Nothing, trendlines have no relationship to structure.
WHY: A break is a commonly cited early signal, not an automatic confirmation of a new trend.

NODE 3
TITLE: The Downtrend Line
CELEBRATION: You can read a downtrend line!

INTRO
TITLE: Along the Lower Highs
TEXT: A downtrend line runs above the <b>lower highs</b>. Each bounce that fails below the line keeps the downtrend's structure intact.
CHART: lowerHighsDowntrend

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the lower high (the bounce that failed below the prior high).
CHART: lowerHighsDowntrend
TARGET: 3
WHY: The 3rd candle is the bounce that made a lower high, keeping the downtrend intact.

[TRENDLINE] Drag to connect the two lower highs that define this downtrend line.
CHART: lowerHighsDowntrend
TARGET_START: 1
TARGET_END: 3
WHY: The downtrend line runs from the first high through the bounce's lower high, candles 1 and 3.

[MC] A downtrend line stays intact as long as bounces make...
lower highs (staying below the line).
higher highs (breaking the line).
no highs at all.
WHY: Lower highs staying below the line keep the downtrend structure.

[FILL] A downtrend line is drawn above the falling [___].
highs
lows
wicks
WHY: The line connects the lower highs above price.

[SPOT] Someone draws a downtrend line along the lows instead of the highs.
CHART: lowerHighsDowntrend
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
A downtrend line is drawn along the lower highs (above price), not the lows.
Downtrend lines go along the lows.
It doesn't matter which points you connect.
WHY: Downtrend lines connect the lower highs; uptrend lines connect the higher lows.


[FILL] A downtrend line stays intact as long as bounces stay [___] the line.
below
above
exactly on
WHY: Staying below the line keeps the downtrend structure intact.

[TF] A bounce that breaks clearly above the downtrend line is commonly read as a possible weakening of structure.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Breaking the line is commonly noted as an early sign the downtrend may be shifting.

[MC] What happens if a bounce breaks clearly above the downtrend line?
It's commonly read as a possible early sign of weakening structure.
It automatically confirms a new, permanent uptrend.
Nothing, trendlines have no relationship to structure.
WHY: A break is a commonly cited early signal, not an automatic confirmation of a new trend.

NODE 4
TITLE: Touches Make It Valid
CELEBRATION: You know what makes a trendline valid!

INTRO
TITLE: More Touches, More Meaningful
TEXT: A trendline is commonly considered more meaningful when price has <b>touched it multiple times</b>. A line through only one point isn't really established yet.

[MC] A trendline is commonly considered more meaningful when it has...
multiple touches.
exactly one touch.
no touches.
WHY: Multiple touches make a trendline more established and meaningful.

[TF] A line drawn through a single point is already a well-established trendline.
ANSWER: False
WHY: One point isn't enough, multiple touches are commonly needed to establish a line.

[FILL] More [___] generally make a trendline more meaningful.
touches
colors
candles
WHY: Repeated touches confirm the line matters.

[PICK] Why prefer a trendline with several touches?
CONCERN: Judging validity
Repeated touches show price is respecting that line | detail: More evidence
A single touch is always enough | detail: Undercounts | wrong: One point doesn't establish a line.
Touches don't matter at all | detail: Incorrect | wrong: Touches are the evidence a line is meaningful.
WHY: Multiple touches are the evidence that a trendline is being respected.


[FILL] A trendline with only one touch is [___] established than one with several.
less
more
equally
WHY: More touches make a trendline more established and meaningful.

[TF] A trendline needs at least two points to be drawn, but is considered more meaningful with more touches beyond that.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Two points can draw a line, but additional touches add to how meaningful it's considered.

[MC] Why do more touches make a trendline more meaningful?
They show price has repeatedly respected that line.
They guarantee the line will never break.
They have no effect on how the line is read.
WHY: Repeated respect for the line is commonly cited evidence of its significance, without guaranteeing anything.

NODE 5
TITLE: Breaking a Trendline
CELEBRATION: You can read a trendline break!

INTRO
TITLE: When the Line Gives Way
TEXT: A trendline "break" is commonly read as a possible shift in structure. But like any signal, it's descriptive, not a guarantee.
CHART: pullbackUptrend

[MC] Breaking an uptrend line is commonly read as...
a possible shift in the uptrend's structure.
a guarantee the trend reverses.
completely meaningless.
WHY: A break is a commonly-cited possible shift, descriptive, not a guarantee.

[TF] A trendline break guarantees a full reversal.
ANSWER: False
WHY: A break is a possible shift, not a guaranteed reversal.

[FILL] A trendline break is read as a [___] shift, not a certainty.
possible
guaranteed
random
WHY: It's a descriptive, possible shift in structure.

[SPOT] Someone treats a single trendline break as a guaranteed reversal and bets everything on it.
CHART: pullbackUptrend
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
A trendline break is a possible shift, not a guaranteed reversal. Treating it as certain overstates it.
A break always means a full reversal.
Breaks are fully predictable.
WHY: A break is descriptive context, not a guarantee.


[FILL] A trendline break is read as a [___] shift, not a guaranteed one.
possible
guaranteed
random
WHY: Like other signals in this course, a break is descriptive, not a certainty.

[TF] Every trendline break leads to a full trend reversal.
ANSWER: False
WHY: A break can also fail, with the prior trend resuming. It's not a guaranteed reversal.

[MC] How should a trendline break be treated?
As a possible, descriptive signal worth noting.
As an absolute guarantee of reversal.
As something to always ignore entirely.
WHY: A balanced read treats a break as a notable but non-guaranteed signal.

NODE 6
TITLE: Trendlines Review
CELEBRATION: You've mastered trendlines!

INTRO
TITLE: Bringing It Together
TEXT: A final review of uptrend lines, downtrend lines, touches, and breaks.

[MATCH] Match each to its description.
Uptrend line => Drawn along the higher lows
Downtrend line => Drawn along the lower highs
Valid trendline => One with multiple touches
WHY: A quick recap of the unit's ideas.

[FILL2] An uptrend line connects the higher [1]; a downtrend line connects the lower [2].
BLANK1: *lows | highs | wicks
BLANK2: *highs | lows | wicks
WHY: Higher lows for up, lower highs for down.

[MC] What makes a trendline more meaningful?
More touches, showing price respects the line.
A brighter color.
Being perfectly vertical.
WHY: Multiple touches are the evidence a line matters.

[PICK] Which best summarizes trendlines?
CHART: pullbackUptrend
CONCERN: Reviewing the big picture
Diagonal guides along a trend's turns, more meaningful with more touches | detail: The central theme
Lines that guarantee where price goes | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Contradicts the unit.
The same as horizontal support/resistance | detail: Wrong | wrong: Trendlines are diagonal, not flat.
WHY: Descriptive diagonal guides, validity from touches, is the throughline.


[FILL] This review ties together uptrend lines, downtrend lines, touches, and [___].
breaks
random guesses
company names
WHY: All four core ideas from this unit are reviewed together here.

[TF] Trendlines are diagonal guides that gain meaning from repeated touches, not fixed rules.
ANSWER: True
WHY: This has been the throughline: diagonal, descriptive, and strengthened by repetition, never a fixed rule.

[MC] What is the single most useful habit from this unit?
Drawing trendlines along real turning points and reading their touches and breaks.
Drawing random diagonal lines anywhere on the chart.
Ignoring trendlines entirely.
WHY: Grounding trendlines in actual turning points, then reading touches/breaks, is the core skill.

UNIT 16 — Ranges & Consolidation
NODE 1
TITLE: What Is a Range?
CELEBRATION: You can spot a range!

INTRO
TITLE: Between Two Levels
TEXT: A range (or consolidation) is price moving sideways between a <b>support</b> below and a <b>resistance</b> above, bouncing between the two without a clear trend.
CHART: range

[MC] The chart above bounces between a floor and a ceiling. This is commonly called a...
range (consolidation).
strong uptrend.
head & shoulders.
WHY: Sideways movement between support and resistance is a range.

[MARKLEVEL] Drag the line to the resistance (the ceiling) of this range.
CHART: range
LEVEL: resistance
WHY: The top of the range is the resistance price keeps failing to break.

[MARKLEVEL] Drag the line to the support (the floor) of this range.
CHART: range
LEVEL: support
WHY: The bottom of the range is the support price keeps bouncing from.

[CONSOLIDATION] Drag across every candle that's part of this range's consolidation.
CHART: range
TARGET_LO: 1
TARGET_HI: 6
WHY: All six candles here stay bounded between the support and resistance, the whole chart is the consolidation.

[FILL] A range is bounded by a support below and a [___] above.
resistance
trendline
gap
WHY: Support below, resistance above, bound a range.

[TF] A range has a clear, strong trend in one direction.
CHART: range
ANSWER: False
WHY: A range is sideways, the opposite of a strong one-directional trend.


[FILL] A range is bounded by support below and [___] above.
resistance
a trendline
a gap
WHY: Support and resistance together bound a range from below and above.

[TF] A range describes price making clear directional progress.
ANSWER: False
WHY: A range is sideways, bounded movement, the opposite of clear directional progress.

[MC] What does "consolidation" describe, in this context?
Price moving sideways within a range.
Price making a strong new high.
Price gapping overnight.
WHY: Consolidation is another common term for this sideways, range-bound behavior.

NODE 2
TITLE: Range-Bound vs. Trending
CELEBRATION: You can tell ranges from trends!

INTRO
TITLE: Sideways or Directional?
TEXT: The core question: is price <b>range-bound</b> (sideways between levels) or <b>trending</b> (making progress in one direction)? Reading which regime you're in is a key skill.

[MC] The chart above bounces sideways between two levels. It is...
range-bound.
trending up.
trending down.
CHART: range
WHY: Sideways between levels is range-bound, not trending.

[MC] The chart above makes higher highs and higher lows. It is...
trending up.
range-bound.
trending down.
CHART: uptrend
WHY: Progress in one direction (higher highs/lows) is trending, not range-bound.

[TF] A range-bound market and a trending market are read the same way.
ANSWER: False
WHY: They're different regimes, sideways vs directional, and read differently.

[FILL2] A [1] market moves sideways between levels; a [2] market makes progress one way.
BLANK1: *range-bound | trending | vertical
BLANK2: *trending | range-bound | flat
WHY: Range-bound = sideways; trending = directional.

[SPOT] Someone treats a clearly sideways range as a strong uptrend.
CHART: range
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Bouncing sideways between levels is range-bound, not a strong uptrend. The regime was misread.
Any chart is always trending.
Ranges and trends are identical.
WHY: Reading the regime (range vs trend) correctly is the skill.


[FILL] Reading whether a market is range-bound or [___] is a key first step.
trending
colored
volatile-only
WHY: Classifying the regime (range-bound vs trending) frames how everything else is read.

[TF] The same chart can shift from range-bound to trending over time.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Market regimes change; a range can eventually resolve into a trend.

[MC] What distinguishes range-bound from trending price action?
Range-bound is sideways between levels; trending makes directional progress.
There is no difference between them.
Range-bound always lasts longer than trending.
WHY: Sideways-bounded vs. directional-progress is the key distinction.

NODE 3
TITLE: Reading Range Boundaries
CELEBRATION: You can read a range's boundaries!

INTRO
TITLE: The Floor and Ceiling
TEXT: In a range, price commonly bounces down from resistance and up from support. Reading where those boundaries are is how you read the range.
CHART: range

[MARKLEVEL] Drag the line to the resistance of this range.
CHART: range
LEVEL: resistance
WHY: The ceiling the highs keep testing is the range's resistance.

[MARKLEVEL] Drag the line to the support of this range.
CHART: range
LEVEL: support
WHY: The floor the lows keep testing is the range's support.

[MC] Within a range, price commonly bounces DOWN from the...
resistance (ceiling).
support (floor).
middle only.
WHY: Resistance caps the range; price bounces down from it.

[TF] Within a range, price commonly bounces UP from support.
CHART: range
ANSWER: True
WHY: Support is the floor price bounces up from within the range.


[FILL] In a range, price commonly bounces [___] from resistance.
down
up
sideways only
WHY: Resistance caps the range from above, so price commonly bounces down from it.

[TF] In a range, price commonly bounces up from support.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Support is the range's floor, so price commonly bounces up from it.

[MC] Reading a range's boundaries means identifying...
where support and resistance sit.
the exact company name.
the account's total balance.
WHY: The range's floor (support) and ceiling (resistance) are what define its boundaries.

NODE 4
TITLE: Breaking Out of a Range
CELEBRATION: You understand range breakouts!

INTRO
TITLE: Leaving the Box
TEXT: Eventually price often leaves the range by breaking above resistance or below support, a <b>breakout</b>. The direction isn't guaranteed in advance.
CHART: breakout

[MC] The chart above pushes above the range's resistance and keeps going. This is a...
breakout.
range that's still intact.
reversal into the range.
WHY: Clearing resistance and continuing is a breakout from the range.

[TF] You can always know in advance which way a range will break.
ANSWER: False
WHY: Break direction isn't guaranteed in advance. It's read as it happens.

[FILL] Leaving a range by clearing a boundary is called a [___].
breakout
bounce
gap
WHY: Clearing support or resistance out of the range is a breakout.

[PICK] What does a range breakout describe?
CHART: breakout
CONCERN: Reading the move
Price leaving the range by clearing a boundary | detail: Descriptive move
A guaranteed big trend afterward | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Breakouts can also fail; nothing is guaranteed.
That the range never existed | detail: Backwards | wrong: The breakout is defined relative to the range.
WHY: A breakout describes price leaving the range. Context, not a guaranteed trend.


[FILL] Leaving a range by clearing a boundary is called a [___].
breakout
bounce
gap
WHY: "Breakout" describes price clearing a range's boundary and continuing beyond it.

[TF] The direction a range breaks out is known in advance with certainty.
ANSWER: False
WHY: Break direction isn't guaranteed in advance. It's read as it happens.

[MC] What happens when price clears a range's resistance and keeps going?
A breakout.
A false breakout.
A pullback.
WHY: Clearing resistance and continuing beyond it is the definition of a breakout.

NODE 5
TITLE: False Breakouts
CELEBRATION: You can recognize a false breakout!

INTRO
TITLE: A Break That Doesn't Hold
TEXT: Sometimes price pokes past a range boundary, then falls back inside, a <b>false breakout</b> (or fakeout). It's why a break isn't a guarantee.
CHART: resistanceRejection

[MC] The chart above pokes above resistance, then falls back inside the range. This is a...
false breakout (fakeout).
clean breakout.
strong trend.
WHY: A poke past the level that fails back inside is a false breakout.

[TF] A false breakout is one reason a boundary break isn't a guarantee.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Because breaks can be false, a break is read as a clue, not a certainty.

[FILL] A break that fails back inside the range is commonly called a [___] breakout.
false
guaranteed
perfect
WHY: "False breakout" (or fakeout) is the standard term.

[SPOT] Someone treats every poke past resistance as a guaranteed breakout.
CHART: resistanceRejection
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Some pokes are false breakouts that fail back inside, a poke isn't a guaranteed breakout.
Every poke past a level always continues.
False breakouts don't exist.
WHY: False breakouts are exactly why a break isn't a guarantee.


[FILL] A break that fails and falls back inside the range is called a [___] breakout.
false
confirmed
guaranteed
WHY: "False breakout" (or fakeout) describes a failed break that reverses back inside.

[TF] False breakouts are one reason a boundary break isn't treated as a guarantee.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Because breaks can be false, they're read as clues, not certainties.

[MC] What is a "fakeout," in simple terms?
A poke past a level that reverses back inside.
A guaranteed continuation past the level.
A candle with no wicks.
WHY: A fakeout is specifically a failed breakout that falls back inside.

NODE 6
TITLE: Ranges Review
CELEBRATION: You've mastered ranges!

INTRO
TITLE: Bringing It Together
TEXT: A final review of ranges, boundaries, breakouts, and false breakouts.

[MATCH] Match each term to its meaning.
Range => Sideways between support and resistance
Breakout => Price leaving the range past a boundary
False breakout => A break that fails back inside
WHY: A quick recap of the unit's vocabulary.

[MARKLEVEL] Drag the line to the resistance of this range.
CHART: range
LEVEL: resistance
WHY: Reviewing the range's ceiling.

[MC] What best describes a range?
Sideways movement between a support and a resistance.
A strong one-directional trend.
A single candle.
WHY: Sideways between two levels is the definition of a range.

[PICK] Which best summarizes this unit?
CHART: range
CONCERN: Reviewing the big picture
Price bouncing between levels until it breaks out, with breaks that can be false | detail: The central theme
Ranges that guarantee their breakout direction | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Contradicts the unit.
Ranges unrelated to support and resistance | detail: Backwards | wrong: The boundaries ARE support and resistance.
WHY: Bounded sideways movement with non-guaranteed breaks is the throughline.

[SPOT] Someone calls a strong trending chart a "range."
CHART: uptrend
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Higher highs and higher lows is a trend, not a sideways range between fixed levels.
Any chart is a range.
Trends and ranges are the same.
WHY: A range is sideways between levels; a trend makes directional progress.


[FILL] This review ties together ranges, boundaries, breakouts, and [___].
false breakouts
random guesses
company names
WHY: All four core ideas from this unit are reviewed together here.

[TF] A range eventually resolves into a breakout in one direction or another.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Ranges are commonly described as temporary, eventually resolving into a directional move.

[MC] What is the single most useful habit from this unit?
Reading a range's boundaries and treating any break with healthy skepticism.
Assuming every range lasts forever.
Assuming every breakout is guaranteed to continue.
WHY: Reading boundaries plus healthy skepticism about breaks is the core skill here.

UNIT 17 — Breakouts & Retests
NODE 1
TITLE: What Is a Breakout?
CELEBRATION: You can spot a breakout!

INTRO
TITLE: Clearing the Level
TEXT: A breakout is price clearing a resistance (or support) with momentum and continuing past it. It's read as price escaping a level, not a guaranteed trend.
CHART: breakout

[MC] The chart above clears resistance with a strong candle and keeps going. This is a...
breakout.
bounce back down.
range still intact.
WHY: Clearing the level with momentum and continuing is a breakout.

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the breakout candle (the strong one clearing the level).
CHART: breakout
TARGET: 4
WHY: The 4th candle clears resistance with momentum, the breakout candle.

[FILL] A breakout is price [___] a level and continuing past it.
clearing
bouncing off
ignoring
WHY: Clearing the level (not bouncing) is what makes a breakout.

[TF] A breakout guarantees a large trend afterward.
ANSWER: False
WHY: Breakouts can fail; nothing after a breakout is guaranteed.


[FILL] A breakout is price clearing a level with [___].
momentum
no volume
zero movement
WHY: Clearing the level with momentum, and continuing, defines a breakout.

[TF] A breakout guarantees a large trend afterward.
ANSWER: False
WHY: Breakouts can fail; nothing after a breakout is guaranteed.

[MC] What best describes a breakout?
Price clearing a resistance with momentum and continuing past it.
Price bouncing off a level and reversing.
A candle with a tiny body and long wicks.
WHY: Clearing and continuing past the level is the core of a breakout.

NODE 2
TITLE: What Is a Breakdown?
CELEBRATION: You can spot a breakdown!

INTRO
TITLE: Breaking Support
TEXT: A breakdown is the downside version: price clearing a <b>support</b> and continuing lower. Same idea as a breakout, opposite direction.
CHART: breakdown

[MC] The chart above breaks below support and keeps falling. This is a...
breakdown.
breakout up.
support bounce.
WHY: Clearing support downward and continuing is a breakdown.

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the breakdown candle (the one clearing support).
CHART: breakdown
TARGET: 4
WHY: The 4th candle breaks below support, the breakdown candle.

[TF] A breakdown clears a support level to the downside.
CHART: breakdown
ANSWER: True
WHY: A breakdown is price clearing support and continuing lower.

[FILL] A breakdown is a breakout to the [___] side.
down
up
flat
WHY: A breakdown is the downside version of a breakout.


[FILL] A breakdown is the [___] version of a breakout.
downside
identical
random
WHY: Breakdown mirrors breakout, but to the downside through support.

[TF] A breakdown clears a support level and continues lower.
ANSWER: True
WHY: That's the defining description of a breakdown.

[MC] What best describes a breakdown?
Price clearing a support with momentum and continuing lower.
Price bouncing off support and rallying.
A candle with a tiny body and long wicks.
WHY: Clearing support and continuing lower is the core of a breakdown.

NODE 3
TITLE: The Retest
CELEBRATION: You understand the retest!

INTRO
TITLE: Coming Back to the Level
TEXT: After a breakout, price often returns to <b>retest</b> the level it broke, the old resistance now acting as support, before continuing. The highlighted candle is the retest.
CHART: breakoutRetest

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the retest candle (where price returns to the broken level).
CHART: breakoutRetest
TARGET: 4
WHY: The 4th candle pulls back to retest the level that was just broken.

[CONSOLIDATION] Drag across the candles that form the base before the breakout.
CHART: breakoutRetest
TARGET_LO: 1
TARGET_HI: 2
WHY: Candles 1 and 2 are the base building up against resistance, right before the breakout candle.

[MC] A retest is when price, after a breakout...
returns to the broken level before continuing.
never comes back at all.
breaks a different level.
WHY: A retest is the return to the broken level to test it as new support/resistance.

[FILL] After a breakout, returning to the broken level is called a [___].
retest
reversal
gap
WHY: "Retest" is the standard term for returning to the broken level.

[TF] A retest often shows the old resistance acting as new support.
CHART: breakoutRetest
ANSWER: True
WHY: After breaking up, the old resistance commonly acts as new support on the retest.


[FILL] A retest happens [___] the original breakout.
after
before
without regard to
WHY: A retest only makes sense once a level has already broken.

[TF] In a retest, the old resistance can act as new support.
ANSWER: True
WHY: This flip, old resistance becoming new support, is what a retest commonly tests.

[MC] What is a retest, in simple terms?
Price returning to the broken level before (potentially) continuing.
A brand new all-time high.
Price gapping overnight.
WHY: Retesting means revisiting the level that was already broken.

NODE 4
TITLE: Support/Resistance Flip
CELEBRATION: You understand the level flip!

INTRO
TITLE: Roles Reverse
TEXT: When a level breaks, its role commonly <b>flips</b>: broken resistance often becomes support, and broken support often becomes resistance. The retest is where this flip is tested.
CHART: breakoutRetest

[MARKLEVEL] Drag the line to the level that was resistance and is now being retested as support.
CHART: breakoutRetest
LEVEL: resistance
WHY: The broken level, old resistance, now support, is what price retests.

[MC] When resistance breaks and holds, it commonly becomes...
support.
a trendline.
a gap.
WHY: Broken resistance commonly flips to act as support.

[TF] Broken support can commonly flip to act as resistance.
ANSWER: True
WHY: The flip works both ways, broken support often becomes resistance.

[FILL] A broken level commonly [___] its role (support ↔ resistance).
flips
keeps
doubles
WHY: The level's role commonly flips after a clean break.


[FILL] When a level breaks, its role commonly [___].
flips
stays the same
disappears
WHY: A broken level commonly flips, resistance becomes support, or vice versa.

[TF] Broken support can flip to act as resistance afterward.
ANSWER: True
WHY: The flip works both ways: broken support can later act as resistance.

[MC] What is being tested during a retest?
Whether the flipped role (support/resistance) holds.
The account's total balance.
The candle's exact color.
WHY: A retest specifically tests whether the newly flipped role holds.

NODE 5
TITLE: Clean vs. Weak Breakouts
CELEBRATION: You can judge a breakout's strength!

INTRO
TITLE: How Convincing Is It?
TEXT: A "clean" breakout clears the level with a strong, decisive candle. A "weak" one barely pokes through and struggles. Reading strength is descriptive context, not a guarantee.
CHART: breakout

[MC] A clean breakout is commonly described as...
clearing the level with a strong, decisive candle.
barely poking through and stalling.
never reaching the level.
WHY: A decisive, strong candle through the level reads as a cleaner breakout.

[MC] The chart above only pokes past the level, then falls back. This looks like a...
false / weak breakout.
clean breakout.
strong trend.
CHART: resistanceRejection
WHY: A poke that fails back is a weak or false breakout, not a clean one.

[TF] Even a clean-looking breakout can still fail.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Strength is descriptive context; a clean breakout is not a guarantee.

[FILL] Reading breakout strength is [___] context, not a guarantee.
descriptive
guaranteed
random
WHY: It informs the read; it doesn't promise the outcome.


[FILL] A clean breakout clears the level with a [___] candle.
strong, decisive
tiny, hesitant
random
WHY: A strong, decisive candle through the level reads as a cleaner breakout.

[TF] Even a clean-looking breakout can still fail.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Strength is descriptive context, not a guarantee, any breakout can still fail.

[MC] What describes a "weak" breakout?
One that barely pokes through the level and struggles.
One with a large, decisive candle.
One that never approaches the level at all.
WHY: A weak breakout barely clears the level and lacks conviction.
