INTRO
TITLE: Bringing It Together
TEXT: A final review of breakouts, breakdowns, retests, and the level flip.

[MATCH] Match each term to its meaning.
Breakout => Clearing resistance and continuing up
Breakdown => Clearing support and continuing down
Retest => Returning to the broken level before continuing
WHY: A quick recap of the unit's vocabulary.

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the retest candle.
CHART: breakoutRetest
TARGET: 4
WHY: The 4th candle is the retest of the broken level.

[MC] When resistance breaks and holds, its role commonly...
flips to support.
disappears entirely.
stays resistance forever.
WHY: A broken level commonly flips its role.

[FILL2] A breakout clears a [1]; a retest returns to that broken [2].
BLANK1: *level | candle | gap
BLANK2: *level | trend | wick
WHY: Clear the level, then retest that same level.

[PICK] Which best summarizes this unit?
CHART: breakoutRetest
CONCERN: Reviewing the big picture
Price clears a level, often retests it as a flipped role, and continues, read descriptively | detail: The central theme
Breakouts that guarantee a big move | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Contradicts the unit.
Levels that never change roles | detail: Wrong | wrong: Broken levels commonly flip roles.
WHY: Clear, retest, flip, descriptively, is the throughline.

[SPOT] Someone treats every breakout as a guaranteed trend and never watches for a retest or failure.
CHART: resistanceRejection
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Breakouts can fail or retest first, treating every one as a guaranteed trend ignores that.
Every breakout always trends immediately.
Retests and failures never happen.
WHY: Breakouts are descriptive clues that can fail or retest, not guarantees.


[FILL] This review ties together breakouts, breakdowns, retests, and the level [___].
flip
random guess
company name
WHY: All four core ideas from this unit are reviewed together here.

[TF] A retest can fail, with price breaking back through the flipped level.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Like other signals, retests aren't guaranteed to hold. They can fail too.

[MC] What is the single most useful habit from this unit?
Reading breakouts/breakdowns and their retests with healthy skepticism.
Assuming every breakout is guaranteed to hold.
Ignoring breakouts entirely.
WHY: Healthy skepticism paired with reading the retest is the core skill here.

UNIT 18 — Pullbacks & Continuation
NODE 1
TITLE: What Is a Pullback?
CELEBRATION: You understand pullbacks!

INTRO
TITLE: A Pause Against the Trend
TEXT: A pullback is a temporary counter-move within a trend, a small dip in an uptrend, or a small bounce in a downtrend, before the trend often resumes.
CHART: pullbackUptrend

[MC] The chart above rises, dips briefly, then rises again. The brief dip is a...
pullback.
full reversal.
breakout.
WHY: A temporary counter-move within a trend is a pullback.

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the pullback (the dip within the uptrend).
CHART: pullbackUptrend
TARGET: 3
WHY: The 3rd candle is the brief dip, the pullback, before the uptrend resumes.

[TF] A pullback is a temporary move against the trend, not necessarily a reversal.
ANSWER: True
WHY: A pullback pauses the trend briefly; it isn't automatically a reversal.

[FILL] In an uptrend, a pullback is a temporary [___] before price often rises again.
dip
surge
gap
WHY: A pullback in an uptrend is a small dip against the up-move.


[FILL] A pullback is a [___] counter-move within a trend.
temporary
permanent
guaranteed
WHY: A pullback is described as temporary, not a permanent change in direction.

[TF] A pullback within an uptrend commonly resolves as the trend resuming.
ANSWER: True
WHY: The commonly cited tendency is for the trend to resume after a healthy pullback.

[MC] What is a pullback, in simple terms?
A small counter-trend dip or bounce within an ongoing trend.
A full trend reversal.
A candle with no wicks.
WHY: A pullback describes a temporary pause, not a full reversal.

NODE 2
TITLE: Higher Lows Continue the Uptrend
CELEBRATION: You can read a healthy uptrend!

INTRO
TITLE: Each Dip Holds Higher
TEXT: In a healthy uptrend, each pullback bottoms at a <b>higher low</b> than the last, the dips get bought before reaching the previous low.
CHART: pullbackUptrend

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the higher low (the pullback that held above the prior low).
CHART: pullbackUptrend
TARGET: 3
WHY: The 3rd candle makes a higher low, keeping the uptrend healthy.

[TRENDLINE] Drag to connect the sequence of higher lows in this healthy uptrend.
CHART: pullbackUptrend
TARGET_START: 1
TARGET_END: 3
WHY: Candles 1 and 3 are the two higher lows, each pullback bottoming above the last.

[MC] A healthy uptrend makes a series of...
higher highs and higher lows.
lower highs and lower lows.
equal highs and lows.
WHY: Higher highs and higher lows define a healthy uptrend.

[FILL] Each pullback in a healthy uptrend bottoms at a [___] low.
higher
lower
zero
WHY: Higher lows keep the uptrend structure intact.

[TF] If a pullback makes a LOWER low than the previous one, the uptrend structure is weakening.
ANSWER: True
WHY: A lower low breaks the higher-low pattern, commonly read as weakening.


[FILL] A healthy uptrend's pullbacks bottom at [___] lows each time.
higher
lower
identical
WHY: Higher lows through the pullbacks is what keeps an uptrend structurally healthy.

[TF] A lower low during a pullback can be an early sign the uptrend structure is weakening.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Breaking the higher-low pattern is commonly read as a possible early warning sign.

[MC] What keeps an uptrend's structure intact through its pullbacks?
Each pullback holding above the prior low.
Each pullback breaking below the prior low.
Pullbacks never happening at all.
WHY: Holding above the prior low (a higher low) keeps the uptrend structure intact.

NODE 3
TITLE: Lower Highs Continue the Downtrend
CELEBRATION: You can read a healthy downtrend!

INTRO
TITLE: Each Bounce Fails Lower
TEXT: In a healthy downtrend, each bounce tops at a <b>lower high</b> than the last, the bounces get sold before reaching the previous high.
CHART: lowerHighsDowntrend

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the lower high (the bounce that failed below the prior high).
CHART: lowerHighsDowntrend
TARGET: 3
WHY: The 3rd candle makes a lower high, keeping the downtrend healthy.

[TRENDLINE] Drag to connect the sequence of lower highs in this healthy downtrend.
CHART: lowerHighsDowntrend
TARGET_START: 1
TARGET_END: 3
WHY: Candles 1 and 3 are the two lower highs, each bounce topping below the last.

[MC] A healthy downtrend makes a series of...
lower highs and lower lows.
higher highs and higher lows.
equal highs and lows.
WHY: Lower highs and lower lows define a healthy downtrend.

[FILL] Each bounce in a healthy downtrend tops at a [___] high.
lower
higher
flat
WHY: Lower highs keep the downtrend structure intact.

[SPOT] Someone sees a bounce make a HIGHER high and still calls the downtrend perfectly healthy.
CHART: lowerHighsDowntrend
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
A higher high breaks the lower-high pattern. That's commonly read as the downtrend weakening, not healthy.
Higher highs keep a downtrend healthy.
The highs don't matter in a downtrend.
WHY: Lower highs keep a downtrend intact; a higher high weakens it.


[FILL] A healthy downtrend's bounces top at [___] highs each time.
lower
higher
identical
WHY: Lower highs through the bounces is what keeps a downtrend structurally healthy.

[TF] A higher high during a bounce can be an early sign the downtrend structure is weakening.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Breaking the lower-high pattern is commonly read as a possible early warning sign.

[MC] What keeps a downtrend's structure intact through its bounces?
Each bounce staying below the prior high.
Each bounce breaking above the prior high.
Bounces never happening at all.
WHY: Staying below the prior high (a lower high) keeps the downtrend structure intact.

NODE 4
TITLE: Pullback vs. Reversal
CELEBRATION: You can tell a pullback from a reversal!

INTRO
TITLE: Pause or Turn?
TEXT: A pullback pauses the trend but keeps its structure (higher lows / lower highs). A reversal breaks that structure. Reading which is happening is a key skill.

[MC] The chart above dips but still makes a higher low, then continues up. This is a...
pullback (trend intact).
reversal (trend broken).
breakdown.
CHART: pullbackUptrend
WHY: A dip that holds a higher low and continues up is a pullback, not a reversal.

[MC] The chart above breaks below the prior low and keeps falling. This is closer to a...
reversal / breakdown (structure broken).
healthy pullback.
continuation up.
CHART: breakdown
WHY: Breaking the prior low breaks the up-structure, closer to a reversal.

[TF] A pullback keeps the trend's structure; a reversal breaks it.
ANSWER: True
WHY: That's the core difference, structure intact (pullback) vs broken (reversal).

[FILL2] A [1] keeps higher lows intact; a [2] breaks them.
BLANK1: *pullback | reversal | gap
BLANK2: *reversal | pullback | bounce
WHY: Pullbacks preserve structure; reversals break it.


[FILL] A pullback keeps the trend's [___] intact; a reversal breaks it.
structure
color
volume
WHY: Structure (higher lows / lower highs) staying intact is what separates a pullback from a reversal.

[TF] It's often unclear in the moment whether a counter-move is a pullback or the start of a reversal.
ANSWER: True
WHY: This distinction is frequently only clear in hindsight.

[MC] What is the key difference between a pullback and a reversal?
A pullback preserves trend structure; a reversal breaks it.
They are exactly the same thing.
A reversal always happens faster than a pullback.
WHY: Preserved vs. broken structure is the defining difference.

NODE 5
TITLE: Continuation After a Pullback
CELEBRATION: You understand continuation!

INTRO
TITLE: The Trend Resumes
TEXT: After a healthy pullback, the trend often <b>continues</b> in its original direction. "Often," not "always". It's a tendency, not a guarantee.
CHART: pullbackUptrend

[MC] After a healthy pullback in an uptrend, price commonly...
continues higher (though not guaranteed).
always reverses down.
stops moving forever.
WHY: Continuation is the common tendency after a healthy pullback, not a guarantee.

[TF] Continuation after a pullback is guaranteed.
ANSWER: False
WHY: It's a common tendency, not a certainty, the trend can still fail.

[FILL] After a healthy pullback, the trend often [___] its original direction.
resumes
abandons
reverses
WHY: The trend commonly resumes after a healthy pullback.

[SPOT] Someone assumes every pullback is guaranteed to continue the trend and ignores a broken structure.
CHART: breakdown
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Continuation is a tendency, not a guarantee. A broken structure can mean a reversal instead.
Every pullback always continues.
Structure never matters.
WHY: Continuation is likely after a healthy pullback but not guaranteed, especially if structure breaks.


[FILL] Continuation after a healthy pullback is a [___], not a guarantee.
tendency
certainty
rule
WHY: "Often, not always" describes a tendency, not a certainty.

[TF] Continuation is guaranteed after every healthy pullback.
ANSWER: False
WHY: It's a common tendency, not a guarantee, the trend can still fail to continue.

[MC] How should "the trend often continues after a pullback" be read?
As a commonly observed tendency, not a certainty.
As an absolute, guaranteed rule.
As something that never happens.
WHY: This course consistently frames such patterns as tendencies, not guarantees.

NODE 6

TITLE: Bringing It Together

[MATCH] Match each to its meaning.
Pullback => A temporary counter-move within a trend
Higher low => A pullback bottom above the prior low (uptrend intact)
Continuation => The trend resuming after a pullback

[MC] What keeps an uptrend "healthy" through its pullbacks?
Each pullback makes a higher low.
Each pullback makes a lower low.
Pullbacks never happen.
WHY: Higher lows through the pullbacks keep an uptrend healthy.

BLANK2: *tendency | certainty | rule

[PICK] Which best summarizes this unit?
CHART: pullbackUptrend

Pullbacks that guarantee continuation | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Contradicts the unit.
Pullbacks identical to full reversals | detail: Wrong | wrong: Pullbacks keep structure; reversals break it.
WHY: Structure-preserving pauses with likely (not guaranteed) continuation is the throughline.


[FILL] This review ties together pullbacks, higher lows/lower highs, and [___].
continuation
random guesses
company names
WHY: All three core ideas from this unit are reviewed together here.

[TF] Even after this review, continuation after a pullback should be read as a tendency, not a certainty.
ANSWER: True
WHY: This remains true throughout, a tendency, never a guarantee.

[MC] What is the single most useful habit from this unit?
Checking whether pullback structure (higher lows/lower highs) is holding.
Assuming every pullback is a full reversal.

UNIT 19 — Momentum & Exhaustion
NODE 1
TITLE: Reading Momentum From Bodies
CELEBRATION: You can read momentum off a chart!

TEXT: A large candle body reflects <b>momentum</b>, price moved decisively one way. Reading body size is a quick read of how strong a move is.
CHART: marubozu

[MC] The big, near-wickless candle highlighted above reflects...
strong momentum that period.
deep indecision.
no movement.
WHY: A large body (marubozu) reflects decisive momentum.

CHART: marubozu
TARGET: 2
WHY: The 2nd candle is the big-bodied momentum candle.

[FILL] A [___] body reflects stronger momentum.
large
small
zero
WHY: Bigger bodies reflect stronger, more one-sided moves.

[TF] A large candle body reflects a decisive, one-sided move.
ANSWER: True
WHY: The full body shows price pushed one way and held.


[FILL] A large candle body reflects [___] in that period.
momentum
indecision
volume only
WHY: A large body shows price moved decisively one way. That's momentum.

[TF] Reading body size is a quick way to gauge how strong a move looks.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Body size is a simple, descriptive read of a period's momentum.

[MC] What does a large, decisive candle body suggest?
Strong momentum in one direction that period.
Deep indecision.
No price movement at all.

NODE 2
TITLE: Exhaustion: Long Wicks
CELEBRATION: You can read exhaustion from wicks!

INTRO
TITLE: Pushed, Then Rejected
TEXT: A long wick shows a push that got <b>rejected</b>, a sign a move may be running out of steam (exhaustion) at that extreme.
CHART: rejectionWickUp

[MC] The long upper wick highlighted above shows...
an up-push that got rejected back down.
strong continued buying.
no movement.
WHY: A long upper wick shows buyers were rejected at the highs.

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the rejection candle (the long upper wick).
CHART: rejectionWickUp
TARGET: 2
WHY: The 2nd candle's long upper wick shows the rejected push up.

[FILL] A long wick is commonly read as a sign of possible [___] at that extreme.
exhaustion
momentum
volume
WHY: A rejected push suggests the move may be running out of steam there.

[TF] A long lower wick shows a down-push that got rejected (buyers stepped in).
CHART: rejectionWickDown
ANSWER: True
WHY: A long lower wick shows sellers were rejected and buyers recovered price.


[FILL] A long wick shows a push that got [___].
rejected
confirmed
guaranteed
WHY: The wick marks a level price touched but couldn't hold, a rejection.

[TF] A long wick can be a sign a move is running out of steam at that extreme.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Rejection at an extreme is commonly read as a possible sign of exhaustion there.

[MC] What does a long wick suggest, descriptively?
A push to an extreme that was rejected before the close.
Guaranteed continuation in that direction.
Zero price movement that period.
WHY: A long wick describes a rejected push, not a guarantee of what's next.

indecision (neither side in control).
strong momentum.
a guaranteed reversal.

indecision (a spinning top).
overwhelming momentum.
a large decisive move.
CHART: spinningTop
WHY: A spinning top's small body and balanced wicks reflect indecision.

[TF] A doji reflects strong one-directional momentum.
CHART: doji
ANSWER: False
WHY: A doji reflects balance/indecision, the opposite of strong momentum.

[FILL] Small-bodied candles reflect momentum [___], not a strong move.
stalling
surging
doubling
WHY: A small body means neither side pushed decisively, momentum stalling.

indecision
strong momentum
guaranteed reversal
WHY: Small bodies on both these shapes reflect a balanced, indecisive period.

exhaustion
continuation

UNIT 20 — Confluence
NODE 1
TITLE: What Is Confluence?

INTRO
TITLE: When Signals Line Up
TEXT: Confluence is when <b>two or more</b> readings point the same way at the same spot, for example, a support level AND a bounce candle together. More agreement makes a stronger read (still not a guarantee).
CHART: supportBounce

[MC] Confluence means...
two or more readings lining up at the same spot.
a single reading on its own.
ignoring all readings.
WHY: Confluence is multiple factors agreeing at once.

[TF] More confluence makes a read stronger, but still not guaranteed.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Agreement strengthens the read; it doesn't make it a certainty.

[FILL] Confluence is when [___] or more readings agree at the same spot.
two
zero
one
WHY: Confluence requires at least two agreeing factors.

[PICK] Why is confluence useful to read?
CHART: supportBounce
CONCERN: Reading multiple factors
Several readings agreeing is stronger evidence than one alone | detail: More agreement, stronger read
It guarantees the outcome | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: More agreement isn't a guarantee.
It replaces reading the chart | detail: Backwards | wrong: Confluence IS reading several chart factors together.
WHY: Confluence stacks evidence, stronger, but never a guarantee.


[FILL] Confluence is [___] or more readings agreeing at the same spot.
two
zero
exactly one
WHY: Confluence requires at least two agreeing factors at one spot.

[TF] More agreeing factors make a read stronger, but never guarantee the outcome.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Agreement strengthens the read; it doesn't make it a certainty.

A single isolated signal.
A guaranteed trading outcome.

WHY: The pattern plus the level (confluence) is stronger than the pattern alone.

[FILL] A double top at resistance combines a pattern with a [___].
level
color
gap
WHY: The resistance level plus the double top pattern is confluence.


[FILL] A pattern forming at a key level is an example of [___] confluence.
level + pattern
random
guaranteed
WHY: The pattern and the level reinforcing each other is this type of confluence.

[TF] A pattern in empty space (with no nearby level) is generally weaker evidence than the same pattern at a level.
ANSWER: True
WHY: A pattern reinforced by a level (confluence) is stronger than the pattern alone.

[MC] What two factors combine in "level + pattern" confluence?
A key level and a chart pattern forming there.
Two unrelated timeframes.
Candle color and account size.
WHY: A pattern reinforced by a level is this specific type of confluence.

NODE 4
TITLE: Trend + Pullback
CELEBRATION: You can read trend-plus-pullback confluence!

INTRO
TITLE: A Pullback in a Trend
TEXT: Confluence also shows up as a healthy <b>pullback within a trend</b>, the trend direction and the higher-low pullback agree on the up-bias.
CHART: pullbackUptrend

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the higher-low pullback that agrees with the uptrend.
CHART: pullbackUptrend
TARGET: 3
WHY: The higher-low pullback within the uptrend is the two factors (trend + pullback) agreeing.

[MC] An uptrend plus a higher-low pullback is an example of...
confluence (trend + pullback agree).
a contradiction.
no signal.
WHY: The trend and the pullback both pointing up is confluence.

[FILL] A higher-low pullback within an uptrend is [___] with the trend.
aligned
opposed
unrelated
WHY: The pullback and the trend agree. They're aligned (confluence).

[TF] A pullback that agrees with the trend adds confluence to an up-bias read.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Trend plus an aligned pullback is two factors agreeing, confluence.


[FILL] A healthy pullback within a trend is an example of [___] confluence.
trend + pullback
random
guaranteed
WHY: The trend direction and the pullback's structure agreeing is this type of confluence.

[TF] A pullback that breaks the trend's structure would NOT add confluence to the trend's bias.
ANSWER: True

[MC] What two factors combine in "trend + pullback" confluence?
The overall trend direction and an aligned, healthy pullback.
Two unrelated candle colors.
The account balance and volume.
WHY: Trend direction plus an aligned pullback is this specific type of confluence.

NODE 5
TITLE: More Confluence, Stronger Read
CELEBRATION: You can weigh confluence honestly!

INTRO
TITLE: Stronger, Not Certain
TEXT: The more independent readings that agree, the stronger the read. But even lots of confluence is <b>never a guarantee</b>. It stacks probability, not certainty.

[MC] Stacking more agreeing readings makes a read...
stronger, but still not guaranteed.
completely certain.
weaker.
WHY: More agreement strengthens the read without guaranteeing it.

[TF] Enough confluence eventually makes an outcome guaranteed.
ANSWER: False
WHY: Confluence stacks probability, never certainty. Nothing is guaranteed.

[FILL] Confluence stacks [___], not certainty.
probability
guarantees
volume
WHY: More agreeing factors raise the odds in the read, not a guarantee.

[SPOT] Someone finds three agreeing signals and bets everything, sure it "can't fail."
CHART: resistanceRejection
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Even strong confluence isn't a guarantee, treating it as "can't fail" overstates the read.
More confluence makes it certain.
Confluence removes all risk.
WHY: Confluence improves the read's odds but never removes uncertainty.


[FILL] Confluence stacks [___], not certainty.
probability
guarantees
volume
WHY: More agreeing factors raise the odds in the read, never guaranteeing an outcome.

[TF] Enough confluence can eventually make an outcome fully guaranteed.
ANSWER: False
WHY: No amount of confluence removes uncertainty entirely. It only stacks probability.

[MC] What is the honest limit of confluence, even with many agreeing factors?
It strengthens the read but never guarantees the outcome.
It eventually guarantees the outcome.
It has no effect on the read at all.
WHY: Confluence improves the odds of a read without ever making it certain.

It stacks probability but never guarantees an outcome.

It has no effect on a read.

UNIT 21 — Gaps
NODE 1
TITLE: What Is a Gap?
CELEBRATION: You can spot a gap!

INTRO
TITLE: A Jump With No Trading
TEXT: A gap is a jump in price where one candle opens well away from the previous candle, leaving an empty space with no trading in between. It often happens between sessions or on news.
CHART: gapUp

[MC] The chart above shows a candle opening well above the previous candle, leaving empty space. This is a...
gap.
long wick.
doji.
WHY: A jump leaving empty space between candles is a gap.

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the gap candle (the one that jumped away from the previous candle).
CHART: gapUp
TARGET: 3
WHY: The 3rd candle opens above the prior candle's high, the gap candle.

[FILL] A gap leaves an empty space with no [___] in between.
trading
color
wicks
WHY: Price jumped, so no trades happened across the gap.

[TF] A gap up opens above the previous candle's high.
CHART: gapUp
ANSWER: True
WHY: A gap up jumps above the prior candle, leaving space below the new open.


[FILL] A gap leaves an empty space with no [___] in between.
trading
color
volume only
WHY: Price jumped, so no trades happened across the gap.

[TF] A gap can occur between one candle's close and the next candle's open.
ANSWER: True
WHY: That jump between close and next open is the defining feature of a gap.

[MC] What best describes a gap?
A jump where price opens well away from the prior candle, leaving empty space.
A candle with a tiny body and long wicks.
A level tested twice by price.
WHY: A gap is specifically about a jump in price, leaving no trading in between.

NODE 2
TITLE: Gap Up vs. Gap Down
CELEBRATION: You can tell gap direction!

INTRO
TITLE: Which Way Did It Jump?
TEXT: A <b>gap up</b> opens above the prior candle's high; a <b>gap down</b> opens below the prior candle's low. Direction is just which way the jump went.
CHART: gapDown

[MC] The chart above opens well below the previous candle's low. This is a...
gap down.
gap up.
support bounce.
WHY: Opening below the prior low is a gap down.

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the gap-down candle.
CHART: gapDown
TARGET: 3
WHY: The 3rd candle opens below the prior candle's low, the gap down.

[TF] A gap down opens below the previous candle's low.
CHART: gapDown
ANSWER: True
WHY: A gap down jumps below the prior candle.

[FILL2] A gap [1] opens above the prior high; a gap [2] opens below the prior low.
BLANK1: *up | down | flat
BLANK2: *down | up | flat
WHY: Up = above prior high; down = below prior low.


[FILL] A gap up opens [___] the prior candle's high.
above
below
exactly at
WHY: Opening above the prior high is what defines a gap up.

[TF] A gap down opens below the prior candle's low.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Opening below the prior low is what defines a gap down.

[MC] What separates a gap up from a gap down?
The direction of the jump, above the prior high, or below the prior low.
The candle's color only.
The exact time of day.
WHY: Direction of the jump is what separates the two gap types.

NODE 3
TITLE: Why Gaps Happen
CELEBRATION: You understand why gaps form!

INTRO
TITLE: News and Session Breaks
TEXT: Gaps commonly form when price can't trade continuously, overnight between sessions, or when news hits and the next available price is far from the last.

[MC] Gaps commonly form because of...
session breaks or news, when trading isn't continuous.
the color of the candles.
the number of wicks.
WHY: When price can't trade continuously (overnight, news), the next price can be far away, a gap.

[TF] Gaps commonly appear between sessions or around news.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Non-continuous trading (overnight/news) is a common cause of gaps.

[FILL] A gap forms when trading isn't [___], so the next price is far from the last.
continuous
colorful
slow
WHY: Discontinuous trading lets price jump, forming a gap.

[SPOT] Someone assumes gaps only appear randomly for no reason.
CHART: gapUp
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Gaps commonly have causes, session breaks or news, not pure randomness.
Gaps never have any cause.
Gaps only depend on candle color.
WHY: Gaps usually reflect non-continuous trading (sessions/news), not randomness.


[FILL] Gaps commonly form when trading isn't [___].
continuous
colorful
random
WHY: Discontinuous trading (overnight/news) lets price jump, forming a gap.

[TF] News released while the market is closed is a commonly cited cause of gaps.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Overnight or weekend news is a commonly cited reason for the next open to jump.

[MC] Why do gaps commonly form between trading sessions?
Trading isn't continuous, so news can shift sentiment before the next open.
The chart software resets randomly.
Volume always hits zero overnight.
WHY: Non-continuous trading is the commonly cited reason gaps form between sessions.

NODE 4
TITLE: The Gap Fill
CELEBRATION: You understand gap fills!

INTRO
TITLE: Returning to the Gap
TEXT: A "gap fill" is when price later returns to trade back through the empty gap space. It happens <b>often but not always</b>, a tendency, never a guarantee.
CHART: gapUp

[MC] A "gap fill" is when price...
returns to trade back through the gap's empty space.
gaps a second time.
never moves again.
WHY: Filling the gap means price comes back through the empty space.

[TF] Every gap is guaranteed to fill.
ANSWER: False
WHY: Gaps fill often but not always. It's a tendency, not a guarantee.

[FILL] A gap fill happens [___], not always.
often
never
instantly
WHY: Gap fills are a common tendency, not a certainty.

[PICK] How should you read "gaps often fill"?
CHART: gapUp
CONCERN: Managing expectations
As a common tendency, not a guarantee | detail: Descriptive tendency
As a certainty every time | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Not every gap fills.
As something that never happens | detail: Backwards | wrong: Gap fills are common, just not guaranteed.
WHY: "Often fills" is a descriptive tendency, never a guarantee.


[FILL] A gap fill happens [___], not always.
often
never
instantly
WHY: Gap fills are a common tendency, not a certainty.

[TF] Every gap is guaranteed to eventually fill.
ANSWER: False
WHY: Gaps fill often but not always. It's a tendency, not a guarantee.

[MC] What is a "gap fill," in simple terms?
Price later trading back through the gap's empty space.
A gap that never gets touched again.
A candle with no wicks.
WHY: A gap fill specifically describes price retracing back through the skipped range.

NODE 5
TITLE: Gaps Review
CELEBRATION: You've mastered gaps!

INTRO
TITLE: Bringing It Together
TEXT: A final review of gaps, direction, causes, and gap fills.

[MATCH] Match each term to its meaning.
Gap up => Opens above the prior candle's high
Gap down => Opens below the prior candle's low
Gap fill => Price returning through the gap's empty space
WHY: A quick recap of the unit's vocabulary.

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the gap candle.
CHART: gapDown
TARGET: 3
WHY: The 3rd candle is the one that gapped away from the previous candle.

[MC] What causes most gaps?
Non-continuous trading, session breaks or news.
The candles being green.
Having too many wicks.
WHY: Discontinuous trading is the common cause of gaps.

[PICK] Which best summarizes gaps?
CHART: gapUp

Jumps leaving empty space, from discontinuous trading, that often (not always) fill | detail: The central theme
Gaps that always fill immediately | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Not every gap fills.
Random marks with no cause | detail: Wrong | wrong: Gaps usually have a cause.
WHY: Cause + a fill tendency (not guarantee) is the throughline.

gap fills
random guesses
company names
WHY: All three core ideas from this unit are reviewed together here.

ANSWER: True
WHY: This remains true throughout, a common tendency, never a guarantee.

[MC] What is the single most useful habit from this unit?
Reading gap direction and cause, without assuming a fill is guaranteed.
Assuming every gap fills immediately.

UNIT 22 — Multi-Timeframe Basics
NODE 1
TITLE: What Is a Timeframe?
CELEBRATION: You understand timeframes!

INTRO
TITLE: Each Candle's Duration
TEXT: A timeframe is how much time each candle represents, a 1-minute candle, an hourly candle, a daily candle. The same market looks different on different timeframes.
CHART: uptrend

[MC] A "timeframe" describes...
how much time each candle represents.
the color of the candles.
the number of candles only.
WHY: Timeframe is the duration each candle covers (1m, 1h, 1d, …).

[TF] The same market can look different on different timeframes.
ANSWER: True
WHY: A move that's a blip on a daily chart can be a whole trend on a 1-minute chart.

[FILL] A daily candle represents [___] time than a 1-minute candle.
more
less
the same
WHY: Each daily candle covers a full day, far more than a minute.

[MC] Which is a "higher" timeframe?
The daily chart.
The 1-minute chart.
They are the same.
WHY: Higher timeframes cover more time per candle (daily > hourly > 1-minute).


[FILL] A timeframe describes how much [___] each candle represents.
time
volume
color
WHY: Timeframe defines the duration each candle covers.

[TF] The same market can look different across different timeframes.
ANSWER: True
WHY: A move that's a blip on a daily chart can look like a full trend on a 1-minute chart.

[MC] What determines what a single candle represents?
The chosen timeframe.
The candle's color.
The number of wicks.
WHY: Timeframe determines the duration each candle summarizes.

NODE 2
TITLE: Higher vs. Lower Timeframe
CELEBRATION: You can compare timeframes!

INTRO
TITLE: Zoomed Out vs. Zoomed In
TEXT: A <b>higher</b> timeframe (daily) is the zoomed-out big picture; a <b>lower</b> timeframe (1-minute) is the zoomed-in detail. Both describe the same market at different scales.

[MC] The higher timeframe shows...
the zoomed-out big picture.
only the last candle.
the smallest detail.
WHY: Higher timeframes are the big-picture, zoomed-out view.

[MC] The lower timeframe shows...
the zoomed-in detail.
the whole year at once.
nothing useful.
WHY: Lower timeframes zoom in on fine detail within the bigger move.

[FILL2] A higher timeframe is more [1]; a lower timeframe is more [2].
BLANK1: *zoomed-out | zoomed-in | random
BLANK2: *zoomed-in | zoomed-out | flat
WHY: Higher = zoomed out (big picture); lower = zoomed in (detail).

[TF] Higher and lower timeframes describe the same market at different scales.
ANSWER: True
WHY: They're the same price data viewed zoomed out vs zoomed in.


[FILL] A higher timeframe shows the [___] picture.
zoomed-out, big
zoomed-in, small
identical
WHY: Higher timeframes compress more history into each candle, the big picture.

[TF] A lower timeframe shows more fine-grained detail over a shorter period.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Lower timeframes zoom in, offering finer detail over less total time.

[MC] What do higher and lower timeframes have in common?
Both describe the same market, just at different scales.
They describe two entirely unrelated markets.
Only one of them is ever accurate.
WHY: They're the same underlying price data, viewed at different zoom levels.

NODE 3
TITLE: Trends Can Differ by Timeframe
CELEBRATION: You can read trends across timeframes!

INTRO
TITLE: Up Here, Down There
TEXT: A market can be in an uptrend on the higher timeframe while showing a short-term downtrend on a lower one. A pullback on the daily can look like a full downtrend on the 1-minute.
CHART: pullbackUptrend

[MC] The chart above is a higher-timeframe uptrend with a dip. On a lower timeframe, that dip might look like a...
short-term downtrend.
higher-timeframe uptrend.
gap.
WHY: A higher-timeframe pullback can appear as a full downtrend when zoomed in.

[TF] A pullback on a higher timeframe can look like a downtrend on a lower timeframe.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Zooming into a pullback can show a complete small downtrend.

[FILL] A market can trend one way on a higher timeframe and [___] on a lower one.
differently
identically
never
WHY: Trends can differ across timeframes for the same market.

[SPOT] Someone reads only the 1-minute chart and ignores the daily trend entirely.
CHART: pullbackUptrend
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Ignoring the higher timeframe misses the big-picture trend the short-term move sits inside.
The higher timeframe never matters.
Only the 1-minute chart matters.
WHY: The higher-timeframe context frames what the lower-timeframe move means.


[FILL] A pullback on a higher timeframe can look like a full [___] on a lower one.
downtrend
uptrend
gap
WHY: Zooming into a higher-timeframe pullback can reveal what looks like a complete small downtrend.

[TF] Trend direction can differ depending on which timeframe you're viewing.
ANSWER: True
WHY: The same market can trend one way on one timeframe and differently on another.

[MC] Why might a chart look like it's trending down on a 1-minute chart but up on the daily?
The lower timeframe may just be showing a pullback within the larger uptrend.
The two timeframes are showing completely different assets.

WHY: A higher-timeframe pullback can appear as its own trend when zoomed in.

NODE 4
TITLE: Top-Down Reading
CELEBRATION: You understand top-down reading!

INTRO
TITLE: Big Picture First
TEXT: "Top-down" reading means checking the <b>higher</b> timeframe first for the big-picture trend and key levels, then zooming into a lower one for detail. Context first, detail second.

[MC] Top-down reading starts with the...
higher timeframe (big picture).
lower timeframe (detail).
smallest possible timeframe.
WHY: Top-down means big-picture (higher timeframe) first, then detail.

[ORDER] A top-down reading process:
Check the higher timeframe for the big-picture trend
Note the higher-timeframe key levels
Zoom into a lower timeframe for detail
Read the lower-timeframe move inside that context
WHY: Context first (higher timeframe), then zoom in. That's top-down.

[TF] Top-down reading checks the big-picture timeframe before the detail one.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Big picture first, then detail, is the top-down idea.

[FILL] Top-down reading sets [___] from the higher timeframe first.
context
color
volume
WHY: The higher timeframe provides the context to read the detail within.


[FILL] Top-down reading checks the [___] timeframe first.
higher
lower
smallest possible
WHY: Top-down means starting with the big picture (higher timeframe) before zooming in.

[TF] Top-down reading means checking the lower timeframe before the higher one.
ANSWER: False
WHY: Top-down specifically means higher timeframe first, then lower for detail.

[MC] What is the point of checking a higher timeframe before a lower one?
It sets big-picture context before looking at fine detail.
It replaces the need to ever look at a lower timeframe.
It has no real purpose.
WHY: Context from the higher timeframe frames how the lower-timeframe detail is read.

NODE 5

CHART: uptrend

[MC] When the higher and lower timeframe both trend up, that agreement is a form of...
confluence (a stronger read).
contradiction.
a gap.
WHY: Agreement across timeframes is confluence, a stronger read.

[TF] Timeframe agreement makes a read stronger but not guaranteed.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Alignment stacks evidence (confluence), which is stronger but never certain.

[FILL] When timeframes agree, it adds [___] to the read.
confluence
randomness
volume
WHY: Agreeing timeframes are another agreeing factor, confluence.

[SPOT] Someone sees both timeframes agree and assumes the trade "can't lose."
CHART: uptrend
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Timeframe agreement is stronger evidence but never a guarantee, "can't lose" overstates it.
Agreement makes it certain.
Alignment removes all risk.
WHY: Even aligned timeframes only stack probability, not certainty.


[FILL] Timeframes agreeing with each other is a form of [___].
confluence
contradiction
randomness
WHY: Multiple timeframes pointing the same way is an example of confluence.

[TF] Timeframe alignment guarantees the trade idea will work out.
ANSWER: False
WHY: Alignment strengthens the read but never guarantees the outcome.

[MC] What happens when the higher and lower timeframe both point the same direction?
That agreement is read as a stronger, more confluent signal.
It automatically guarantees success.
It has no added meaning at all.
WHY: Agreement across timeframes adds confluence, stronger, but not certain.

The big picture frames what the short-term move means.

WHY: Context from the higher timeframe frames the lower-timeframe detail.

alignment
random guesses
company names

NODE 2
TITLE: Level Reading Round
CELEBRATION: You can mark levels anywhere!

INTRO
TITLE: Find the Level
TEXT: Read the key level on each chart and mark it.

[MARKLEVEL] Drag the line to the resistance on this chart.
CHART: resistanceRejection
LEVEL: resistance
WHY: The level the highs were rejected at is the resistance.

[MARKLEVEL] Drag the line to the support on this chart.
CHART: supportBounce
LEVEL: support
WHY: The level the lows bounced from is the support.

[MARKLEVEL] Drag the line to the flat resistance of this triangle.
CHART: ascendingTriangle
LEVEL: resistance
WHY: The flat top of the ascending triangle is the resistance.

[MARKLEVEL] Drag the line to the range's support.
CHART: range
LEVEL: support
WHY: The floor of the range is the support.

[MARKLEVEL] Drag the line to the neckline of this head & shoulders.
CHART: headShoulders
LEVEL: support
WHY: The neckline runs along the two troughs of the head & shoulders.


[MARKLEVEL] Drag the line to the support in this range.
CHART: range
LEVEL: support
WHY: The floor of the range is its support.

[MARKLEVEL] Drag the line to the resistance in this breakout chart.
CHART: breakout
LEVEL: resistance
WHY: The level price cleared with momentum is the resistance being broken out of.

[CONSOLIDATION] Drag across every candle in this range's consolidation.
CHART: range
TARGET_LO: 1
TARGET_HI: 6
WHY: All six candles here stay bounded between the support and resistance.

[TF] Marking a level is a matter of judgment, not an exact science.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Support and resistance are estimated zones based on past reactions, not precise fixed lines.

NODE 3
TITLE: Candle Reading Round
CELEBRATION: You can read any candle!

INTRO
TITLE: Tap the Candle
TEXT: Read each chart and tap the candle described.

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the doji.
CHART: doji
TARGET: 2
WHY: The middle candle's tiny body with wicks both sides is the doji.

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the hammer.
CHART: hammer
TARGET: 3
WHY: The last candle, small body up top, long lower wick, is the hammer.

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the shooting star.
CHART: shootingStar
TARGET: 3
WHY: The last candle, small body down low, long upper wick, is the shooting star.

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the engulfing candle.
CHART: bearishEngulfing
TARGET: 2
WHY: The larger down candle covering the prior up candle is the bearish engulfing.

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the big-momentum candle.
CHART: marubozu
TARGET: 2
WHY: The big-bodied, near-wickless candle is the momentum (marubozu) candle.


[TAPCANDLE] Tap the spinning top.
CHART: spinningTop
TARGET: 2
WHY: The small body with even wicks on both sides is the spinning top.

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the inverted hammer.
CHART: invertedHammer
TARGET: 3
WHY: A long upper wick with a small low body after a decline is the inverted hammer.

[TF] Reading candles well means considering both body and wicks together.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Body and wicks together tell the fuller story of a candle's period.

NODE 4
TITLE: Multi-Candle Round
CELEBRATION: You can tap groups of candles!

INTRO
TITLE: Tap All That Match
TEXT: Some reads need more than one candle. Tap every candle that matches.

[TAPCANDLES] Tap BOTH peaks of the double top.
CHART: doubleTop
TARGETS: 2, 4
WHY: The 2nd and 4th candles are the two peaks at resistance.

[TAPCANDLES] Tap BOTH troughs of the double bottom.
CHART: doubleBottom
TARGETS: 2, 4
WHY: The 2nd and 4th candles are the two troughs at support.

[TAPCANDLES] Tap BOTH shoulders of the head & shoulders.
CHART: headShoulders
TARGETS: 2, 6
WHY: The 2nd and 6th candles are the left and right shoulders.

[TAPCANDLES] Tap the three touches of the flat resistance.
CHART: ascendingTriangle
TARGETS: 1, 3, 5
WHY: The 1st, 3rd, and 5th candles push into the flat resistance.


[TAPCANDLES] Tap the three touches of the flat support in this descending triangle.
CHART: descendingTriangle
TARGETS: 1, 3, 5
WHY: The 1st, 3rd, and 5th candles push down into the flat support.

[TRENDLINE] Drag to connect the two troughs that form this head & shoulders' neckline.
CHART: headShoulders
TARGET_START: 3
TARGET_END: 5
WHY: Candles 3 and 5 are the troughs on either side of the head.

[CONSOLIDATION] Drag across the candles that make up this bull flag's pause.
CHART: bullFlag
TARGET_LO: 3
TARGET_HI: 4
WHY: Candles 3 and 4 are the small downward drift after the pole.

[TF] Multi-select questions ask you to identify more than one matching candle at once.
ANSWER: True
WHY: That's the defining feature of this question type, unlike single tap-the-candle questions.

NODE 5
TITLE: Reversal or Continuation?
CELEBRATION: You can classify the setup type!

INTRO
TITLE: Turn or Keep Going?
TEXT: Classify each chart as a reversal shape (a possible turn) or a continuation shape (a likely resume), descriptively.

[MC] Is a head & shoulders commonly read as a reversal or a continuation shape?
Reversal (a possible turn after an up-move).
Continuation.
Neither.
CHART: headShoulders
WHY: Head & shoulders is commonly read as a reversal shape.

[MC] Is a bull flag commonly read as a reversal or a continuation shape?
Continuation (a pause that often resumes up).
Reversal.
Neither.
CHART: bullFlag
WHY: Flags are continuation patterns.

[MC] Is a double bottom commonly read as a reversal or continuation shape?
Reversal (a possible turn up after a down-move).
Continuation down.
Neither.
CHART: doubleBottom
WHY: A double bottom is commonly read as a reversal shape.

[MATCH] Match each to its usual category.
Head & shoulders => Reversal shape
Flag / pennant => Continuation shape
Double top / bottom => Reversal shape
WHY: Reversal shapes suggest a turn; continuation shapes suggest a resume.

[TF] Every reversal shape guarantees a reversal.
ANSWER: False
WHY: Categories describe tendencies; nothing is guaranteed.


[MC] Is a triangle commonly read as continuation, reversal, or either?
Either. It can break in either direction, though flat sides add a bias.
Always continuation.
Always reversal.
WHY: Triangles can break either way, though ascending/descending triangles carry a directional bias.

[TF] Wedges are commonly read as reversal shapes despite their sloped direction.
ANSWER: True
WHY: A rising wedge (despite rising) and falling wedge (despite falling) are both commonly read as reversal-biased.

NODE 6

CELEBRATION: You can read a full chart!

A double top at resistance, a reversal shape at a level (confluence) | detail: Pattern + level
A guaranteed crash | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Confluence isn't a guarantee.
A bull flag | detail: Wrong pattern | wrong: This is two peaks at resistance, not a pole-and-flag.
WHY: Pattern + level agreeing is a confluent, descriptive read, not a guarantee.

[MARKLEVEL] Drag the line to the resistance in this double top.
CHART: doubleTop

WHY: The two peaks mark the resistance being tested.

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the second peak.
CHART: doubleTop
TARGET: 4

ANSWER: True
WHY: Pattern-plus-level is confluence, stronger than either alone.

[SPOT] Someone reads one perfect pattern and ignores the level, the trend, and the candles around it.

QUESTION: What is the mistake?
A full read combines pattern, level, trend, and candles, not one factor in isolation.
Only the pattern ever matters.
Context never helps.
WHY: The strongest reads combine several agreeing factors, not a lone signal.


[TAPCANDLES] Tap BOTH shoulders of this head & shoulders.
CHART: headShoulders
TARGETS: 2, 6
WHY: The 2nd and 6th candles are the left and right shoulders.

[MARKLEVEL] Drag the line to the neckline of this head & shoulders.
CHART: headShoulders
LEVEL: support
WHY: The neckline runs along the two troughs framing the head.

[TF] Combining pattern, level, and candle reads gives a fuller picture than any one alone.

NODE 7

Reading charts descriptively, patterns, levels, and candles as clues, not guarantees.
Finding guaranteed signals.
Ignoring context.

confluence
guesswork

UNIT 24 — Trading Sessions & News
NODE 1
TITLE: Market Sessions Overview
CELEBRATION: You understand the basic session structure!

INTRO
TITLE: Pre-Market and the Open
TEXT: Before the standard session, "pre-market" trading happens with thinner liquidity. "The open" is the first stretch of the standard session, commonly the most active part of the day.

[MC] "Pre-market" refers to...
Trading before the standard session begins.
Trading only on weekends.
A period when markets are fully closed.
WHY: Pre-market is the extended session before the standard open.

[TF] Pre-market trading commonly has thinner liquidity than the standard session.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Fewer participants are commonly active during pre-market hours.

[FILL] "The open" refers to the first stretch of the [___] session.
standard
pre-market
after-hours
WHY: "The open" describes the beginning of the standard trading session.

[MATCH] Match the session period to its common trait.
Pre-market => Thinner liquidity, before standard hours
The open => Commonly the most active part of the day
Standard session => The main trading hours most participants use
WHY: Each session period commonly has a distinct liquidity and activity profile.

[PICK] Why is "the open" commonly considered especially active?
CONCERN: Understanding session structure
Many participants who were waiting through pre-market act once standard hours begin | detail: A commonly cited pattern
The open has no relationship to activity level | detail: Incorrect | wrong: The open is commonly one of the most active periods.
Trading is impossible during the open | detail: Incorrect | wrong: The open is trading, just often the busiest part.
WHY: Pent-up activity from pre-market commonly concentrates right at the open.

[SPOT] Someone assumes pre-market liquidity is always identical to the standard session's.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Pre-market commonly has thinner liquidity than the standard session, treating them as identical ignores that difference.
Pre-market always has more liquidity than standard hours.
Session timing never affects liquidity.
WHY: Liquidity commonly varies a lot by session, a pattern worth being aware of.

NODE 2
TITLE: Midday Lull and Power Hour
CELEBRATION: You understand the midday lull and power hour!

INTRO
TITLE: The Quiet Middle, the Busy End
TEXT: Midday ("lunch") is commonly a quieter stretch with reduced activity. "Power hour" is the final hour of the standard session, commonly picking activity back up.

[MC] The midday period is commonly described as...
Quieter, with reduced activity compared to the open or close.
The single busiest period of the entire day.
A period when the market is fully closed.
WHY: Midday is commonly a lull in activity between the open and the close.

[TF] "Power hour" refers to the final hour of the standard session.
ANSWER: True
WHY: That's the standard meaning of the term.

[FILL] Power hour commonly sees activity [___] compared to the midday lull.
pick back up
disappear entirely
stay identical
WHY: Activity commonly increases again heading into the close.

[MATCH] Match the session period to its common activity level.
Midday lull => Reduced activity
Power hour => Activity picking back up
Standard session shape => Busy-quiet-busy across open, midday, and close
WHY: Standard sessions commonly follow this busy-quiet-busy activity pattern.

[PICK] Why might power hour commonly see increased activity?
CONCERN: Understanding this session period
Participants commonly position or adjust ahead of the session's close | detail: A commonly cited pattern
Power hour has no relationship to activity level | detail: Incorrect | wrong: It's commonly one of the more active periods.
The market briefly reopens during power hour | detail: Incorrect | wrong: Power hour is part of the same standard session, not a reopening.
WHY: End-of-day positioning is a commonly cited reason for power hour's added activity.

[SPOT] Someone assumes midday is always the busiest part of the trading day.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Midday is commonly the quieter lull, not the busiest period. That's more commonly the open or power hour.
Midday is always busier than the open.
Session timing has no effect on typical activity level.
WHY: The open and power hour are more commonly cited as the busier periods, not midday.

NODE 3
TITLE: After-Hours Trading
CELEBRATION: You understand after-hours trading!

INTRO
TITLE: After the Bell
TEXT: "After-hours" trading happens once the standard session ends, commonly with thinner liquidity, similar in spirit to pre-market, just on the other side of the day.

[MC] "After-hours" trading refers to...
Trading after the standard session has ended.
Trading only during the standard session.
A period when no trading of any kind occurs.
WHY: After-hours describes the extended session following the standard close.

[TF] After-hours trading commonly has thinner liquidity, similar to pre-market.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Both extended sessions commonly see reduced participation compared to standard hours.

[FILL] After-hours is the [___]-session counterpart to pre-market.
extended
standard
identical
WHY: Both are extended sessions, just on opposite sides of the standard hours.

[MATCH] Match the extended session to its position relative to standard hours.
Pre-market => Before standard hours
After-hours => After standard hours
Both => Commonly thinner liquidity than standard hours
WHY: Pre-market and after-hours are the two extended sessions bookending the standard day.

[PICK] Why might a large order have a bigger price impact after-hours?
CONCERN: Applying the liquidity idea
Thinner liquidity commonly means the same order size has more relative price impact | detail: A commonly cited pattern
After-hours liquidity always exceeds standard-hours liquidity | detail: Incorrect | wrong: The reverse is more commonly true.
Order size never affects price impact | detail: Incorrect | wrong: With thinner liquidity, size matters even more.
WHY: This connects the liquidity idea from earlier units to the after-hours session specifically.

[SPOT] Someone expects an after-hours fill to behave identically to a standard-hours fill.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Thinner after-hours liquidity can commonly lead to bigger gaps between expected and actual fill prices.
After-hours fills always match standard-hours fills exactly.
Liquidity has no relationship to fill behavior.
WHY: As covered in the Orders & Execution unit, thinner liquidity commonly affects fill behavior.

NODE 4
TITLE: Earnings Reports
CELEBRATION: You understand earnings reports!

INTRO
TITLE: A Scheduled Information Event
TEXT: An earnings report is a company's scheduled release of financial results, commonly associated with sharply increased volatility around the release.
CHART: gapUp

[MC] An "earnings report" is...
A company's scheduled release of financial results.
A random, unscheduled event.
A type of order.
WHY: Earnings reports are scheduled releases of a company's financial performance.

[TF] Earnings reports are commonly associated with increased volatility around the release.
ANSWER: True
WHY: New information hitting the market at once commonly produces sharper price swings.

[FILL] Earnings reports are commonly [___] in advance, unlike random news.
scheduled
unpredictable
irrelevant
WHY: Being scheduled in advance is what distinguishes earnings from random breaking news.

[MATCH] Match the event to its commonly cited trait.
Earnings report => Scheduled, often volatile around the release
Gap on earnings => A commonly cited example of a scheduling-driven gap
Volatility spike => A commonly cited effect around the release
WHY: Earnings connect directly back to the gaps and volatility concepts from earlier units.

[PICK] Why are earnings reports commonly linked to gaps?
CHART: gapUp
CONCERN: Connecting concepts
New information released while the market is closed can shift the next open sharply | detail: Connects to the Gaps unit
Earnings reports never affect price at all | detail: Incorrect | wrong: They're commonly linked to some of the largest volatility events.
Gaps only happen for reasons unrelated to earnings | detail: Incomplete | wrong: Earnings are a commonly cited gap driver.
WHY: This ties the earlier Gaps unit directly to a commonly cited real-world cause.

[SPOT] Someone assumes earnings reports have no relationship to price volatility.
CHART: gapUp
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Earnings reports are commonly associated with some of the sharpest volatility spikes around their release.
Earnings reports never affect price behavior.
Only random, unscheduled news can affect volatility.
WHY: Scheduled information events like earnings are commonly cited volatility drivers.

NODE 5
TITLE: Economic Releases
CELEBRATION: You understand economic releases!

INTRO
TITLE: Broader Information Events
TEXT: Economic releases (like employment or inflation data) are scheduled reports about the broader economy, commonly associated with volatility across many assets at once, not just one company.

[MC] Economic releases commonly affect...
Many assets at once, since they describe the broader economy.
Only one single company.
Nothing at all in the markets.
WHY: Broader economic data commonly moves many assets simultaneously, unlike company-specific earnings.

[TF] Economic releases are commonly scheduled in advance, similar to earnings reports.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Both are commonly known events with a set release time.

[FILL] Economic releases commonly affect [___] assets at once, unlike single-company earnings.
many
zero
exactly one
WHY: Broad economic data is commonly linked to market-wide moves, not one company alone.

[MATCH] Match the event type to its commonly cited scope.
Earnings report => One specific company
Economic release => Many assets across the broader market
Both => Scheduled events commonly linked to volatility
WHY: Scope (one company vs. the broader market) is the key difference between these two event types.

[PICK] Why might economic releases commonly cause broader market volatility?
CONCERN: Understanding scope
They describe the broader economy, which can affect sentiment across many assets at once | detail: A wide-reaching event
They only ever affect a single company | detail: Incorrect | wrong: That describes earnings, not economic releases.
They have no relationship to market behavior | detail: Incorrect | wrong: They're commonly cited as broad volatility drivers.
WHY: Broad economic data commonly shifts sentiment market-wide, unlike a single company's earnings.

[SPOT] Someone assumes economic releases only affect the specific company mentioned in the report.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Economic releases commonly affect many assets across the market, not just one specific company.
Economic releases always affect only one company.
Economic releases and earnings reports describe the same scope.
WHY: Economic releases are commonly cited as broader, market-wide events.

NODE 6
TITLE: Why Volatility Spikes Around News
CELEBRATION: You understand why volatility spikes around news!

INTRO
TITLE: New Information Hits at Once
TEXT: Volatility commonly spikes around scheduled news because a lot of new information gets absorbed by the market all at once, rather than gradually.
CHART: volatile

[MC] Why does volatility commonly spike around scheduled news?
New information gets absorbed by the market all at once, rather than gradually.
News releases always calm the market down.
Volatility has no relationship to news timing.
WHY: A sudden burst of new information commonly produces sharper, faster price swings.

[TF] Volatility spikes around news are commonly temporary, not a permanent state.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Volatility commonly settles back down after the initial reaction to new information.

[FILL] Volatility spikes because information is absorbed all at [___], not gradually.
once
random
no particular time
WHY: A sudden burst of information, rather than a gradual release, is what commonly drives the spike.

[MATCH] Match the concept to its commonly cited connection to news.
Earnings/economic releases => Common sources of sudden new information
Volatility spike => A commonly cited effect of that sudden information
Gap => Another commonly cited effect around scheduled news
WHY: These concepts connect back to ideas from Gaps and Market Mechanics earlier in the course.

[PICK] Which best explains why news-driven volatility is commonly temporary?
CHART: volatile
CONCERN: Reading it honestly
Once the market absorbs the new information, activity commonly settles back toward typical levels | detail: A commonly cited pattern
News-driven volatility never settles back down | detail: Overstates it | wrong: It's commonly described as temporary, not permanent.
Volatility has no relationship to how information gets absorbed | detail: Backwards | wrong: This lesson's core point is that connection.
WHY: Volatility spikes are commonly read as a temporary reaction, not a lasting new state.

[SPOT] Someone assumes volatility around a news event will stay permanently elevated forever.
CHART: volatile
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
News-driven volatility is commonly temporary, settling back down once the information is absorbed.
Volatility spikes always last permanently.
News has no relationship to volatility at all.
WHY: This is a commonly cited temporary pattern, not a permanent shift.

NODE 7
TITLE: Trading Around News
CELEBRATION: You understand the cautious approach to trading around news!

INTRO
TITLE: Extra Caution, Not a Guarantee
TEXT: Because of the added volatility and wider spreads commonly seen around news, many traders commonly apply extra caution, like reduced size or wider stops, rather than a rigid rule.

[MC] A commonly cited approach around scheduled news is to...
Apply extra caution, like reduced size or wider stops.
Ignore risk management entirely during news.
Always dramatically increase size right before news.
WHY: Extra caution around the added volatility is a commonly cited approach, not a guaranteed rule.

[TF] Spreads commonly widen around major scheduled news.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Increased uncertainty commonly widens the bid/ask spread around news events.

[FILL] Extra caution around news is a commonly cited [___], not a rigid universal rule.
approach
guarantee
requirement
WHY: Different traders commonly apply this idea differently. It's a general approach, not one fixed rule.

[MATCH] Match the news-related risk factor to its commonly cited behavior.
Spread => Commonly widens around major news
Volatility => Commonly spikes around major news
Position sizing => Commonly reduced by some traders around news
WHY: These three factors are commonly discussed together when planning around news events.

[PICK] Why might someone reduce position size around scheduled news?
CONCERN: Applying risk concepts to news
Because added volatility and wider spreads can increase risk per trade around news | detail: Connects to risk management
Because news guarantees a losing trade | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: News doesn't guarantee an outcome, it adds uncertainty.
Because position size never matters around news | detail: Incorrect | wrong: Size is exactly the risk lever some traders adjust.
WHY: This connects the Risk Management unit's ideas directly to a real-world scenario.

[SPOT] Someone dramatically increases their position size right before a major scheduled news release, assuming it's the same as any other moment.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?

News has no relationship to typical risk levels.
Increasing size before news always leads to bigger profits.
WHY: This ignores the commonly cited added risk around scheduled news events.

CHART: volatile

[MC] Why might a chart look different during pre-market versus the standard open?
Liquidity and participation commonly differ by session, changing the chart's rhythm.
Charts are always identical no matter the session.
Session timing has no relationship to chart behavior.
WHY: Session-specific liquidity differences are commonly cited as changing how a chart behaves.

[TF] The open, midday, and power hour commonly have different typical rhythms within the same day.
ANSWER: True

context
guesswork
random noise
WHY: Like other context factors in this course, session timing adds to (not replaces) the full read.

WHY: A recap of the session-based rhythm differences covered across this unit.

[PICK] Why combine session awareness with everything else you've learned?
CHART: volatile

Because it's one more piece of context that can change how a chart's move should be read | detail: Adds to the full picture
Because session timing replaces the need for any other analysis | detail: Overstates it | wrong: It's one added factor, not a replacement for everything else.
Because session timing has no bearing on reading a chart | detail: Backwards | wrong: This whole unit shows it commonly does.

[SPOT] Someone reads a choppy, thin pre-market move exactly the same way as an identical-looking move during the standard open.

UNIT 25 — Risk Management
NODE 1
TITLE: What Is Risk Per Trade?
CELEBRATION: You understand risk per trade!

INTRO
TITLE: How Much Is on the Line
TEXT: Risk per trade describes how much of your capital could be lost on a single trade if it goes against you.
CHART: downtrend

[TF] Risk per trade describes how much capital could be lost on a single trade if it goes against you.
ANSWER: True
WHY: That's the standard definition of risk per trade.

$500.
$100.
WHY: 1R = the amount risked = $250.

[TF] Measuring results in R lets you compare trades of different dollar sizes.
ANSWER: True
WHY: R normalizes results, so a "2R win" means the same regardless of position size.


[FILL] "R" is shorthand for the amount [___] on a trade.
risked
made
skipped
WHY: R specifically represents the risk amount, used to measure results.

[TF] Measuring results in R lets you compare trades of different sizes fairly.
ANSWER: True
WHY: R normalizes results across different position sizes.

NODE 4
TITLE: Reward in R
CELEBRATION: You can measure reward in R!

INTRO
TITLE: Wins as Multiples of Risk
TEXT: A win is measured in R too. If you risk $100 (1R) and gain $200, that's a <b>+2R</b> result. A loss of the full risk is <b>−1R</b>.

[MC] You risk $100 and the trade gains $200. That result is...
+2R.
+1R.
−1R.
WHY: A $200 gain on $100 risk is twice the risk, +2R.

[MC] You risk $100 and the trade loses the full risk. That result is...
−1R.
−2R.
+1R.
WHY: Losing the full amount risked is −1R.

[FILL] A gain of three times your risk is a [___] result.
+3R
+1R
−3R
WHY: Three times the risk gained is +3R.

[MC] You risk $200 and gain $600. That result is...
+3R.
+2R.
+6R.
WHY: $600 gain on $200 risk is three times the risk, +3R.

[TF] A full loss is −1R, since you lost exactly the amount you risked.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Losing the whole risked amount is by definition −1R.


[FILL] A gain of twice the amount risked is a [___] result.
+2R
+1R
-1R
WHY: Twice the risk gained is +2R, by definition.

[TF] A full loss of the entire risked amount is described as -1R.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Losing exactly what was risked is, by definition, -1R.

NODE 5
TITLE: Position Size From Risk & Stop
CELEBRATION: You understand position sizing!

INTRO
TITLE: Size to Fit the Risk
TEXT: Position size is commonly worked <b>backward</b> from your dollar risk and stop distance: choose a size so that if the stop is hit, the loss equals your planned dollar risk.

[MC] Position size is commonly calculated from your...
dollar risk and stop distance.
favorite number.
the candle color.
WHY: Size is derived from how much you'll risk and how far the stop is.

[MC] If your stop is further away, to keep the same dollar risk you use a...
smaller position size.
larger position size.
the same size always.
WHY: A wider stop means a smaller size keeps the dollar loss the same.

[FILL] Position size is worked [___] from your dollar risk and stop.
backward
forward
randomly
WHY: You start from the risk and stop, then solve for size.

[SPOT] Someone uses the same fixed position size no matter how far away the stop is.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
A fixed size regardless of stop distance makes the dollar risk vary unpredictably between trades.
Size should never change.
Stop distance never matters for sizing.
WHY: Consistent dollar risk requires adjusting size to the stop distance.


[FILL] Position size is worked [___] from your dollar risk and stop distance.
backward
forward
randomly
WHY: You start from the risk and stop, then solve for the position size.

[TF] A wider stop distance calls for a larger position size to keep the same dollar risk.
ANSWER: False
WHY: A wider stop calls for a SMALLER size to keep the dollar risk the same, not larger.

NODE 6
TITLE: Position Sizing & R Review
CELEBRATION: You've mastered position sizing & R!

INTRO
TITLE: Bringing It Together
TEXT: A final review of risk per trade, dollar risk, R, and position sizing.

[MC] On a $10,000 account, risking 1% is...
$100 (which is 1R).
$1,000.
$10.
WHY: 1% of $10,000 is $100, that trade's 1R.

[MC] You risk $100 and gain $300. That's...
+3R.
+1R.
−1R.
WHY: $300 on $100 risk is +3R.

[MATCH] Match each term to its meaning.
1R => The amount risked on the trade
+2R => A gain of twice the risk
Position size => Chosen so a stopped-out loss equals the planned risk
WHY: A recap of the unit's core math.

[FILL2] Dollar risk = account size × risk [1]; results are measured in multiples of [2].
BLANK1: *percent | color | volume
BLANK2: *R | candles | days
WHY: Percent gives dollar risk; R measures the results.

[PICK] Which best summarizes this unit?
CONCERN: Reviewing the big picture
Risk a small consistent percent, size from the stop, and measure results in R | detail: The central theme
Size randomly and hope | detail: No method | wrong: The unit is about a consistent method.
Bigger size always means bigger profit | detail: Overstates | wrong: Size sets risk, not guaranteed profit.
WHY: Consistent percent risk, stop-based sizing, and R measurement is the throughline.


[FILL] This review ties together risk per trade, dollar risk, R, and [___].
position sizing
random guesses
company names
WHY: All four core ideas from this unit are reviewed together here.

[TF] R lets you compare trade results across different position sizes.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Measuring in R (multiples of risk) makes results comparable regardless of size.

UNIT 28 — Expectancy & Edge
NODE 1
TITLE: Win Rate Isn't Everything
CELEBRATION: You understand why win rate alone misleads!

INTRO
TITLE: How Often vs. How Much
TEXT: Win rate is how <b>often</b> you win. But it ignores how <b>much</b> you win or lose. A high win rate with tiny wins and huge losses can still lose money overall.

[MC] Win rate measures...
how often trades win.
how much each trade wins.
the account size.
WHY: Win rate is the frequency of wins, not their size.

[TF] A high win rate always means a profitable system.
ANSWER: False
WHY: If wins are tiny and losses are huge, even a high win rate can lose overall.

[FILL] Win rate ignores how [___] you win or lose per trade.
much
often
fast
WHY: Win rate is frequency only; it ignores the size of wins and losses.

[PICK] Why isn't win rate enough on its own?
CONCERN: Reading results honestly
Because it ignores the size of the wins and losses | detail: How much matters too
Because it's always wrong | detail: Overstates | wrong: Win rate is useful, just incomplete.
Because winning never matters | detail: Backwards | wrong: Both how often and how much matter.
WHY: You need both how often (win rate) and how much (R) to judge results.


[FILL] Win rate measures how [___] you win, not how much.
often
much
fast
WHY: Win rate is purely a frequency measure, not a measure of size.

[TF] A high win rate always means a profitable system.
ANSWER: False
WHY: Tiny wins and huge losses can still lose money overall, even with a high win rate.

NODE 2
TITLE: Combining Win Rate and R
CELEBRATION: You can combine win rate and R!

INTRO
TITLE: Frequency Meets Size
TEXT: The honest picture combines <b>win rate</b> (how often) with <b>average R</b> (how much). A lower win rate can still come out ahead if the average win in R is large enough.

[MC] To judge a system honestly, combine win rate with...
average R per trade.
the candle color.
the time of day only.
WHY: Win rate plus average R (size) gives the honest picture.

[TF] A 40% win rate can still be profitable if the average winner is large in R.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Big average wins (in R) can outweigh more frequent small losses.

[FILL2] Combine how [1] you win with how [2] you win to judge a system.
BLANK1: *often | fast | green
BLANK2: *much | often | slow
WHY: Frequency (win rate) and size (R) together tell the story.

[MC] A system wins 40% of the time but averages +3R on wins and −1R on losses. This can be...
profitable overall, thanks to the large average win.
guaranteed to lose because win rate is under 50%.
impossible to evaluate.
WHY: Large average wins can more than offset frequent small losses.


[FILL] Combining win rate with average [___] gives the honest picture.
R
color
volume
WHY: Frequency (win rate) plus size (average R) together tell the full story.

[TF] A 40% win rate can still be profitable if the average win in R is large enough.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Large average wins can outweigh more frequent, smaller losses.

NODE 3
TITLE: What Is Expectancy?
CELEBRATION: You understand expectancy!

INTRO
TITLE: Average Result Per Trade
TEXT: Expectancy is the <b>average result per trade</b>, measured in R, over many trades. A positive expectancy means the average trade gains R over the long run.

[MC] Expectancy is the...
average result per trade (in R) over many trades.
result of a single trade.
account balance.
WHY: Expectancy is the long-run average result per trade.

[TF] A positive expectancy means the average trade, over many trades, gains R.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Positive expectancy = the average trade comes out ahead in R over the long run.

[FILL] Expectancy is the average result per trade measured in [___].
R
dollars only
candles
WHY: Expressed in R, expectancy compares across position sizes.

[PICK] Why is expectancy a long-run idea?
CONCERN: Reading it correctly
Because it's an average that only holds over many trades, not any single one | detail: Long-run average
Because it predicts the next trade exactly | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: It says nothing certain about one trade.
Because it ignores wins and losses | detail: Backwards | wrong: It's built from wins and losses.
WHY: Expectancy is an average over many trades, not a promise about any single one.


[FILL] Expectancy is the average [___] per trade, in R, over many trades.
result
guaranteed
random
WHY: Expectancy is a long-run average, measured in R.

[TF] A positive expectancy means the average trade, over many trades, gains R.
ANSWER: True
WHY: That's the definition of positive expectancy.

NODE 4
TITLE: Winning With Under 50% Wins
CELEBRATION: You understand how low win rates can still win!

INTRO
TITLE: Big Wins, Small Losses
TEXT: A system can be profitable with a win rate <b>below 50%</b> if its winners are much larger (in R) than its losers. Size can outweigh frequency.

[MC] A system with a 35% win rate can still be profitable if...
its winners are much larger in R than its losers.
it never loses.
its win rate is secretly 90%.
WHY: Large winners in R can outweigh more frequent, smaller losers.

[TF] You must win more than half your trades to be profitable.
ANSWER: False
WHY: With large enough winners in R, a sub-50% win rate can still profit.

[FILL] A low win rate can still win if the [___] are much larger in R.
winners
losers
fees
WHY: Larger winners in R can more than offset frequent small losses.

[SPOT] Someone dismisses a system purely because its win rate is under 50%, without checking the average R.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Win rate alone isn't enough, a sub-50% system with large winners in R can still have positive expectancy.
Under 50% always means unprofitable.
Average R never matters.
WHY: Both win rate and average R matter; ignoring R misjudges the system.


[FILL] A system can be profitable with a win rate below [___]%.
50
100
0
WHY: Sub-50% win rates can still be profitable if the average winner in R is large enough.

[TF] You must win more than half your trades to be profitable.
ANSWER: False
WHY: With large enough winners in R, a sub-50% win rate can still profit overall.

NODE 5
TITLE: Sample Size Matters
CELEBRATION: You understand sample size!

INTRO
TITLE: A Few Trades Prove Little
TEXT: Expectancy and win rate only mean something over a <b>large enough sample</b>. A handful of trades can look great or terrible purely by chance.

[MC] Expectancy is meaningful only over...
a large enough sample of trades.
a single trade.
two trades.
WHY: Averages need many trades to be meaningful; small samples are noisy.

[TF] Three winning trades in a row prove a system has positive expectancy.
ANSWER: False
WHY: A tiny sample can happen by chance; it doesn't prove long-run expectancy.

[FILL] A small [___] of trades can look great or terrible purely by chance.
sample
color
gap
WHY: Small samples are dominated by luck, not the true average.

[PICK] Why be careful with a small sample?
CONCERN: Reading results honestly
Because chance can make a few trades misleading in either direction | detail: Small samples are noisy
Because small samples are always accurate | detail: Backwards | wrong: They're the least reliable.
Because sample size never matters | detail: Wrong | wrong: It's central to trusting an average.
WHY: Small samples are dominated by luck, so they can badly mislead.


[FILL] Expectancy is meaningful only over a [___] sample of trades.
large
tiny
single
WHY: A large sample is needed for averages like expectancy to be meaningful.

[TF] Three winning trades in a row proves a system has positive expectancy.
ANSWER: False
WHY: A tiny sample can happen by chance; it doesn't prove long-run expectancy.

NODE 6
TITLE: Expectancy & Edge Review
CELEBRATION: You've mastered expectancy & edge!

INTRO
TITLE: Bringing It Together
TEXT: A final review of win rate, R, expectancy, and sample size.

[MATCH] Match each term to its meaning.
Win rate => How often trades win
Average R => How much trades win or lose, in R
Expectancy => The average result per trade over many trades
WHY: A recap of the unit's core ideas.

[MC] What two things must you combine to judge a system honestly?
Win rate and average R.
Candle color and time of day.
Account size only.
WHY: Frequency (win rate) plus size (average R) gives the honest picture.

[FILL2] A system can profit with a [1] win rate if its winners are [2] in R.
BLANK1: *low | perfect | 100%
BLANK2: *large | tiny | equal
WHY: Large winners in R can offset a lower win rate.

[PICK] Which best summarizes this unit?
CONCERN: Reviewing the big picture
Judge a system by expectancy, win rate and R together, over a large sample | detail: The central theme
Judge a system by win rate alone | detail: Incomplete | wrong: Win rate alone can mislead.
A few trades prove everything | detail: Wrong | wrong: Sample size matters.
WHY: Expectancy (win rate × R over many trades) is the honest measure.

[SPOT] Someone judges a brand-new system as "amazing" after two lucky wins.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Two trades is far too small a sample to judge expectancy. That's chance, not proof.
Two wins prove a positive expectancy.
Sample size is irrelevant.
WHY: Expectancy needs a large sample; a couple of wins prove nothing.


[FILL] This review ties together win rate, average R, expectancy, and [___].
sample size
random guesses
company names
WHY: All four core ideas from this unit are reviewed together here.

[TF] Judging a system honestly requires combining win rate and average R over a large sample.
ANSWER: True
WHY: This has been the throughline, combine frequency and size, over enough trades.

UNIT 29 — Market Condition Classification
NODE 1
TITLE: Trending, Ranging, or Volatile?
CELEBRATION: You can classify market conditions!

INTRO
TITLE: Three Broad Conditions
TEXT: Markets are commonly classified into broad conditions: <b>trending</b> (directional), <b>ranging</b> (sideways between levels), and <b>volatile</b> (large, erratic swings). Reading the condition guides how you read everything else.
CHART: uptrend

trending.
ranging.
volatile.
