ranging.
trending.
volatile.
CHART: range
WHY: Sideways between support and resistance is a ranging condition.

[MC] The chart above shows large, erratic swings with no clear direction. This condition is...
volatile.
trending.
ranging.
CHART: volatile
WHY: Big, erratic swings with no clear direction is a volatile condition.

[MATCH] Match each condition to its description.
Trending => Directional progress one way
Ranging => Sideways between levels
Volatile => Large, erratic swings
WHY: These three broad conditions describe most markets.


[FILL] Markets are commonly classified as trending, ranging, or [___].
volatile
guaranteed
random
WHY: Trending, ranging, and volatile are the three broad commonly cited conditions.

[TF] Reading the market condition first frames how you read everything else.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Condition is commonly treated as the context for reading levels, patterns, and candles.

downtrend.

TARGET: 3
WHY: The 3rd candle is the bounce that made a lower high, confirming the downtrend.

[TRENDLINE] Drag to connect the lower highs that confirm this trending condition.
CHART: lowerHighsDowntrend
TARGET_START: 1
TARGET_END: 3
WHY: Candles 1 and 3 mark the falling sequence of lower highs that define a trending condition here.

[FILL] A trending condition has a clear [___] in one direction.
progression
absence
random walk
WHY: Directional progression is what makes it a trend.

progression
absence
randomness
WHY: Directional progression (higher highs/lows or lower highs/lows) defines trending.

volatile.

TARGET_LO: 1
TARGET_HI: 6

CHART: range
ANSWER: False
WHY: Ranging is sideways, the opposite of directional progress.

sideways

straight down
WHY: Sideways bounded movement is a range.


[FILL] A ranging condition moves [___] between two levels.
sideways
straight up
straight down
WHY: Sideways movement between support and resistance defines a range.

[TF] A ranging market makes strong directional progress.
ANSWER: False
WHY: A range is sideways, the opposite of directional progress.

CHART: volatile

[MC] The chart above shows large swings both ways with no clean direction. It is...
volatile.
a tidy uptrend.
a quiet range.
WHY: Big erratic two-way swings is a volatile condition.

WHY: Volatility means bigger, faster moves than a calm, tight range.

[FILL] Volatile conditions show large, [___] swings in both directions.
erratic
tiny

Volatile and calm ranges are the same.
Swing size doesn't matter.
WHY: Volatile conditions have far larger, more erratic swings than a tidy range.


[FILL] A volatile condition shows large, [___] swings.
erratic
tiny
flat
WHY: Large, erratic two-way swings define a volatile condition.

[TF] Volatile conditions have larger, faster swings than a calm range.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Volatility means bigger, faster moves than a calm, tight range.

[MC] Why classify the market condition first?
Because it frames how you read levels, patterns, and candles.
Because it changes the candle colors.
Because it has no effect on anything.
WHY: The condition sets the context for every other read.

[FILL] The market condition [___] how you read everything else.
frames
ignores
replaces
WHY: Condition is the context that frames the rest of the read.

It's the context you read first, before levels and patterns | detail: Context first
It's irrelevant to reading a chart | detail: Backwards | wrong: It's central context.

WHY: Condition is the first-read context that frames every other read.

frames
ignores
replaces

UNIT 30 — Moving Averages
NODE 1
TITLE: What Is a Moving Average?
CELEBRATION: You understand moving averages!

INTRO
TITLE: The Average of Recent Prices
TEXT: A moving average (MA) is the <b>average of the last N prices</b>, recalculated each candle. A 20-period MA averages the last 20 closes. It's a smoothed line that follows price.

[MC] A moving average is the...
average of the last N prices, updated each candle.
single highest price ever.
account balance.
WHY: An MA averages the most recent N prices and updates as new ones arrive.

[FILL] A 20-period moving average averages the last [___] prices.
20
2
200
WHY: The number is how many recent prices it averages.

[TF] A moving average updates as each new candle arrives.
ANSWER: True
WHY: It's "moving" because it recalculates with each new price.

[PICK] What does a moving average give you?
CONCERN: Understanding the tool
A smoothed line summarizing recent price
A guarantee of the next price
The exact future value
WHY: An MA smooths recent price into a line; it doesn't predict the future.


[FILL] A moving average is the average of the last [___] prices, updated each candle.
N
one
zero
WHY: An MA averages a set number (N) of recent prices, recalculating with each new one.

[TF] A moving average predicts future prices with certainty.
ANSWER: False
WHY: An MA summarizes recent price into a smoothed line. It doesn't guarantee the future.

NODE 2
TITLE: Smoothing Out Noise
CELEBRATION: You understand smoothing!

INTRO
TITLE: Less Jitter, Clearer Direction
TEXT: Because it averages, a moving average <b>smooths</b> out the candle-to-candle jitter, making the broader direction easier to see.

[MC] Averaging recent prices makes a moving average...
smoother than the raw candles.
more jagged than the candles.
unrelated to price.
WHY: Averaging smooths the noise, so the line is steadier than raw price.

[FILL] A moving average [___] out short-term noise.
smooths
amplifies
removes forever
WHY: Averaging reduces the jitter, smoothing the line.

[TF] A longer moving average is generally smoother than a shorter one.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Averaging more prices smooths more, so longer MAs are steadier.

[FILL] Smoothing makes the broader [___] easier to see.
direction
color
volume
WHY: With less noise, the general direction is clearer.


[FILL] Averaging recent prices makes a moving average [___] than raw candles.
smoother
more jagged
identical
WHY: Averaging reduces candle-to-candle jitter, producing a smoother line.

[TF] A longer moving average is generally smoother than a shorter one.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Averaging more prices smooths more, making longer MAs steadier.

NODE 3
TITLE: The Slope Reflects Trend
CELEBRATION: You can read an MA's slope!

INTRO
TITLE: Rising, Falling, or Flat
TEXT: A moving average's <b>slope</b> reflects the trend: a rising MA reflects an uptrend, a falling MA a downtrend, and a flat MA a range. It's a smoothed read of direction.
CHART: uptrend

[MC] On a rising market like the chart above, a moving average would be...
sloping up.
sloping down.
perfectly flat.
WHY: In an uptrend, the average of recent prices rises, the MA slopes up.

[TF] A falling moving average reflects a downtrend.
CHART: downtrend
ANSWER: True
WHY: When recent prices fall, their average falls, the MA slopes down.

[MC] A flat moving average most likely reflects a...
range.
strong uptrend.
strong downtrend.
CHART: range
WHY: Sideways price keeps the average roughly flat, a range.

[FILL] A moving average's [___] reflects the trend direction.
slope
color
width
WHY: The slope (up/down/flat) mirrors the trend.


[FILL] A moving average's slope reflects the [___].
trend
color
volume
WHY: Rising, falling, or flat slope mirrors the underlying trend direction.

[TF] A flat moving average most likely reflects a strong trend.
ANSWER: False
WHY: A flat MA more likely reflects a range, not a strong trend.

NODE 4
TITLE: Price Above vs. Below the MA
CELEBRATION: You can read price against the MA!

INTRO
TITLE: Which Side Is Price On?
TEXT: Price trading <b>above</b> a rising MA is commonly described as strength; <b>below</b> a falling MA as weakness. It's a descriptive read, not a signal to act.
CHART: priceAboveMA

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the candle where price crosses above the MA line.
CHART: priceAboveMA
TARGET: 4
WHY: The 4th candle is where price closes back above the MA, the crossing point.

[MC] Price holding above a rising moving average is commonly described as...
relative strength.
relative weakness.
a guaranteed reversal.
WHY: Staying above a rising MA is commonly read as strength, descriptively.

[TF] Price below a falling MA is commonly described as relative weakness.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Trading under a falling average is commonly read as weakness.

[FILL] Price vs the MA is a [___] read, not a signal to act.
descriptive
guaranteed
random
WHY: It describes strength/weakness; it isn't an automatic buy/sell.

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the candle where price crosses below the MA line.
CHART: priceBelowMA
TARGET: 4
WHY: The 4th candle is where price closes back below the MA, the crossing point.

[SPOT] Someone treats "price above the MA" as an automatic, guaranteed buy signal.
CHART: priceAboveMA
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Price above the MA is a descriptive strength read, not a guaranteed signal to act on.
It's always an automatic buy.
The MA guarantees the next move.
WHY: An MA read is descriptive context, never a guaranteed signal.


[FILL] Price holding above a rising MA is commonly described as relative [___].
strength
weakness
randomness
WHY: Staying above a rising MA is commonly read as strength.

[TF] Price vs the MA is a guaranteed signal to act on.
ANSWER: False
WHY: It's a descriptive strength/weakness read, not a guaranteed signal.

NODE 5
TITLE: Short vs. Long MAs
CELEBRATION: You understand fast vs slow MAs!

INTRO
TITLE: Fast and Slow
TEXT: A <b>short</b> MA (few periods) reacts quickly to price. It's "fast." A <b>long</b> MA (many periods) reacts slowly. It's "smooth and slow." Each shows a different scale of trend.

[MC] A short-period MA is...
fast. It reacts quickly to price.
slow and very smooth.
unrelated to price.
WHY: Fewer periods means quicker reaction, a "fast" MA.

[MC] A long-period MA is...
slow and smooth.
the fastest to react.
identical to a short MA.
WHY: More periods means slower reaction, a "slow," smooth MA.

[FILL2] A short MA is [1]; a long MA is [2].
BLANK1: *fast | slow | flat
BLANK2: *slow | fast | jagged
WHY: Short = fast reaction; long = slow, smooth.

[TF] A short MA hugs price more closely than a long MA.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Reacting faster, the short MA tracks price more tightly.


[FILL] A short-period MA is [___]. It reacts quickly to price.
fast
slow
unrelated
WHY: Fewer periods means quicker reaction, making it a "fast" MA.

[TF] A long-period MA hugs price more closely than a short-period MA.
ANSWER: False
WHY: A SHORT MA hugs price more closely; a long MA is smoother and slower.

NODE 6
TITLE: MA Crossovers
CELEBRATION: You understand MA crossovers!

INTRO
TITLE: When Fast Crosses Slow
TEXT: A "crossover" is when a fast MA crosses a slow MA. It's commonly used as a descriptive trend cue. But MAs are <b>lagging</b> (built from past prices), so crossovers are not guaranteed signals.
CHART: maCrossUp

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the candle where the fast MA crosses above the slow MA.
CHART: maCrossUp
TARGET: 4
WHY: The 4th candle is where the fast (blue) MA crosses above the slow (amber) MA, a bullish crossover.

[MC] An MA crossover is when...
a fast MA crosses a slow MA.
price makes a new high.
a candle gaps.
WHY: A crossover is the fast and slow MAs crossing each other.

[TF] Moving averages are lagging, since they're built from past prices.
ANSWER: True
WHY: An MA is an average of past prices, so it lags current price.

[FILL] Because MAs are built from past prices, they are [___].
lagging
leading
predictive
WHY: Averaging past prices makes MAs lag, not lead.

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the candle where the fast MA crosses below the slow MA.
CHART: maCrossDown
TARGET: 4
WHY: The 4th candle is where the fast (blue) MA crosses below the slow (amber) MA, a bearish crossover.

[PICK] How should a crossover be read?
CHART: maCrossUp
CONCERN: Reading it honestly
As a descriptive, lagging trend cue, not a guaranteed signal | detail: Lagging context
As a guaranteed entry | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Crossovers lag and can whipsaw.
As a leading predictor of price | detail: Backwards | wrong: MAs lag; they don't lead.
WHY: A crossover is a lagging, descriptive cue, never a guarantee.


[FILL] Moving averages are [___] because they're built from past prices.
lagging
leading
predictive
WHY: Averaging past prices makes MAs lag current price, rather than lead it.

[TF] An MA crossover is a guaranteed trading signal.
ANSWER: False
WHY: A crossover is a descriptive, lagging cue, not a guarantee.

NODE 7
TITLE: Moving Averages Review
CELEBRATION: You've mastered moving averages!

INTRO
TITLE: Bringing It Together
TEXT: A final review of what an MA is, smoothing, slope, price-vs-MA, and crossovers.

[MATCH] Match each idea to its meaning.
Moving average => The average of the last N prices
Slope => Reflects the trend direction
Crossover => Fast MA crossing slow MA (a lagging cue)
WHY: A recap of the unit's core ideas.

[MC] What does an MA's slope reflect?
The trend direction.
The account balance.
The candle color.
WHY: Slope up/down/flat mirrors the trend.

[FILL2] A moving average [1] price and [2] the current price (it's lagging).
BLANK1: *smooths | predicts | ignores
BLANK2: *lags | leads | equals
WHY: MAs smooth and lag; they don't predict.

[PICK] Which best summarizes moving averages?
CHART: uptrend
CONCERN: Reviewing the big picture
A smoothed, lagging line whose slope reads the trend, descriptive, not predictive | detail: The central theme
A predictor of the next price | detail: Overstates | wrong: MAs lag, they don't predict.
Unrelated to price | detail: Wrong | wrong: An MA is built directly from price.
WHY: Smoothed, lagging, descriptive trend reading is the throughline.


[FILL] This review ties together what an MA is, smoothing, slope, and [___].
crossovers
random guesses
company names
WHY: All the core ideas from this unit are reviewed together here.

[TF] Moving averages are descriptive and lagging, not predictive.
ANSWER: True
WHY: This has been the throughline, smoothed, lagging, descriptive trend reading.

UNIT 31 — Oscillators: RSI & MACD
NODE 1
TITLE: What Is an Oscillator?
CELEBRATION: You understand oscillators!

INTRO
TITLE: A Bounded, Derived Measure
TEXT: An oscillator is an indicator <b>derived from price</b> that moves within a bounded range. It summarizes something about price (like the speed of moves) rather than showing price itself.

[MC] An oscillator is...
a price-derived measure that moves in a bounded range.
the raw price line.
the account balance.
WHY: Oscillators are derived measures bounded within a range (e.g., 0–100).

[FILL] An oscillator is [___] from price, not the price itself.
derived
independent
random
WHY: Oscillators are calculated from price data.

[TF] An oscillator shows the raw price directly.
ANSWER: False
WHY: It shows a derived measure about price, not the price line itself.

[FILL] An oscillator moves within a [___] range.
bounded
infinite
negative-only
WHY: Oscillators are bounded (e.g., 0–100 for RSI).


[FILL] An oscillator is [___] from price and moves within a bounded range.
derived
independent
random
WHY: Oscillators are calculated from price data, bounded within a fixed range.

[TF] An oscillator shows the raw price directly.
ANSWER: False
WHY: It shows a derived measure about price, not the price line itself.

NODE 2
TITLE: What RSI Measures
CELEBRATION: You understand RSI!

INTRO
TITLE: Speed and Size of Moves
TEXT: RSI (Relative Strength Index) is an oscillator from <b>0 to 100</b> that measures the speed and size of recent price moves. Higher RSI = stronger recent up-moves; lower = stronger down-moves.

[MC] RSI measures the...
speed and size of recent price moves, on a 0–100 scale.
account balance.
number of candles.
WHY: RSI summarizes recent up vs down move strength from 0 to 100.

[FILL] RSI is bounded between 0 and [___].
100
50
10
WHY: RSI runs from 0 to 100.

[TF] A higher RSI reflects stronger recent up-moves.
ANSWER: True
WHY: RSI rises when recent gains dominate recent losses.

[MC] RSI is an example of a(n)...
oscillator (bounded, derived measure).
raw price line.
support level.
WHY: RSI is a bounded, price-derived oscillator.


[FILL] RSI runs from [___] to 100.
0
-100
50
WHY: RSI is bounded between 0 and 100.

[TF] A higher RSI reflects stronger recent up-moves.
ANSWER: True
WHY: RSI rises when recent gains dominate recent losses.

NODE 3
TITLE: Overbought & Oversold Zones
CELEBRATION: You can read RSI zones honestly!

INTRO
TITLE: Labels, Not Signals
TEXT: RSI above ~70 is commonly labeled "overbought" and below ~30 "oversold." These are <b>descriptive labels</b>, not buy/sell signals, a strong trend can stay "overbought" for a long time.

[MC] RSI above ~70 is commonly labeled...
overbought.
oversold.
neutral guaranteed.
WHY: High RSI (~70+) is commonly labeled overbought, a description.

[TF] "Overbought" is a guaranteed sell signal.
ANSWER: False
WHY: It's a descriptive label; a strong uptrend can stay overbought for a long time.

[FILL] "Overbought" and "oversold" are descriptive [___], not signals to act.
labels
guarantees
trades
WHY: They describe RSI's zone; they aren't automatic trade signals.

[SPOT] Someone shorts every time RSI hits "overbought," sure it must reverse.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
"Overbought" is a label, not a signal, strong trends can stay overbought a long time.
Overbought always means an immediate reversal.
RSI zones are guaranteed signals.
WHY: Zones describe RSI; they don't guarantee a reversal.


[FILL] "Overbought" and "oversold" are descriptive [___], not signals.
labels
guarantees
trades
WHY: These are labels describing an RSI zone, not automatic trade signals.

[TF] "Overbought" is a guaranteed sell signal.
ANSWER: False
WHY: A strong uptrend can stay overbought a long time. It's a label, not a guarantee.

NODE 4
TITLE: What MACD Shows
CELEBRATION: You understand MACD!

INTRO
TITLE: Two Moving Averages' Relationship
TEXT: MACD is built from the <b>relationship between two moving averages</b> (a fast and a slow one). It summarizes momentum, how the fast MA is pulling away from or converging with the slow one.

[MC] MACD is derived from...
the relationship between two moving averages.
the account balance.
the candle color.
WHY: MACD tracks how a fast and slow MA relate, a momentum read.

[TF] MACD is built from moving averages, so it is also lagging.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Because it's built from MAs (past prices), MACD lags too.

[FILL] MACD summarizes [___] from two moving averages.
momentum
volume
account size
WHY: The gap/relationship between the MAs reads as momentum.

[MC] Since MACD comes from moving averages, it is...
lagging (derived from past prices).
a leading predictor.
unrelated to price.
WHY: MA-based indicators lag price.


[FILL] MACD tracks the relationship between two [___] averages.
moving
random
fixed
WHY: MACD is built from a fast and slow moving average's relationship.

[TF] Because MACD is built from moving averages, it is also lagging.
ANSWER: True
WHY: MA-based indicators lag price, and MACD is no exception.

NODE 5
TITLE: Divergence
CELEBRATION: You understand divergence!

INTRO
TITLE: When Price and Indicator Disagree
TEXT: Divergence is when price and an oscillator move in <b>different directions</b>, e.g., price makes a higher high but RSI makes a lower high. It's a descriptive "disagreement," not a guaranteed reversal.

[MC] Divergence is when price and the indicator...
move in different directions.
move in perfect lockstep.
are the same thing.
WHY: Divergence is a disagreement between price and the oscillator.

[TF] Divergence guarantees a reversal.
ANSWER: False
WHY: It's a descriptive disagreement, a clue, never a guarantee.

[FILL] Divergence is price and an oscillator [___] on direction.
disagreeing
agreeing
identical
WHY: The two disagreeing on direction is divergence.

[PICK] How should divergence be read?
CONCERN: Reading it honestly
As a descriptive disagreement worth noting, not a guarantee | detail: A clue
As a guaranteed reversal | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Divergence can persist without a reversal.
As proof the data is broken | detail: Wrong | wrong: It's a normal, valid reading.
WHY: Divergence is a descriptive clue, not a guaranteed signal.


[FILL] Divergence is price and an oscillator [___] on direction.
disagreeing
agreeing
identical
WHY: Divergence specifically describes price and the oscillator moving in different directions.

[TF] Divergence guarantees a reversal.
ANSWER: False
WHY: It's a descriptive disagreement, a clue, never a guarantee.

NODE 6
TITLE: Oscillators Review
CELEBRATION: You've mastered oscillators!

INTRO
TITLE: Bringing It Together
TEXT: A final review of oscillators, RSI, its zones, MACD, and divergence.

[MATCH] Match each to its meaning.
RSI => A 0–100 oscillator of recent move strength
Overbought/oversold => Descriptive RSI labels, not signals
MACD => A momentum read from two moving averages
WHY: A recap of the unit's core ideas.

[MC] What do RSI and MACD have in common?
Both are indicators derived from price (and both lag it).
Both are the raw price line.
Both guarantee reversals.
WHY: Both are derived, lagging indicators, not guarantees.

[FILL2] Oscillators are [1] from price and their extreme zones are [2], not signals.
BLANK1: *derived | independent | random
BLANK2: *labels | guarantees | trades
WHY: Derived measures with descriptive zones, not automatic signals.

[PICK] Which best summarizes this unit?
CONCERN: Reviewing the big picture
Oscillators derive momentum reads from price; their zones and divergence are descriptive, not guarantees | detail: The central theme
Oscillators are guaranteed signal generators | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: They describe, they don't guarantee.
Oscillators lead price | detail: Backwards | wrong: They're derived and lagging.
WHY: Descriptive, derived, lagging, never guaranteed, is the throughline.


[FILL] This review ties together oscillators, RSI, its zones, MACD, and [___].
divergence
random guesses
company names
WHY: All the core ideas from this unit are reviewed together here.

[TF] RSI and MACD are both derived, lagging indicators, not guarantees.

UNIT 32 — Trade Planning & Process
NODE 1
TITLE: What Is a Trade Plan?
CELEBRATION: You understand trade plans!

INTRO
TITLE: Decide Before, Not During
TEXT: A trade plan decides the key things <b>in advance</b>: the idea (why), the invalidation (where you're wrong), the target (planned exit), and the size (risk). Planning ahead keeps decisions off the emotional moment.

[MC] A trade plan is best decided...
in advance, before entering.
in the heat of the moment.
after the trade closes.
WHY: Planning ahead keeps decisions out of the emotional moment.

[MATCH] Match each part of a plan to what it defines.
Idea => Why the trade makes sense
Invalidation => Where the idea is proven wrong
Target => The planned exit
WHY: Idea, invalidation, target (and size) are the core of a plan.

[FILL] A plan decides the key things [___] entering.
before
after
without
WHY: The point of a plan is deciding in advance.

[TF] A trade plan includes where the idea would be proven wrong.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Invalidation, where you're wrong, is a core part of a plan.


[FILL] A trade plan decides the key things [___] entering.
before
after
without
WHY: Planning ahead keeps decisions out of the emotional moment.

[TF] A trade plan includes where the idea would be proven wrong.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Invalidation is a core part of a plan.

NODE 2
TITLE: Invalidation
CELEBRATION: You understand invalidation!

INTRO
TITLE: Where the Idea Is Wrong
TEXT: Invalidation is the price/level where your trade idea is <b>proven wrong</b>. It's what a stop is commonly based on, not an arbitrary number.

[MC] Invalidation is the point where...
the trade idea is proven wrong.
the trade is guaranteed to win.
the market closes.
WHY: Invalidation marks where the reasoning no longer holds.

[TF] A stop is commonly based on where the idea is invalidated, not a random number.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Tying the stop to invalidation gives it a clear, reasoned basis.

[FILL] Invalidation is where the trade idea is [___].
wrong
guaranteed
doubled
WHY: It's the level that disproves the idea.

[SPOT] Someone enters a trade with no idea of where they'd be wrong.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Without an invalidation point, there's no clear place to stop or admit the idea failed.
Invalidation is unnecessary.
A trade never needs a wrong-point.
WHY: A defined invalidation is what makes risk manageable and the idea testable.


[FILL] Invalidation is where the trade idea is proven [___].
wrong
guaranteed
doubled
WHY: Invalidation marks where the reasoning no longer holds.

[TF] A stop is commonly based on where the idea is invalidated, not a random number.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Tying the stop to invalidation gives it a clear, reasoned basis.

NODE 3
TITLE: Process Over Prediction
CELEBRATION: You understand process over prediction!

INTRO
TITLE: You Control the Process, Not the Outcome
TEXT: You can't control whether one trade wins, only whether you <b>followed your process</b>. Good process with a bad outcome is still good process; the edge shows over many trades.

[MC] In trading, you directly control...
your process (following the plan).
the outcome of any single trade.
the market's direction.
WHY: You control the process; outcomes vary trade to trade.

[TF] A good process can still produce a losing trade.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Any single outcome is uncertain; process is judged over many trades.

[FILL] You control the [___], not the outcome of one trade.
process
result
market
WHY: Process is the controllable part; outcomes vary.

[PICK] Why focus on process over outcome?
CONCERN: Reading results honestly
Because the edge shows over many trades, not any single one | detail: Long-run thinking
Because outcomes are fully controllable | detail: Wrong | wrong: You can't control a single result.
Because process never matters | detail: Backwards | wrong: Process is the controllable edge.
WHY: Process is what you control; the edge appears over a large sample.


[FILL] You control the [___], not the outcome of any single trade.
process
result
market
WHY: Process is the controllable part; outcomes vary trade to trade.

[TF] A good process can still produce a losing trade.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Any single outcome is uncertain; process is judged over many trades.

NODE 4
TITLE: A Pre-Trade Checklist
CELEBRATION: You can build a pre-trade checklist!

INTRO
TITLE: Same Questions Every Time
TEXT: A checklist asks the same questions before every trade: what's the <b>condition</b>, is there a <b>level</b>, is there a <b>signal</b>, and what's the <b>risk</b>? Consistency comes from repeating the process.

[ORDER] A simple pre-trade checklist order:
Classify the market condition
Identify the key level
Look for a signal at that level
Define the risk (invalidation and size)
WHY: Condition, level, signal, risk, a repeatable pre-trade sequence.

[MC] The point of a checklist is to...
ask the same questions consistently every time.
guarantee every trade wins.
replace having a plan.
WHY: Consistency, not a guarantee, is the value of a checklist.

[FILL] A checklist brings [___] to the pre-trade process.
consistency
randomness
luck
WHY: Repeating the same checks each time builds consistency.

[TF] A checklist guarantees profitable trades.
ANSWER: False
WHY: It brings consistency to the process; it can't guarantee outcomes.


[FILL] A pre-trade checklist brings [___] to the process.
consistency
randomness
luck
WHY: Repeating the same checks each time builds consistency.

[TF] A checklist guarantees profitable trades.
ANSWER: False
WHY: It brings consistency; it can't guarantee outcomes.

NODE 5
TITLE: Sticking to the Plan
CELEBRATION: You understand plan discipline!

INTRO
TITLE: Follow It, Even When Tempted
TEXT: A plan only helps if you <b>follow it</b>. Moving your stop, skipping your checklist, or abandoning the idea mid-trade undoes the plan's whole purpose.

[MC] A trade plan only helps if you...
actually follow it.
change it mid-trade whenever tempted.
never write it down.
WHY: The plan's value comes from following it consistently.

[TF] Widening your stop mid-trade to avoid a loss undermines the plan.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Moving the stop breaks the invalidation the plan was built on.

[FILL] A plan helps only if you [___] it.
follow
ignore
rewrite constantly
WHY: Following the plan is what gives it value.

[SPOT] Someone hits their planned invalidation but moves the stop further away to avoid taking the loss.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Moving the stop past invalidation abandons the plan and lets a small planned loss grow.
Moving the stop is following the plan.
Invalidation is optional once you're in.
WHY: Honoring invalidation is core discipline; moving it undoes the plan.


[FILL] A trade plan only helps if you [___] it.
follow
ignore
rewrite constantly
WHY: Following the plan is what gives it value.

[TF] Widening your stop mid-trade to avoid a loss undermines the plan.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Moving the stop breaks the invalidation the plan was built on.

NODE 6
TITLE: Trade Planning Review
CELEBRATION: You've mastered trade planning & process!

INTRO
TITLE: Bringing It Together
TEXT: A final review of plans, invalidation, process, checklists, and discipline.

[MATCH] Match each to its meaning.
Trade plan => Idea, invalidation, target, and size decided in advance
Process over prediction => Controlling the process, not the outcome
Discipline => Actually following the plan
WHY: A recap of the unit's core ideas.

[MC] What's the core of "process over prediction"?
You control the process; outcomes vary over many trades.
You control every outcome.
The plan doesn't matter.
WHY: Process is controllable; the edge shows over a large sample.

[FILL2] A plan defines things in [1], and it only helps if you [2] it.
BLANK1: *advance | hindsight | secret
BLANK2: *follow | ignore | hide
WHY: Plan ahead, then follow it.

[PICK] Which best summarizes this unit?
CONCERN: Reviewing the big picture
Plan the idea, invalidation, target, and size in advance, then follow the process | detail: The central theme
Predict each outcome and control it | detail: Overstates control | wrong: You can't control single outcomes.
Wing it and hope | detail: No method | wrong: The unit is about a repeatable process.
WHY: Plan-in-advance plus disciplined process is the throughline.


[FILL] This review ties together plans, invalidation, process, and [___].
discipline
random guesses
company names
WHY: All the core ideas from this unit are reviewed together here.

[TF] Plan-in-advance plus disciplined follow-through is the throughline of this unit.
ANSWER: True

UNIT 33 — VWAP & Bollinger Bands
NODE 1
TITLE: What Is VWAP?
CELEBRATION: You understand VWAP!

INTRO
TITLE: The Volume-Weighted Average
TEXT: VWAP is the <b>Volume-Weighted Average Price</b>, the average price over a session, weighted by how much volume traded at each price. It's commonly used intraday as a reference "fair value" line.

[MC] VWAP stands for...
Volume-Weighted Average Price.
Very Wide Average Price.
Variable Weekly Average Price.
WHY: VWAP is the volume-weighted average price over a session.

[FILL] VWAP weights each price by its [___].
volume
color
timeframe
WHY: More volume at a price gives it more weight in the average.

[TF] VWAP is commonly used as an intraday reference for average price.
ANSWER: True
WHY: VWAP is a common intraday "fair value" reference line.

[MC] How does VWAP differ from a simple moving average?
It weights prices by volume, not just by time.
It ignores price entirely.
It only uses the highest price.
WHY: VWAP weights by volume; a simple MA weights each period equally.


[FILL] VWAP stands for Volume-[___] Average Price.
Weighted
Wide
Weekly
WHY: VWAP weights each price by the volume traded there.

[TF] VWAP is commonly used as an intraday reference for average price.
ANSWER: True
WHY: VWAP is a common intraday "fair value" reference line.

NODE 2
TITLE: Reading Price vs. VWAP
CELEBRATION: You can read price against VWAP!

INTRO
TITLE: Above or Below Fair Value
TEXT: Price trading <b>above</b> VWAP is commonly described as trading above the session's average (relative strength); <b>below</b> as relative weakness. It's descriptive context, not a signal.
CHART: priceAboveVWAP

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the candle where price crosses above VWAP.
CHART: priceAboveVWAP
TARGET: 4
WHY: The 4th candle is where price closes back above VWAP, the crossing point.

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the candle where price crosses below VWAP.
CHART: priceBelowVWAP
TARGET: 4
WHY: The 4th candle is where price closes back below VWAP, the crossing point.

[MC] Price above VWAP is commonly described as...
trading above the session average (relative strength).
guaranteed to fall.
unrelated to VWAP.
WHY: Above the volume-weighted average is commonly read as relative strength.

[TF] Price below VWAP is commonly described as relative weakness.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Below the session's average price reads as relative weakness.

[FILL] Price vs VWAP is a [___] read, not a signal to act.
descriptive
guaranteed
random
WHY: It describes strength/weakness; it isn't an automatic trade.

[SPOT] Someone treats "price above VWAP" as an automatic guaranteed buy.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Above VWAP is a descriptive strength read, not a guaranteed signal to buy.
It's always an automatic buy.
VWAP guarantees the next move.
WHY: VWAP context is descriptive, never a guaranteed signal.


[FILL] Price above VWAP is commonly described as relative [___].
strength
weakness
randomness
WHY: Trading above the session's volume-weighted average reads as relative strength.

[TF] Price below VWAP is commonly described as relative weakness.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Below the session average reads as relative weakness.

NODE 3
TITLE: What Are Bollinger Bands?
CELEBRATION: You understand Bollinger Bands!

INTRO
TITLE: A Band Around a Moving Average
TEXT: Bollinger Bands draw a <b>moving average</b> with an upper and lower band set a number of <b>standard deviations</b> away. The bands measure how far price is straying from its average.

[MC] Bollinger Bands are built around a...
moving average, with bands set by standard deviation.
single fixed price.
volume total.
WHY: The middle is an MA; the bands are standard deviations from it.

[FILL] The Bollinger band width is based on [___] deviation.
standard
average
random
WHY: The bands are placed a number of standard deviations from the MA.

[TF] The middle Bollinger line is a moving average.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Bollinger Bands center on a moving average, with bands around it.

[MC] The bands measure how far price is straying from its...
average.
opening price only.
all-time high.
WHY: The bands quantify deviation from the moving-average center.


[FILL] Bollinger Bands are built around a moving [___].
average
volume total
random line
WHY: The middle line is a moving average, with bands set by standard deviation.

[TF] The middle Bollinger line is a moving average.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Bollinger Bands center on a moving average, with bands around it.

NODE 4
TITLE: Bands Widen and Narrow
CELEBRATION: You can read band width!

INTRO
TITLE: Width Reflects Volatility
TEXT: When volatility rises, the bands <b>widen</b>; when it's calm, they <b>narrow</b>. Band width is a quick read of how volatile price is right now.
CHART: bandsWidening

[MC] Wide Bollinger Bands reflect...
CHART: bandsWidening
high volatility.
low volatility.
no price movement.
WHY: Bigger deviations (volatility) push the bands wider, as shown widening left to right above.

[MC] Narrow ("squeezing") Bollinger Bands reflect...
CHART: bandsNarrowing
low volatility.
high volatility.
a guaranteed breakout.
WHY: Small deviations (calm) pull the bands narrow, as shown squeezing left to right above.

[TF] Band width is a quick read of current volatility.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Width tracks how far price is deviating, i.e., volatility.

[FILL2] Bands [1] when volatile and [2] when calm.
BLANK1: *widen | narrow | vanish
BLANK2: *narrow | widen | double
WHY: Volatility widens the bands; calm narrows them.


[FILL] Bollinger Bands [___] when volatility rises.
widen
narrow
disappear
WHY: Bigger price deviations (volatility) push the bands wider.

[TF] Narrow ("squeezing") Bollinger Bands reflect low volatility.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Small deviations (calm markets) pull the bands narrow.

NODE 5
TITLE: Price at the Bands
CELEBRATION: You can read price at the bands honestly!

INTRO
TITLE: Touching a Band Isn't a Signal
TEXT: Price touching the upper or lower band shows it's stretched far from its average. But in a strong trend, price can "ride" a band for a long time. It's descriptive, not a reversal signal.
CHART: touchUpperBand

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the candle that stretches out to touch the upper band.
CHART: touchUpperBand
TARGET: 5
WHY: The 5th candle is where price stretches out to touch the upper band.

[TAPCANDLE] Tap the candle that stretches out to touch the lower band.
CHART: touchLowerBand
TARGET: 5
WHY: The 5th candle is where price stretches out to touch the lower band.

[MC] Price touching the upper band means price is...
CHART: touchUpperBand
stretched far above its average.
guaranteed to reverse down.
below its average.
WHY: An upper-band touch means price is far above the moving-average center.

[TF] A band touch is a guaranteed reversal signal.
ANSWER: False
WHY: In a strong trend, price can ride a band for a long time, no guarantee.

[FILL] A band touch shows price is [___] from its average, not a guaranteed reversal.
stretched
identical
detached
WHY: It describes stretch/deviation, not an automatic turn.

[SPOT] Someone sells every time price touches the upper band, sure it must revert.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Price can ride the upper band in a strong trend, a touch isn't a guaranteed reversal.
An upper-band touch always reverses.
Band touches are guaranteed signals.
WHY: Band touches are descriptive stretch reads, not guaranteed reversals.


[FILL] A band touch shows price is [___] from its average, not a guaranteed reversal.
stretched
identical
detached
WHY: It describes stretch/deviation, not an automatic turn.

[TF] A band touch is a guaranteed reversal signal.
ANSWER: False
WHY: In a strong trend, price can ride a band for a long time, no guarantee.

UNIT 34 — Drawdowns & Losing Streaks
NODE 1
TITLE: What Is a Drawdown?
CELEBRATION: You understand drawdowns!

INTRO
TITLE: The Drop From a Peak
TEXT: A drawdown is the percentage decline from an account's peak value to a later low. Every account has drawdowns. They're a normal part of the equity curve.

[MC] A drawdown measures the decline from an account's...
peak value to a later low.
starting value only.
single best trade.
WHY: A drawdown is the drop from a prior peak to a subsequent low.

[FILL] A drawdown is measured as a [___] decline from the peak.
percentage
dollar-only
guaranteed
WHY: Drawdowns are commonly expressed as a percent from peak.

[TF] Every account experiences drawdowns at some point.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Drawdowns are a normal part of any equity curve.

[MC] A 20% drawdown means the account is down 20% from its...
prior peak.
starting deposit.
best single day.
WHY: Drawdown is measured from the prior peak value.


[FILL] A drawdown measures percent decline from an account's [___].
peak
starting deposit
best trade
WHY: Drawdown specifically measures decline from the prior peak value.

[TF] Every account experiences drawdowns at some point.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Drawdowns are a normal part of any equity curve.

NODE 2
TITLE: Recovery Math
CELEBRATION: You understand recovery math!

INTRO
TITLE: Losses Need Bigger Gains Back
TEXT: A key fact: a loss needs a <b>larger</b> percentage gain to recover. A 50% loss needs a 100% gain to break even. This is why limiting drawdowns matters.

[MC] A 50% loss requires what gain to break even?
100%.
50%.
25%.
WHY: Halving your account means you must double what's left, a 100% gain.

[MC] A 20% loss requires roughly what gain to break even?
25%.
20%.
10%.
WHY: From 80% of the start, you need +25% (0.80 × 1.25 = 1.00).

[TF] A loss needs a larger percentage gain than the loss itself to recover.
ANSWER: True
WHY: You're gaining on a smaller base, so the required percent is larger.

[FILL] The bigger the drawdown, the [___] the gain needed to recover.
larger
smaller
same
WHY: Deeper losses require disproportionately larger recovery gains.

[SPOT] Someone assumes a 50% loss just needs a 50% gain to get back.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
A 50% loss needs a 100% gain to recover, since you're rebuilding from a smaller base.
50% back always fixes a 50% loss.
Recovery math doesn't apply.
WHY: Recovering a loss requires a larger percent gain than the loss itself.


[FILL] A 50% loss requires a [___]% gain to recover.
100
50
25
WHY: Halving your account means you must double what's left to break even.

[TF] A loss needs a larger percentage gain than the loss itself to recover.
ANSWER: True
WHY: You're gaining on a smaller base, so the required percent is larger.

NODE 3
TITLE: Losing Streaks Are Normal
CELEBRATION: You understand losing streaks!

INTRO
TITLE: Even a Good System Loses in a Row
TEXT: A string of losses in a row happens even with a positive-expectancy system, purely by chance. A losing streak alone doesn't prove the system is broken.

[MC] A losing streak with a sound system is...
normal. It can happen by chance.
proof the system is broken.
impossible.
WHY: Even a good system sees losing streaks by chance.

[TF] A few losses in a row proves a system has negative expectancy.
ANSWER: False
WHY: Short streaks happen by chance; they don't prove the long-run expectancy.

[FILL] A losing streak alone doesn't prove a system is [___].
broken
profitable
random
WHY: Streaks are normal variance, not proof of a broken system.

[PICK] Why don't losing streaks prove a system is broken?
CONCERN: Reading variance honestly
Because streaks happen by chance even with positive expectancy | detail: Normal variance
Because systems never lose | detail: Wrong | wrong: All systems have losing trades.
Because expectancy doesn't exist | detail: Backwards | wrong: Expectancy is the long-run measure.
WHY: Losing streaks are normal variance, not proof about the long-run edge.


[FILL] A losing streak with a sound system is [___]. It can happen by chance.
normal
impossible
proof of failure
WHY: Even a good system sees losing streaks by chance.

[TF] A few losses in a row proves a system has negative expectancy.
ANSWER: False
WHY: Short streaks happen by chance; they don't prove the long-run expectancy.

NODE 4
TITLE: Streaks and Sample Size
CELEBRATION: You understand streaks and chance!

INTRO
TITLE: Short Runs Are Noisy
TEXT: Over a <b>large sample</b>, expectancy shows through. But short runs are noisy. Even a fair coin flips several heads (or tails) in a row sometimes.

[MC] Short runs of results are...
noisy, dominated by chance.
perfectly reliable.
proof of the long-run average.
WHY: Small samples are dominated by luck, not the true average.

[TF] Even a positive-expectancy system can have a rough short stretch.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Variance means good systems still have losing stretches short-term.

[FILL] Expectancy shows through only over a [___] sample.
large
tiny
single
WHY: Averages need many trades to be reliable.

[FILL] Short runs are dominated by [___], not the true average.
chance
skill only
the plan
WHY: Small samples reflect luck more than the underlying edge.


[FILL] Short runs of results are [___], dominated by chance.
noisy
perfectly reliable
guaranteed
WHY: Small samples are dominated by luck, not the true average.

[TF] Even a positive-expectancy system can have a rough short stretch.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Variance means good systems still have losing stretches short-term.

NODE 5
TITLE: Capital Preservation Mindset
CELEBRATION: You understand capital preservation!

INTRO
TITLE: Protect the Base
TEXT: Because losses need bigger gains back and streaks happen, keeping drawdowns small protects the account. A preserved account can keep participating; a blown-up one can't.

[MC] Keeping drawdowns small matters because...
recovery gets much harder as losses deepen.
it guarantees profits.
it removes all risk.
WHY: Deep drawdowns need disproportionate gains to recover, so limiting them helps.

[TF] A preserved account can keep participating; a blown-up one cannot.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Without capital, there's nothing left to trade with.

[FILL] Capital preservation keeps [___] small to protect the account.
drawdowns
targets
timeframes
WHY: Limiting drawdowns is the core of capital preservation.

[PICK] Why is capital preservation foundational?
CONCERN: Long-run survival
Because staying in the game long-term requires not blowing up | detail: Survival first
Because it guarantees returns | detail: Overstates | wrong: It protects, it doesn't guarantee gains.
Because losses don't matter | detail: Backwards | wrong: Losses are exactly what it manages.
WHY: Preserving capital keeps you able to participate over the long run.


[FILL] Capital preservation keeps [___] small to protect the account.
drawdowns
targets
timeframes
WHY: Limiting drawdowns is the core of capital preservation.

[TF] A preserved account can keep participating; a blown-up one cannot.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Without capital, there's nothing left to trade with.

100%.
50%.
150%.
WHY: Doubling the remaining half is a 100% gain.

TEXT: A trading journal records what you did and why, so you can <b>review and improve</b>. Memory is unreliable; a written record is honest.

[MC] The main purpose of a trading journal is to...
record trades so you can review and improve.
guarantee future profits.
replace having a plan.
WHY: Journaling creates a record to learn from, not a profit guarantee.

WHY: Memory is biased and incomplete; a written journal is more honest.

review

WHY: Reviewing a written record is how you improve.

Memory is biased; a written record is honest and complete | detail: Honest data
Because memory is perfect | detail: Wrong | wrong: Memory is unreliable.
Because journaling guarantees profit | detail: Overstates | wrong: It aids learning, not a guarantee.
WHY: An honest written record beats biased memory for improvement.

why
guaranteed profit
random guesses

Plan => Entry, invalidation, target, size

WHY: Setup, plan, execution, and outcome make a complete entry.

compare trades of different sizes fairly.
guarantee the next trade.
ignore the plan.
WHY: R normalizes results across position sizes for fair comparison.

[FILL] A good entry logs whether you actually [___] your plan.
followed
hid

WHY: Logging execution (did you follow the plan?) is key to reviewing process.

ANSWER: True

outcome
color
volume

did I follow my process?

Only winners are worth reviewing.
Process doesn't matter in review.
WHY: The point of review is the process, which you control, not just outcomes.


[FILL] The most useful review question is: did I follow my [___]?
process
luck
mood

review
guess
quit

UNIT 36 — Average True Range (ATR)
NODE 1
TITLE: What Is ATR?
CELEBRATION: You understand what ATR measures!

INTRO
TITLE: A Volatility Yardstick
TEXT: ATR (Average True Range) is an indicator that measures the average size of price movement over a recent period. It's expressed in price, not as a bounded 0-100 oscillator.
CHART: volatile

[MC] ATR measures...
The average size of price movement over a recent period.
The exact direction price will move next.
The company's total trading volume.
WHY: ATR is a volatility measure based on the average size of recent price movement.

[TF] ATR is expressed in price terms, not as a bounded 0-100 scale.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Unlike RSI, ATR is expressed in the asset's own price units.

[FILL] ATR measures the average [___] of price movement.
size
direction
color
WHY: ATR is specifically about size (range) of movement, not direction.

[MATCH] Match the term to its focus.
ATR => Average size of price movement
RSI => Speed/strength of recent moves, bounded 0-100
Volatility => The broader concept ATR is one way of measuring
WHY: ATR is one specific, price-based way of quantifying volatility.

[PICK] Which best describes what ATR tells you?
CHART: volatile
CONCERN: Understanding the tool
How large price moves have typically been recently, in price terms | detail: A volatility yardstick
The exact next price target | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: ATR describes typical move size, not a target.
Whether price is overbought or oversold | detail: Different concept | wrong: That's closer to RSI's role, not ATR's.
WHY: ATR is a descriptive measure of typical recent move size, not a price target or overbought/oversold read.

[SPOT] Someone treats ATR as a signal for market direction.
CHART: volatile
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
ATR measures size of movement, not direction.
ATR always points up when volatility rises.
ATR predicts the next candle's color.
WHY: ATR describes magnitude of movement, not which way price is heading.

NODE 2
TITLE: Higher ATR vs. Lower ATR
CELEBRATION: You can compare ATR readings!

INTRO
TITLE: Bigger Swings, Smaller Swings
TEXT: A higher ATR reading means recent price swings have been larger; a lower ATR means they've been smaller and calmer.

[MC] A rising ATR reading commonly reflects...
Larger recent price swings.
A guaranteed reversal.
Lower trading volume.
WHY: Rising ATR reflects bigger recent price movement, nothing about direction or volume specifically.

[TF] A falling ATR reading reflects calmer, smaller recent price swings.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Lower ATR commonly reflects reduced volatility.

[FILL] Comparing ATR over time helps gauge whether volatility is [___] or shrinking.
expanding
guaranteed
random
WHY: ATR trends up when volatility expands and down when it contracts.

[MATCH] Match the ATR reading to its meaning.
Rising ATR => Volatility expanding
Falling ATR => Volatility contracting
Flat ATR => Volatility staying roughly steady
WHY: ATR's direction over time reflects whether typical move size is growing or shrinking.

[PICK] Why compare current ATR to its recent history rather than a fixed number?
CONCERN: Reading it relatively
Because "high" or "low" ATR is relative to that asset's own typical range | detail: A relative judgment, like volume
Because ATR has one universal threshold for every asset | detail: Incorrect | wrong: Typical ATR varies by asset, just like volume.
Because ATR never changes over time | detail: Incorrect | wrong: ATR moves as volatility expands and contracts.
WHY: Like volume, ATR is judged relative to what's typical for that specific asset.

[SPOT] Someone assumes a specific ATR number is universally "high" for every single asset.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
ATR is judged relative to that asset's own typical range, not one fixed universal number.
Every asset shares the identical ATR scale.
ATR values never vary between assets.
WHY: ATR readings are relative to each asset's own typical volatility.

NODE 3
TITLE: ATR and Position Sizing
CELEBRATION: You understand how ATR connects to position sizing!

INTRO
TITLE: Sizing to Typical Movement
TEXT: Some traders use ATR to help gauge a reasonable stop distance, sizing positions around how much an asset typically moves rather than an arbitrary number.

[MC] How might ATR be used alongside position sizing?
To help gauge a reasonable stop distance based on typical movement.
To guarantee a winning trade.
To replace the need for any stop-loss at all.
WHY: ATR can inform how far a stop might reasonably need to sit given typical movement, connecting back to the Position Sizing unit.

[TF] A wider ATR (more typical movement) might call for a wider stop distance to avoid being caught by normal noise.
ANSWER: True
WHY: A stop set too tight relative to typical movement risks getting triggered by ordinary volatility.

[FILL] Using ATR for stops means basing distance on typical [___], not an arbitrary number.
movement
color
volume
WHY: ATR-based stops are grounded in the asset's own typical price movement.

[MATCH] Match the concept to how it connects to ATR.
Stop-loss placement => Can be informed by typical movement (ATR)
Position sizing => Still derived from dollar risk and stop distance
Both => Connect back to the Risk Management and Position Sizing units
WHY: ATR is one more input into the same risk-management process covered earlier in the course.

[PICK] Why might a stop based on ATR make sense?
CONCERN: Connecting risk concepts
It's sized to typical movement, reducing the chance normal noise triggers it | detail: Grounded in actual volatility
It guarantees the stop is never hit | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: No stop placement guarantees anything.
It removes the need for position sizing entirely | detail: Incorrect | wrong: Sizing is still calculated from risk and stop distance.
WHY: ATR-informed stops are grounded in typical movement, not a guarantee against being stopped out.

[SPOT] Someone sets a stop far tighter than the asset's typical ATR and is surprised it gets hit by normal noise.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
A stop set well inside typical movement (ATR) can get triggered by ordinary volatility, not a meaningful shift.
Stop distance should never consider typical movement.
ATR has no relationship to stop placement.
WHY: This connects back to the earlier Stop-Loss Placement Logic lesson — a stop too tight for normal movement risks getting hit by noise.

TITLE: Trending, Ranging, and ATR
TEXT: ATR commonly rises during volatile or strongly trending conditions and commonly falls during quiet ranges or consolidation.
CHART: range

[MC] ATR commonly does what during a tight, quiet range?
Falls, reflecting smaller typical movement.
Rises sharply.
Has no relationship to market condition.
WHY: Quiet, range-bound conditions commonly show lower ATR readings.

[TF] ATR commonly rises during volatile or strongly trending conditions.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Bigger typical moves during volatile/trending conditions commonly push ATR higher.

[FILL] ATR connects back to the Market Condition Classification unit by [___] typical movement size.
quantifying
guessing
ignoring
WHY: ATR gives a numeric way to describe how much price typically moves in a given condition.

[MATCH] Match the market condition to its commonly cited ATR behavior.
Range-bound => Lower ATR
Volatile/trending => Higher ATR
ATR itself => A way to quantify this difference numerically
WHY: ATR gives numeric texture to the trending/ranging/volatile classifications from earlier in the course.

[PICK] How does ATR add to market condition classification?
CHART: range
CONCERN: Connecting the concepts
It gives a numeric read on how much price is typically moving, reinforcing the classification | detail: Confluence with condition
It replaces classifying trending vs. ranging entirely | detail: Overstates it | wrong: ATR adds to, not replaces, that classification.
It has no relationship to market condition | detail: Incorrect | wrong: ATR commonly shifts with condition.
WHY: ATR is one more piece of confluence alongside the condition classification skill from Advanced.

CHART: range

ATR never changes regardless of condition.
Market condition has no bearing on ATR.
WHY: ATR moves with the underlying volatility of the current condition.

WHY: That's the core, consistent idea across this whole unit.

[FILL] ATR is judged [___] to what's typical for that specific asset.
relative
absolute
random

It can inform a reasonable stop distance based on typical movement.
It guarantees a specific trade outcome.
It has no relationship to stops or sizing.
WHY: ATR is commonly used as one input into stop placement, connecting back to earlier Risk Management lessons.

[MATCH] Match the term to its one-line meaning.
ATR => Average size of recent price movement

UNIT 37 — Relative Volume (RVOL)
NODE 1
TITLE: What Is Relative Volume?
CELEBRATION: You understand what RVOL measures!

INTRO
TITLE: Volume Compared to Normal
TEXT: Relative Volume (RVOL) compares today's volume to that same stock's typical volume at this point in the day. It answers "is this unusually active, or a normal day?"

[MC] RVOL compares today's volume to...
That stock's own typical volume at this point in the day.
The volume of every other stock in the market.
The stock's all-time high volume ever recorded.
WHY: RVOL is a same-stock, same-time-of-day comparison, not a cross-market ranking.

[TF] RVOL is about comparing a stock's volume to its own typical pattern, not to other stocks.
ANSWER: True
WHY: RVOL is stock-specific and time-of-day-specific.

[FILL] RVOL helps answer whether today's volume is [___] for this stock.
unusual
guaranteed
random
WHY: RVOL flags whether current activity is normal or standout compared to that stock's own history.

[MATCH] Match the term to its focus.
RVOL => Comparing current volume to typical volume, same stock
Raw volume => Total shares traded, no comparison built in
High RVOL => Unusually active relative to normal
WHY: RVOL adds context that raw volume alone doesn't provide.

[PICK] Why might RVOL matter more than raw share volume alone?
CONCERN: Reading volume with context
Because raw volume alone doesn't say whether that's unusual for this particular stock | detail: Context matters
Because raw volume is always identical across all stocks | detail: Incorrect | wrong: Typical volume varies enormously by stock.
Because RVOL replaces the need to look at price at all | detail: Incorrect | wrong: RVOL is one added piece of context, not a replacement for price.
WHY: A million shares might be huge for one stock and tiny for another — RVOL adds that missing context.

[SPOT] Someone sees "2 million shares traded" and assumes that's automatically a big deal for every stock.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Whether 2 million shares is unusual depends on that stock's own typical volume, which RVOL captures.
Raw share count means the same thing for every stock.
Volume numbers never need any context.
WHY: Without comparing to typical volume, a raw number alone doesn't reveal whether it's unusual.

NODE 2
TITLE: Reading an RVOL Reading
CELEBRATION: You can interpret RVOL readings!

INTRO
TITLE: Above and Below "Normal"
TEXT: An RVOL reading is often expressed as a multiple of normal, for example "2x" meaning twice the typical volume for this time of day.

[MC] An RVOL reading of "3x" commonly means...
Volume is roughly three times this stock's typical level.
Price has moved three points.
The stock has three times as many shares outstanding.
WHY: RVOL multiples describe volume relative to typical, not price or share count.

[TF] An RVOL below 1x commonly reflects quieter-than-usual volume.
ANSWER: True
WHY: A reading under 1x means volume is running below what's typical for that time.

[FILL] An RVOL reading is often expressed as a [___] of normal volume.
multiple
percentage of price
guarantee
WHY: RVOL is commonly shown as a multiple (like 2x or 0.5x) of typical volume.

[MATCH] Match the RVOL reading to its meaning.
Above 1x => Busier than typical
Around 1x => Roughly typical
Below 1x => Quieter than typical
WHY: The 1x baseline represents this stock's own normal volume for that time of day.

[PICK] Why compare RVOL to 1x rather than to a fixed universal number?
CONCERN: Reading it relatively
Because 1x represents this specific stock's own typical volume as the baseline | detail: A relative benchmark
Because 1x is a fixed number that means the same thing for every stock | detail: Incorrect | wrong: 1x is relative to each stock's own typical volume.
Because RVOL has no baseline at all | detail: Incorrect | wrong: 1x serves as the "typical" baseline.
WHY: The 1x baseline is specific to each stock, which is what makes RVOL a relative measure.

[SPOT] Someone assumes an RVOL of "1.5x" means the same absolute share count for every stock.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
1.5x is relative to that stock's own typical volume, which differs stock to stock.
1.5x always equals exactly the same number of shares everywhere.
RVOL has no connection to that stock's own history.
WHY: The multiple is anchored to each stock's own typical volume, not a shared absolute number.

NODE 3
TITLE: RVOL and News or Catalysts
CELEBRATION: You can connect RVOL to catalysts!

INTRO
TITLE: A Clue, Not a Signal
TEXT: Unusually high RVOL sometimes coincides with news, earnings, or other catalysts, since more participants may be trading around the event. It's descriptive context, not a trade signal.

[MC] Elevated RVOL alongside news commonly reflects...
More participants than usual trading around that event.
A guaranteed price direction.
Confirmation that the news is accurate.
WHY: Elevated RVOL is a participation clue, not a guarantee of direction or of the news itself.

[TF] High RVOL around a catalyst is context, not a buy or sell signal.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Like every indicator in this course, RVOL describes conditions rather than prescribing action.

[FILL] Elevated RVOL near news commonly reflects more [___] than usual.
participation
certainty
profit
WHY: More shares changing hands reflects more participation, not certainty of outcome.

[MATCH] Match the scenario to what it commonly reflects.
High RVOL, earnings day => More participants trading around the event
Low RVOL, quiet day => Fewer participants, less activity
High RVOL alone => Unusual activity worth noting as context
WHY: RVOL by itself describes activity level, and pairing it with a known catalyst adds context.

[PICK] How should elevated RVOL near a catalyst be treated?
CONCERN: Avoiding overreach
As one piece of descriptive context about participation, not a signal | detail: Descriptive framing
As a guarantee that price will move in a specific direction | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: RVOL says nothing about direction.
As proof the news itself is true | detail: Unrelated claim | wrong: Volume doesn't verify news accuracy.
WHY: RVOL adds context about participation levels, never a directional or truth guarantee.

[SPOT] Someone sees high RVOL on earnings day and treats it as a guaranteed breakout signal.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
High RVOL reflects participation, not a guaranteed direction or outcome.
RVOL always predicts a breakout on earnings days.
Earnings always cause identical RVOL patterns.
WHY: RVOL is descriptive context about activity level, never a predictive signal.

NODE 4
TITLE: RVOL Across the Trading Day
CELEBRATION: You understand how RVOL shifts intraday!

INTRO
TITLE: Time-of-Day Matters
TEXT: Because RVOL compares to typical volume at that specific time of day, a stock's expected volume in the first ten minutes differs from its expected volume midday, connecting back to the Trading Sessions unit.

[MC] Why does RVOL account for time of day rather than the whole day at once?
Because typical volume itself varies by time of day, like the open being busier than midday.
Because volume is identical every minute of the day.
Because RVOL only applies after the market closes.
WHY: This connects back to earlier lessons on how volume and activity vary across the trading session.

[TF] Typical volume in the opening minutes commonly differs from typical volume at midday.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Sessions differ in typical activity level, which is why RVOL compares like-for-like time windows.

[FILL] RVOL compares current volume to what's typical for that same [___] of day.
time
color
price
WHY: The comparison window matters since typical volume itself shifts through the session.

[MATCH] Match the session to a commonly cited RVOL consideration.
Opening minutes => Typical volume is commonly higher
Midday lull => Typical volume is commonly lower
RVOL calculation => Accounts for this time-of-day baseline
WHY: This ties RVOL directly back to the Trading Sessions & News unit's session-activity concepts.

[PICK] Why does time-of-day matter for reading RVOL correctly?
CONCERN: Applying the concept accurately
Because comparing to the wrong time window could make normal activity look unusual, or vice versa | detail: Like-for-like comparison
Because RVOL ignores time of day entirely | detail: Incorrect | wrong: RVOL's baseline is specifically time-of-day aware.
Because every session has identical typical volume | detail: Incorrect | wrong: Sessions commonly differ in typical activity.
WHY: A meaningful RVOL reading depends on comparing against the right same-time-of-day baseline.

[SPOT] Someone compares midday volume directly to the opening-minutes baseline and calls it "quiet."
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Comparing across mismatched time-of-day baselines can make normal midday volume look artificially low.
Volume is identical at every point in the trading day.
RVOL has no relationship to time of day.
WHY: RVOL's comparison should use the typical baseline for that same time window, not a different one.

UNIT 38 — Opening Range
NODE 1
TITLE: What Is the Opening Range?
CELEBRATION: You understand what the opening range is!

INTRO
TITLE: The First Few Minutes
TEXT: The opening range refers to the high and low price established during a defined early window after the market opens, commonly the first five, fifteen, or thirty minutes.
CHART: volatile

[MC] The opening range is defined by...
The high and low price during a set early window after the open.
The closing price from the prior day.
The average price over the entire prior week.
WHY: The opening range is specifically about the high/low formed in an early defined window, not prior-day or weekly data.

[TF] The opening range window is commonly the first five, fifteen, or thirty minutes of the session.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Different traders use different defined windows, but these are common choices.

[FILL] The opening range is the high and low formed during a [___] early window.
defined
random
year-long
WHY: The window is a specific, defined stretch of time, not an arbitrary or extended one.

[MATCH] Match the term to its focus.
Opening range => High/low during an early defined window
Opening range high => The top of that early window's range
Opening range low => The bottom of that early window's range
WHY: The opening range establishes a reference high and low from the earliest minutes of trading.

[PICK] Why might traders pay attention to the opening range?
CHART: volatile
CONCERN: Understanding the tool
It's an early reference level formed from the session's first activity | detail: An early reference point
It guarantees the day's exact high and low | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: The opening range is an early reference, not a guarantee of the day's extremes.
It only matters for the last hour of trading | detail: Incorrect | wrong: The opening range is defined by early, not late, price action.
WHY: The opening range gives an early reference level built from the session's first few minutes.

[SPOT] Someone assumes the opening range guarantees the day's final high and low.
CHART: volatile
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
The opening range is an early reference level, not a guarantee of the full day's eventual high or low.
The opening range always matches the day's final high and low exactly.
The opening range has no relationship to the rest of the day.
WHY: Price can move well beyond the opening range as the day continues.

TITLE: Trading Above or Below the Range
TEXT: When price moves above the opening range high or below the opening range low, that's commonly described as breaking out of the opening range.
CHART: breakout

[MC] Price closing above the opening range high is commonly described as...
Breaking out of the opening range to the upside.
Staying inside the opening range.
Breaking down through support from last week.
WHY: Moving beyond the opening range high is the defining description of an upside break of that range.

[TF] Price can also break below the opening range low, described as breaking down out of the range.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Breaks can occur in either direction relative to the established range.

[FILL] Moving beyond the opening range boundary is commonly called [___] the range.
breaking
inventing
erasing
WHY: This is simply the descriptive term for price moving past the established opening range boundary.

[MATCH] Match the scenario to its description.
Price above range high => Breaking out to the upside
Price below range low => Breaking down
Price between the two => Still inside the opening range
WHY: These are the three basic descriptive states relative to the opening range.

[PICK] What does an opening range break describe, on its own?
CHART: breakout
CONCERN: Staying descriptive
That price has moved beyond an early reference level, nothing more by itself | detail: A descriptive observation
A guaranteed continuation for the rest of the day | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: A break describes a boundary crossing, not a guaranteed continuation.
Confirmation that a reversal is now impossible | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Price can reverse after any break.
WHY: An opening range break is a descriptive observation about crossing an early reference level, not a guarantee of what follows.

CHART: breakout
QUESTION: What is the mistake?

Every opening range break continues in the same direction all day.
Price never reverses after breaking the opening range.
WHY: Like any breakout, a range break can continue, stall, or reverse afterward.

CHART: breakoutRetest

[MC] Price briefly moving above the opening range high and then falling back inside is commonly described as...
A false break of the opening range.
A guaranteed reversal signal.
Proof the opening range was calculated incorrectly.

[TF] A false break of the opening range echoes the false breakout concept covered in the Breakouts & Retests unit.
ANSWER: True

false
guaranteed

WHY: This mirrors the "false breakout" terminology already established earlier in the course.

Opening range itself => The specific reference level being tested
WHY: The opening range is simply one more level that can be broken, held, or falsely broken.

[PICK] Why is it useful to recognize a false break of the opening range?
CHART: breakoutRetest

Because it reinforces that any break, including this one, can fail to hold | detail: Consistent with breakout concepts
Because false breaks of the opening range never happen | detail: Incorrect | wrong: They can and do occur, same as any other breakout.
Because it means the opening range concept doesn't apply here | detail: Incorrect | wrong: The opening range concept still applies, it just didn't hold.

Opening range breaks are a completely different phenomenon from regular breakouts.
False breaks are impossible once price crosses the opening range boundary.
WHY: The opening range is one more level subject to the same false-breakout dynamics taught earlier.

More volatile early trading.
A guaranteed downtrend.

[TF] A narrower opening range commonly reflects calmer early trading.

WHY: A tight range in the early minutes reflects smaller early price swings.

ATR
company earnings

QUESTION: What is the mistake?

Opening range width has no connection to volatility at all.

UNIT 39 — Sector & Index Strength
NODE 1
TITLE: Stocks Don't Move Alone
CELEBRATION: You understand sector and index context!

INTRO
TITLE: The Bigger Picture
TEXT: Individual stocks commonly move alongside their sector (a group of related companies) and the broader market indexes, rather than in complete isolation.

[MC] Sector and index context describes...
How a stock's movement commonly relates to its sector and the broader market.
A guaranteed prediction of a stock's next move.
A rule that stocks never share any common movement.
WHY: Sector and index awareness is about relative context, not a prediction rule.

[TF] Individual stocks commonly move somewhat alongside their sector and the broader market.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Shared macro and sector-wide forces commonly influence related stocks together.

[FILL] A sector is a group of companies in the same [___] of business.
type
country
color
WHY: A sector groups companies by the type of business they're in, such as technology or energy.

[MATCH] Match the term to its focus.
Sector => A group of related companies by business type
Index => A broader gauge tracking many stocks together
Individual stock => One company, often influenced by both of the above
WHY: These three layers, stock, sector, and index, are related but distinct levels of context.

[PICK] Why consider sector and index context alongside an individual stock?
CONCERN: Adding context
Because shared forces commonly move related stocks together, adding useful context | detail: Broader context
Because every stock always moves in perfect lockstep with its sector | detail: Overstates it | wrong: Correlation is common but not exact or guaranteed.
Because sector and index context replaces looking at the individual stock | detail: Incorrect | wrong: It's added context, not a replacement.

NODE 2
TITLE: Relative Strength and Weakness
CELEBRATION: You can describe relative strength and weakness!

INTRO
TITLE: Outperforming or Lagging
TEXT: When a stock moves more than its sector or index in the same direction, it's commonly described as showing relative strength; moving less, or opposite, is commonly described as relative weakness.

[MC] A stock rising more than its sector on an up day is commonly described as showing...
Relative strength.
Relative weakness.
A guaranteed reversal.
WHY: Outperforming its peer group to the upside is the definition of relative strength in this context.

[TF] A stock falling more than its sector on a down day, or rising less than its sector on an up day, is commonly described as relative weakness.
ANSWER: True
WHY: Underperforming the sector, in either direction, fits the relative weakness description.

[FILL] Moving more than the sector/index in the same direction is commonly called relative [___].
strength
weakness
volume
WHY: This is simply the descriptive term used for outperformance versus a peer group.

[MATCH] Match the scenario to its description.
Stock up more than sector => Relative strength
Stock down more than sector => Relative weakness
Stock moving in line with sector => Neither notably strong nor weak
WHY: Relative strength/weakness describes divergence from the sector's typical movement.

[PICK] What does relative strength describe, on its own?
CONCERN: Staying descriptive
That a stock is outperforming its sector or index, nothing more by itself | detail: A descriptive comparison

WHY: Relative strength is a descriptive comparison of price movement, not a guarantee or fundamentals claim.

WHY: Like any descriptive read, relative strength can shift from one period to the next.

UNIT 40 — Level II & Order Book Awareness
NODE 1
TITLE: What Is Level II?
CELEBRATION: You understand what Level II data shows!

INTRO
TITLE: Beyond the Last Price
TEXT: Level II data displays a list of current buy orders (bids) and sell orders (asks) at different price levels, sometimes called the order book, rather than just the single last traded price.

[MC] Level II data displays...
A list of current bid and ask orders at different price levels.
Only the single most recent traded price.
A company's quarterly earnings report.
WHY: Level II is specifically about the order book, current bids and asks, not a single last-price figure or fundamentals report.

[TF] Level II data is sometimes called the order book.
ANSWER: True
WHY: "Order book" and "Level II" commonly refer to the same underlying concept.

[FILL] Level II shows current buy and sell [___] at different price levels.
orders
earnings
dividends
WHY: Level II is fundamentally a list of orders, not financial statements.

[MATCH] Match the term to its focus.
Bid => A current buy order, and its price
Ask => A current sell order, and its price
Level II => The full list of bids and asks at various levels
WHY: Level II aggregates many individual bid and ask orders into one view.

[PICK] What does Level II data add beyond a simple last-price quote?
CONCERN: Understanding the tool
Visibility into current buy and sell orders waiting at various price levels | detail: Order book depth
A guarantee of exactly what price comes next | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Level II shows current orders, not a guaranteed next price.
A complete history of the company's past decade of earnings | detail: Unrelated | wrong: Level II is about current orders, not historical financials.
WHY: Level II adds a view of standing orders that a simple last-price quote doesn't show.

[SPOT] Someone assumes Level II data tells you exactly what price will print next.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
Level II shows current standing orders, it doesn't guarantee the exact next traded price.
Level II always predicts the very next trade price precisely.
Level II has no relationship to bids and asks at all.
WHY: Level II is a descriptive snapshot of current orders, not a guaranteed prediction of the next trade.

NODE 2
TITLE: Bids, Asks, and the Spread
CELEBRATION: You understand bids, asks, and the spread!

INTRO
TITLE: The Gap Between Buyers and Sellers
TEXT: The bid is the highest price a buyer is currently willing to pay; the ask is the lowest price a seller is currently willing to accept. The gap between them is called the spread.

[MC] The bid represents...
The highest price a buyer is currently willing to pay.
The lowest price a seller is currently willing to accept.
The average price over the past year.
WHY: The bid and ask represent opposite sides of the current order book, buyers versus sellers.

[TF] The spread is the gap between the current bid and ask price.
ANSWER: True
WHY: This is the standard definition of the bid-ask spread.

[FILL] The ask is the lowest price a [___] is currently willing to accept.
seller
buyer
regulator
WHY: The ask specifically reflects the seller side of the order book.

[MATCH] Match the term to its definition.
Bid => Highest price a buyer will currently pay
Ask => Lowest price a seller will currently accept
Spread => The gap between the bid and ask
WHY: These three terms together describe the basic structure of the order book at any moment.

[PICK] Why might the size of the spread be worth noting descriptively?
CONCERN: Reading the order book
A wider or narrower spread can reflect differing levels of current trading activity | detail: Descriptive context
Because the spread guarantees a specific profit on every trade | detail: Overstates it | wrong: Spread size doesn't guarantee any profit outcome.
Because the spread never changes throughout the day | detail: Incorrect | wrong: Spreads can widen or narrow as conditions change.
WHY: Spread width is simply one more descriptive detail about current market conditions, not a profit guarantee.

[SPOT] Someone assumes the bid-ask spread guarantees a certain profit if crossed.
QUESTION: What is the mistake?
The spread describes the current gap between buy and sell orders, it doesn't guarantee any profit.
Crossing the spread always guarantees a profitable outcome.
The spread has no relationship to buyers and sellers.
WHY: The spread is a descriptive snapshot of order book conditions, not a profit guarantee.

How many shares are represented by orders waiting at each price level.
The company's total shares outstanding.
A guaranteed direction for the next price move.
WHY: Depth is specifically about the size of orders waiting at various levels, not company-wide share counts or predictions.

ANSWER: True
WHY: More depth simply means a larger number of shares represented in waiting orders at that level.
