interest
guarantee
history
WHY: Depth reflects current interest represented by resting orders, not a guarantee of future action.

[MATCH] Match the term to its focus.
Depth => Size of orders waiting at each price level
Bid depth => Resting buy orders at various price levels
Ask depth => Resting sell orders at various price levels
WHY: Depth can be considered separately on the buy side and the sell side of the book.

As a descriptive snapshot of current resting orders, which can change at any moment | detail: A snapshot, not a guarantee
As a fixed, permanent measure that never changes | detail: Incorrect | wrong: Resting orders can be added, canceled, or filled at any time.
As proof of exactly what will happen to price next | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Depth describes current orders, not a guaranteed outcome.

Orders in the book can never be canceled once placed.
Order book depth has no relationship to buying or selling interest.
WHY: The order book is a live, constantly shifting snapshot, not a fixed structure.

TITLE: A Snapshot, Not a Crystal Ball
TEXT: Level II shows a real-time snapshot of orders, but those orders can be added, changed, or canceled at any moment, and Level II alone doesn't reveal the intentions behind them.

Displayed orders can be added, changed, or canceled at any moment.
It always accurately predicts the next ten trades.
It replaces the need for any other form of analysis.
WHY: The order book is a live, changeable snapshot, not a locked-in prediction of future trades.

ANSWER: True
WHY: An order's presence in the book doesn't explain the trader's underlying strategy or intent.

snapshot
guarantee
prophecy
WHY: This is the core, consistent framing for order book data throughout this unit.

Orders can vanish => Resting orders may be canceled before they're filled
Intentions are hidden => The book doesn't reveal why an order was placed
Level II overall => A descriptive snapshot, not a predictive tool
WHY: Recognizing these limitations keeps Level II awareness properly descriptive rather than predictive.

As descriptive awareness of the order book, with real limitations, not a predictive edge | detail: A cautious, descriptive frame
As a tool that guarantees knowing exactly what happens next | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: Level II has real limitations and doesn't guarantee outcomes.
As something irrelevant to understand at all | detail: Understates it | wrong: Conceptual awareness of the order book is still useful context.

INTRO
TITLE: The Fear of Missing Out
TEXT: FOMO describes the urge to jump into a trade because price is already moving fast, often without a clear plan.
CHART: breakout

[TF] FOMO describes jumping into a trade because a plan says it's a good setup.
ANSWER: False
WHY: FOMO specifically describes acting from urgency about missing a move, not from a planned setup.

[FILL] The urge to jump into a trade purely because price is already moving fast is commonly called [___].
FOMO
a stop-loss
a drawdown
WHY: "FOMO" (fear of missing out) is the standard term for this urge.

Entering because of urgency about a fast-moving price, without a clear pre-existing plan.
Entering only after a calm, pre-planned checklist is satisfied.
Entering after carefully calculating position size in advance.
WHY: FOMO entries are commonly described as reactive and plan-less, driven by urgency.

WHY: Contrasting FOMO with planned, patient entries highlights what makes FOMO distinct.

Ask whether this matches a pre-existing plan or setup
Recognize if the urge is driven mainly by fear of missing out
Pause instead of acting immediately on the impulse
WHY: This process gives space to recognize FOMO before automatically acting on it.

A guarantee that the trade will be profitable | detail: Overstates certainty | wrong: FOMO describes a motivation for entering, not an outcome.
WHY: FOMO specifically describes an emotionally reactive, plan-less entry driven by urgency.

guaranteed

trending
choppy

WHY: These are commonly cited classifications for describing different general market conditions.

[ORDER] A commonly described general process for classifying a market as trending:
Look at the sequence of highs and lows over recent history
Check whether they're consistently moving in one direction
Confirm the direction has held for a meaningful stretch of time
Classify the condition as trending if those checks hold
WHY: This process reflects a commonly described, descriptive way to classify market conditions.

WHY: Trending specifically describes a sustained directional move, not a guarantee about the future or a bounded range.

A strong sustained trend with normal-sized swings matches a trending classification, not necessarily a volatile one. Those describe different traits.
They should assume trending and volatile always mean the same thing.
They should ignore the size of the swings entirely.
WHY: Trending describes sustained direction, while volatile specifically describes unusually large or fast swings. They're distinct classifications.
